ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security
Question 10Skipped

A maritime shipping company, OceanRoute Logistics, is using machine learning to predict mechanical failures across its fleet of cargo vessels.


The company has engine usage data, operating temperature data, and maintenance records stored in an Amazon S3 bucket.


The ML engineering team needs to clean and analyze the data, engineer features such as average engine temperature over rolling time windows, and store those features so they can support both batch and real-time prediction workloads.


The company also wants to use a pre-built machine learning model to accelerate deployment and reduce custom model development time.


Which combination of AWS services would best meet all of these requirements?
Correct answer
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler to clean and transform the data, store the engineered features in SageMaker Feature Store, and use SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a pre-built predictive maintenance model.
Explanation
Correct. This combination addresses every stated requirement in a single cohesive workflow. SageMaker Data Wrangler provides a low-code interface for data cleaning, transformation, and time-series feature engineering (e.g., rolling averages of engine temperature), with native export to SageMaker Feature Store. SageMaker Feature Store is purpose-built to serve engineered features for both offline batch workloads (via the offline store backed by Amazon S3) and online real-time inference (via the online store backed by a low-latency key-value backend), directly satisfying the dual-serving requirement. SageMaker JumpStart provides a catalog of pre-built, fine-tunable models for common use cases including predictive maintenance, enabling rapid deployment without building a model from scratch. Keyword tip: when you see 'dual batch and real-time feature serving' combined with 'pre-built model deployment,' the SageMaker Data Wrangler + Feature Store + JumpStart combination is the canonical MLA-C01 answer.
Use SageMaker Notebooks to preprocess the data, store the engineered features in Amazon S3, and use SageMaker Autopilot to automatically train and deploy a predictive model.
Explanation
Incorrect. While SageMaker Notebooks are a valid preprocessing environment, storing engineered features in Amazon S3 alone does not provide the dual online/offline serving capability required for real-time predictions. Amazon S3 serves as the offline store but cannot natively deliver low-latency online feature lookups without additional infrastructure. Furthermore, SageMaker Autopilot automates the training of custom models through AutoML rather than deploying pre-built models, which directly contradicts OceanRoute Logistics's explicit requirement to accelerate deployment by leveraging a pre-built model. Keyword tip: 'real-time feature serving from S3 alone' is an anti-pattern for low-latency online inference; SageMaker Feature Store's online store is required for that capability.
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler to clean and preprocess the data, export the engineered features to Amazon Redshift, and use SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a pre-built predictive maintenance model.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler is correctly chosen for preprocessing, and SageMaker JumpStart is correctly chosen for pre-built model deployment. However, Amazon Redshift is an analytical data warehouse optimized for large-scale SQL-based analytics, not for low-latency online feature retrieval during real-time model inference. Serving features from Amazon Redshift at inference time introduces unacceptable latency and architectural complexity compared to SageMaker Feature Store's online store, which is designed for millisecond-level feature lookup. This option fails the real-time prediction serving requirement. Keyword tip: Amazon Redshift is a distractor when the requirement includes 'real-time inference feature serving'; SageMaker Feature Store's online store is the purpose-built alternative.
Use AWS Glue DataBrew to clean and transform the data, store the engineered features in SageMaker Feature Store, and use SageMaker Autopilot to train and deploy a custom predictive model.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew is a valid data preparation tool capable of cleaning and transforming datasets, and SageMaker Feature Store is correctly identified for dual-mode feature serving. However, SageMaker Autopilot is an AutoML service that trains new custom models by exploring algorithm and hyperparameter combinations — it does not deploy pre-built models from a catalog. OceanRoute Logistics explicitly requires leveraging a pre-built model to reduce development time, which is the specific capability provided by SageMaker JumpStart, not Autopilot. Additionally, AWS Glue DataBrew lacks the native, one-click integration with SageMaker Feature Store that SageMaker Data Wrangler provides within the SageMaker feature engineering pipeline. Keyword tip: when 'pre-built model' is a stated requirement, SageMaker JumpStart is correct; Autopilot is a strong distractor because it automates model training, not the deployment of pre-existing catalog models.
Overall explanation

Question 11Skipped

A machine learning engineer at SiliconEdge Technologies, a semiconductor manufacturer, is training a deep learning model on Amazon SageMaker using TensorFlow to predict wafer defect patterns from process sensor data.


During training, the model’s performance stops improving, and the engineer suspects the model may be experiencing vanishing gradients.


The engineer wants to use SageMaker Debugger to monitor the training job in real time and identify the issue.


Which approach should the engineer take to correctly detect and address vanishing gradients using SageMaker Debugger? 
Correct answer
Use SageMaker Debugger's built-in rules to monitor gradient tensor values during training, and adjust the learning rate or other hyperparameters if vanishing gradients are detected.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Debugger provides real-time monitoring of training jobs and includes built-in rules specifically designed to detect vanishing gradients by analyzing gradient tensor values emitted during the training loop. When the VanishingGradient rule is triggered, the engineer can respond by adjusting the learning rate, switching optimizers, or applying gradient clipping to restore healthy gradient flow. This is the canonical MLOps pattern for diagnosing gradient-related pathologies in SageMaker, and it requires no custom instrumentation code when using supported frameworks such as TensorFlow. Keyword tip: when a scenario describes training stagnation suspected to be caused by vanishing gradients and asks about SageMaker Debugger, the signal is 'built-in rules + gradient tensor inspection' — not resource utilization monitoring.
Configure a custom SageMaker Debugger rule to visualize CPU and memory usage metrics, then adjust the batch size based on resource utilization patterns to indirectly resolve the training stagnation.
Explanation
Incorrect. Monitoring CPU and memory usage with a custom Debugger rule addresses infrastructure-level resource contention, not model training dynamics. Adjusting batch size based on resource utilization does not resolve vanishing gradients, which are caused by very small gradient magnitudes propagated during backpropagation, not by compute resource allocation imbalances. Keyword tip: whenever an option conflates infrastructure resource utilization metrics with model-level training pathologies such as vanishing gradients, it is a strong distractor that targets candidates who confuse SageMaker Debugger's tensor rules with its profiling capabilities.
Enable SageMaker Profiler to collect GPU utilization and kernel-level performance data, and implement early stopping via a SageMaker training callback when gradient norms fall below a defined threshold.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Profiler — part of SageMaker Debugger's profiling capability — is designed to capture system-level performance data such as GPU kernel utilization, CPU bottlenecks, and I/O throughput. It does not inspect model-level tensor data such as gradient norms or weight distributions. Implementing early stopping based on Profiler output would not detect vanishing gradients, which require tensor-level instrumentation available only through Debugger's built-in or custom tensor rules. Keyword tip: 'SageMaker Profiler' targets infrastructure performance efficiency, not model learning dynamics — on the MLA-C01 exam, distinguish it from Debugger's tensor debugging rules whenever the question involves a training pathology.
Configure SageMaker Debugger to emit resource utilization metrics to Amazon CloudWatch and set up CloudWatch Alarms to detect underutilized GPUs as a proxy signal for vanishing gradients.
Explanation
Incorrect. While SageMaker Debugger can surface resource utilization metrics and Amazon CloudWatch can be used to set alarms on those metrics, GPU underutilization is not a reliable or direct proxy indicator for vanishing gradients. Vanishing gradients are a model-specific phenomenon observable only by inspecting gradient tensor values during backpropagation, not by monitoring hardware utilization counters, which reflect compute scheduling behavior rather than gradient magnitude. Keyword tip: conflating hardware-level monitoring via CloudWatch Alarms with model-level tensor diagnostics is a recurring distractor pattern in MLA-C01 questions involving SageMaker Debugger — route training pathologies to tensor rules, not to infrastructure alarms.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Debugger and its tensor-level debugging capabilities.

It focuses on using built-in Debugger rules to detect vanishing gradients during training and then adjusting training configuration or hyperparameters to address the issue.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use SageMaker Debugger's built-in rules to monitor gradient tensor values during training, and adjust the learning rate or other hyperparameters if vanishing gradients are detected: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Debugger can monitor tensor values such as gradients, weights, activations, and losses. Its built-in rules can detect issues such as vanishing gradients.

    Configure a custom SageMaker Debugger rule to visualize CPU and memory usage metrics, then adjust the batch size based on resource utilization patterns to indirectly resolve the training stagnation: This option is incorrect. CPU and memory usage are infrastructure metrics. They do not directly diagnose vanishing gradients, which require inspection of gradient tensor values.

    Enable SageMaker Profiler to collect GPU utilization and kernel-level performance data, and implement early stopping via a SageMaker training callback when gradient norms fall below a defined threshold: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Profiler is used for system-level performance analysis, such as GPU utilization, CPU bottlenecks, I/O, and kernel activity. It is not the right tool for tensor-level gradient diagnostics.

    Configure SageMaker Debugger to emit resource utilization metrics to Amazon CloudWatch and set up CloudWatch Alarms to detect underutilized GPUs as a proxy signal for vanishing gradients: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch can monitor metrics and alarms, but GPU utilization is not a reliable proxy for vanishing gradients. Vanishing gradients must be detected through gradient tensor values.

Why SageMaker Debugger built-in rules fit this scenario.

The model's performance stops improving during training.

The engineer suspects vanishing gradients.

Vanishing gradients occur when gradient values become extremely small during backpropagation.

When this happens, earlier layers may learn very slowly or stop learning effectively.

That is a model training pathology, not an infrastructure utilization problem.

This points directly to Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

Amazon SageMaker Debugger can capture tensor data during training.

This includes gradients, weights, losses, and activations.

The engineer can use built-in Debugger rules, such as rules for detecting vanishing gradients, to monitor gradient behavior during the training job.

If the rule detects vanishing gradients, the engineer can adjust the training configuration.

Possible remediation actions include changing the learning rate, using a different optimizer, modifying the model architecture, applying normalization techniques, or using activation functions less prone to vanishing gradients.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Training pathology detection: Use Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

    Tensor-level monitoring: Inspect gradient tensor values.

    Vanishing gradient diagnosis: Use built-in Debugger rules.

    Real-time training insight: Monitor the training job while it runs.

    Correct tool selection: Use Debugger for model learning issues, not Profiler.

    Targeted remediation: Adjust learning rate, optimizer, architecture, or related hyperparameters.

    Avoid proxy metrics: Do not infer vanishing gradients from GPU utilization or CPU metrics.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Vanishing gradients + training stagnation + tensor values = Amazon SageMaker Debugger built-in rules.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger: Tensor-level debugging for training jobs.

    Gradient tensors: Required to diagnose vanishing or exploding gradients.

    Vanishing gradients: Gradients become too small for effective learning.

    Built-in rules: Predefined checks for common training issues.

    Amazon SageMaker Profiler: System-level performance profiling, not tensor debugging.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Metrics and alarms, not direct model tensor inspection.

    Learning rate and optimizer changes: Common remediation paths after diagnosing training issues.

Question 12Skipped

An aerospace and defense contractor, OrbitShield Systems, is using AWS Glue to run ETL jobs on large sensor telemetry datasets stored in Amazon S3.


The data is organized into partitions based on the year the data was collected, with each year stored in its own folder structure.


The data engineering team frequently runs queries that filter by year, such as retrieving only records from 2023.


In the context of AWS Glue ETL pipelines that feed downstream ML workflows, which statement most accurately describes the benefit of this year-based partitioning strategy?
Correct answer
It allows AWS Glue to skip irrelevant partitions, reducing the amount of data scanned and improving overall query performance and cost efficiency.
Explanation
Correct. Partition pruning in AWS Glue allows the ETL engine to skip partitions that do not satisfy the query predicate — in this case, any year other than 2023. This reduces the volume of data scanned, directly lowering both processing time and associated AWS Glue DPU costs. For the MLA-C01 exam, remember that predicate pushdown combined with S3 partitioning using Hive-style prefixes such as year=2023/ is a foundational pattern for cost-efficient, scalable data preparation pipelines feeding ML workflows. Keyword tip: When a question describes time-based S3 folder partitioning in AWS Glue with year-filtered queries, the correct mechanism is partition pruning (predicate pushdown), which reduces data scanned and DPU cost.
It enables AWS Glue to automatically compress each yearly partition into a single Parquet file, reducing storage costs and improving read throughput across all partitions.
Explanation
Incorrect. While converting data to columnar formats such as Parquet does improve read throughput and reduce storage costs, this benefit comes from the file format choice — not from the partitioning strategy itself. Partitioning alone does not trigger automatic compression or file consolidation into a single file per partition. Keyword tip: When an answer conflates partitioning benefits with columnar file format benefits (Parquet, ORC), recognize these as complementary but entirely distinct optimizations in AWS Glue ETL pipelines.
It causes AWS Glue to distribute processing load equally across all partitions simultaneously, regardless of which year is specified in the query filter.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue does not process all partitions equally when a filter condition is present. When partition pruning is enabled and the query predicate matches the partition key (e.g., year=2023), AWS Glue reads only the relevant partitions. Processing all partitions regardless of the filter would negate the primary performance benefit of partitioning and is inconsistent with how AWS Glue handles predicate pushdown. Keyword tip: An answer describing uniform processing across all partitions regardless of a filter predicate signals a misunderstanding of how partition pruning works in AWS Glue.
It increases the number of Amazon S3 IOPS operations required per query because each partition folder must be individually listed before scanning can begin, reducing overall throughput.
Explanation
Incorrect. Partitioning reduces — not increases — the effective I/O burden by narrowing the set of Amazon S3 objects that must be read. While Amazon S3 LIST operations are performed at the prefix level, the overhead is minimal compared to the substantial reduction in data scanned achieved through partition pruning. In well-designed partitioned datasets, partition pruning dramatically decreases total data read, improving query response times rather than degrading them. Keyword tip: Any answer claiming that S3 partitioning increases I/O overhead or reduces throughput contradicts the core purpose of partition pruning and should be eliminated in data preparation architecture questions.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of partition pruning in AWS Glue ETL pipelines.

It focuses on understanding how partitioned Amazon S3 datasets allow Glue jobs and downstream queries to scan only the relevant data, improving performance and reducing cost.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    It allows AWS Glue to skip irrelevant partitions, reducing the amount of data scanned and improving overall query performance and cost efficiency: This is the correct option. Partition pruning allows AWS Glue to read only the partitions that match the filter condition, such as year=2023, instead of scanning all yearly folders.

    It enables AWS Glue to automatically compress each yearly partition into a single Parquet file, reducing storage costs and improving read throughput across all partitions: This option is incorrect. Compression and columnar storage come from file formats such as Parquet or ORC. Partitioning alone does not automatically compress or consolidate data files.

    It causes AWS Glue to distribute processing load equally across all partitions simultaneously, regardless of which year is specified in the query filter: This option is incorrect. The purpose of partition pruning is to avoid scanning irrelevant partitions when the filter matches a partition key.

    It increases the number of Amazon S3 IOPS operations required per query because each partition folder must be individually listed before scanning can begin, reducing overall throughput: This option is incorrect. Well-designed partitioning reduces the amount of data read from Amazon S3, improving throughput and lowering processing cost.

Why partition pruning fits this scenario.

The telemetry data is stored in Amazon S3.

The data is organized by year, with each year stored under a separate folder structure.

The data engineering team frequently queries only one year, such as 2023.

That points directly to partition pruning.

When the data is partitioned by year, AWS Glue can use the query filter to identify which partition is needed.

For example, if the query filters for year=2023, Glue can skip partitions for other years.

This reduces the amount of data scanned.

It also reduces job runtime and lowers DPU consumption.

This is especially important for large telemetry datasets used in downstream ML workflows, where feature engineering jobs should process only the relevant subset of data.

Partitioning works best when the partition keys match common query filters.

In this scenario, because queries frequently filter by year, year-based partitioning is a strong optimization.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Time-based filtering: Use year as a partition key.

    Reduced data scanning: Skip irrelevant S3 prefixes.

    Lower processing cost: Reduce AWS Glue DPU consumption.

    Improved performance: Read only the partitions needed for the query.

    ML pipeline efficiency: Process only relevant data for feature engineering.

    Schema-on-read compatibility: Support downstream analytics through the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    Complementary optimization: Use columnar formats such as Parquet or ORC when appropriate, but do not confuse file format benefits with partition pruning.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

S3 data partitioned by year + queries filtering by year = partition pruning reduces data scanned and improves cost efficiency.

Remember:

    Partition pruning: Skips partitions that do not match the filter predicate.

    Predicate pushdown: Pushes filter conditions closer to the data scan.

    Hive-style partitions: Common S3 prefix format such as year=2023/.

    AWS Glue Data Catalog: Stores table and partition metadata.

    DPU cost: Reduced when Glue scans and processes less data.

    Parquet and ORC: Improve columnar read efficiency, but are separate from partitioning.

    Good partition key: Matches frequent query filters such as year, region, or event type.

Official AWS references.

    Managing partitions for ETL output in AWS Glue.

    Using pushdown predicates in AWS Glue.

Key exam terms: partition pruning, predicate pushdown, AWS Glue DPU cost, Hive-style Amazon S3 prefix, ETL pipeline optimization, columnar format, data scanning reduction, feature engineering pipeline.

Question 13Skipped

A logistics and freight company, FreightPath Solutions, stores sensitive shipment records, customer personally identifiable information, and financial invoicing data in Amazon S3 buckets to support its ML-driven route optimization and demand forecasting pipelines.


The security team must ensure compliance with data privacy regulations such as GDPR and CCPA.


The team plans to deploy Amazon Macie as part of a broader data governance strategy.


Which benefits does Amazon Macie provide in this scenario?  Select TWO.
Monitors the throughput and I/O performance of Amazon S3 buckets to ensure optimal read/write operations at scale.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Macie is a data security service focused on discovering, classifying, and protecting sensitive data — it does not monitor S3 bucket throughput, I/O performance, or read/write operational metrics. For bucket-level performance monitoring, Amazon CloudWatch metrics and Amazon S3 Storage Lens are the appropriate tools. FreightPath Solutions would need to configure those services separately to track operational performance of their S3-based data pipelines. Keyword tip: When you see 'performance monitoring of S3 operations' or 'read/write throughput,' think Amazon CloudWatch or S3 Storage Lens, not Amazon Macie.
Correct selection

Continuously evaluates the security and access control configuration of Amazon S3 buckets and generates policy findings for potential security or privacy issues.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Macie uses machine learning to analyze AWS CloudTrail event data and Amazon S3 access logs, enabling it to detect anomalous access patterns such as unusual volumes of data being accessed, access originating from unexpected geographic locations, or access by unfamiliar IAM principals. For FreightPath Solutions, this capability is critical for identifying potential unauthorized access to customer PII and financial records before a data breach occurs. These behavioral analytics are central to Amazon Macie's role in a GDPR- and CCPA-compliant data governance posture. Keyword tip: 'Anomalous access detection' combined with 'sensitive data in S3' is a direct signal for Amazon Macie's behavioral analytics capability on the MLA-C01 exam.
Correct selection
Automatically classifies sensitive data such as PII, financial records, and protected health information stored in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Macie employs machine learning models and pattern matching to automatically discover and classify sensitive data categories — including personally identifiable information (PII), financial records, and protected health information (PHI) — stored in Amazon S3 buckets. For FreightPath Solutions, this automated classification provides the data inventory visibility required to demonstrate compliance with GDPR and CCPA, which mandate that organizations know what sensitive data they hold and where it resides. Keyword tip: 'Automatic classification of PII, financial data, or PHI in S3' is the canonical use case for Amazon Macie in compliance-driven scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam.
Automatically rotates data encryption keys associated with Amazon S3 objects to enhance the company's security posture.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Macie has no key management capabilities and does not perform encryption key rotation. Key rotation for S3 objects is managed through AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS), which supports automatic annual rotation for customer-managed keys (CMKs). FreightPath Solutions would configure key rotation policies directly within AWS KMS, entirely independently of Macie. Keyword tip: Whenever 'key rotation' or 'key lifecycle management' appears as an option attributed to Macie, it is a distractor — those responsibilities belong to AWS KMS or AWS Secrets Manager.
Enables server-side encryption for sensitive Amazon S3 objects using AWS KMS without requiring additional configuration from the security team.
Explanation
Incorrect. Configuring server-side encryption for Amazon S3 objects using AWS KMS is handled through Amazon S3 default encryption settings, S3 bucket policies, or infrastructure-as-code configurations such as AWS CloudFormation — not through Amazon Macie. Macie operates as a passive observer and classifier; it has no capability to apply or enforce encryption settings on S3 objects. FreightPath Solutions' security team would need to enable encryption through S3 or AWS Config rules separately. Keyword tip: 'Applying or enforcing encryption on S3 objects' points to S3 bucket policies, AWS Config rules, or AWS Security Hub remediation actions — not Amazon Macie.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon Macie and its role in discovering sensitive data and assessing the security and privacy posture of data stored in Amazon S3.

It focuses on understanding how Macie uses machine learning, pattern matching, and automated security evaluation to classify sensitive information and identify potential policy issues affecting S3 buckets.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

Continuously evaluates the security and access control configuration of Amazon S3 buckets and generates policy findings for potential security or privacy issues: This is a correct option. Amazon Macie evaluates bucket policies, access control lists, public access settings, and other security configurations. It can generate policy findings when it identifies potential security or privacy risks, such as a bucket becoming publicly accessible.

Automatically classifies sensitive data such as PII, financial records, and protected health information stored in Amazon S3: This is a correct option. Amazon Macie uses machine learning, pattern matching, and managed data identifiers to discover and classify sensitive information stored in Amazon S3, including PII and financial data.

Monitors the throughput and I/O performance of Amazon S3 buckets to ensure optimal read/write operations at scale: This option is incorrect. Amazon Macie is a data security and privacy service. Operational metrics such as request rates, latency, and data transfer are monitored by services such as Amazon CloudWatch and Amazon S3 Storage Lens.

Automatically rotates data encryption keys associated with Amazon S3 objects to enhance the company's security posture: This option is incorrect. Encryption key creation, management, and automatic rotation are capabilities of AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS), not Amazon Macie.

Enables server-side encryption for sensitive Amazon S3 objects using AWS KMS without requiring additional configuration from the security team: This option is incorrect. Amazon Macie can help identify sensitive data and security issues, but it does not enable or enforce encryption. Server-side encryption must be configured through Amazon S3, AWS KMS, bucket policies, or infrastructure-as-code controls.

Why Amazon Macie fits this scenario.

FreightPath Solutions stores shipment records, customer personally identifiable information (PII), and financial invoicing data in Amazon S3.

The organization must comply with privacy regulations such as GDPR and CCPA.

That points directly to Amazon Macie.

Macie maintains an inventory of the organization’s S3 buckets and evaluates their security and access control settings.

It can identify potential policy issues, such as buckets that allow public or unintended access.

Macie can also perform automated sensitive data discovery or run targeted sensitive data discovery jobs.

When Macie analyzes S3 objects, it uses managed data identifiers, custom data identifiers, machine learning, and pattern matching to detect sensitive information.

For FreightPath Solutions, this provides visibility into where regulated customer and financial data is stored.

It also helps the security team identify buckets that contain sensitive information and may require stronger access controls or remediation.

This satisfies the key requirements:

Sensitive data discovery: Identify regulated information stored in Amazon S3.

Automatic classification: Detect categories such as PII and financial information.

Security posture assessment: Evaluate bucket policies, ACLs, and public access settings.

Policy findings: Report potential security and privacy issues affecting S3 buckets.

Regulatory compliance: Support governance initiatives associated with GDPR and CCPA.

Centralized visibility: Maintain an inventory of S3 buckets and their sensitivity status.

Low operational overhead: Use automated sensitive data discovery instead of manually inspecting S3 objects.

ML data governance: Identify sensitive information used by training, feature engineering, and analytics pipelines.

Amazon GuardDuty: Detects malicious activity and anomalous access patterns.

Question 14Skipped

A logistics and freight company, FreightAxis Solutions, is ingesting high-velocity shipment tracking and route log data using Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose.


The engineering team needs to analyze this data in near real time using SQL queries to support operational dashboards and ML feature pipelines.


The data is semi-structured and must be loaded into a data warehouse optimized for fast analytical querying at scale.


Which combination of AWS services should the company use to meet these requirements?
Correct answer
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to load the data directly into Amazon Redshift using the native Firehose-to-Redshift integration for fast analytical querying.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose provides a native, serverless integration with Amazon Redshift that automatically stages incoming streaming data in Amazon S3 and then issues a COPY command to load it into Redshift tables. Amazon Redshift is purpose-built as a fully managed, petabyte-scale data warehouse optimized for OLAP-style SQL analytics at high throughput, making it the best architectural fit when the requirements specify near real-time data warehousing and fast SQL querying at scale. This pattern requires no custom code for data loading and supports the sustained high-concurrency analytical queries needed by operational dashboards and ML feature pipelines alike. Keyword tip: when you see 'streaming ingestion + data warehouse + SQL analytics at scale,' the Firehose → Redshift native integration pattern is the canonical answer on the MLA-C01 exam.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to stream the data into an Amazon S3 bucket, then configure an AWS Glue ETL job to transform and catalog the data before querying it with Amazon Athena.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Kinesis Data Firehose → S3 → AWS Glue → Athena is a valid serverless analytics architecture, it introduces an additional ETL hop that adds latency and operational overhead not suited to near real-time data warehousing. More importantly, Amazon Athena is an interactive query service over S3 objects and is not a data warehouse; it lacks the pre-aggregated columnar storage and massively parallel processing (MPP) optimizations that Redshift provides for sustained, high-concurrency analytical workloads. This pattern is better suited to ad-hoc, infrequent queries on historical data rather than a near real-time data warehousing use case. Keyword tip: Athena excels at ad-hoc queries on S3 data; prefer Redshift when 'data warehouse,' 'high concurrency,' or 'fast repeated analytical queries' are explicitly stated requirements.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to ingest the data, then deploy an AWS Lambda function to transform each record and write it to Amazon DynamoDB for downstream SQL-based reporting.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon DynamoDB is a NoSQL key-value and document database designed for single-digit millisecond latency on OLTP workloads; it does not natively support complex analytical SQL queries, JOINs, or aggregations at data warehouse scale. Switching from Kinesis Data Firehose to Kinesis Data Streams also adds unnecessary shard management complexity without satisfying the analytical querying requirement. This combination fundamentally does not meet the 'SQL queries on a data warehouse' requirement stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: whenever 'SQL analytics' or 'data warehouse' appear as requirements, DynamoDB is a distractor — it is optimized for NoSQL, high-throughput OLTP access patterns, not SQL-based analytical workloads.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to stream the data into Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL using the Firehose HTTP endpoint delivery, then run analytical SQL queries against the Aurora cluster.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL is a transactional (OLTP) relational database and is not optimized for large-scale analytical data warehousing. While Aurora supports SQL and offers high availability, it lacks Redshift's columnar storage, MPP architecture, and the native first-class Firehose delivery stream integration that enables automatic data loading via the COPY command. Kinesis Data Firehose does not have a native delivery destination for Amazon RDS or Aurora — routing data to Aurora would require a custom HTTP endpoint, introducing significant additional engineering complexity and negating the serverless simplicity that Firehose provides. Keyword tip: RDS/Aurora targets OLTP transactional workloads while Redshift targets OLAP/data warehousing — this OLTP vs. OLAP distinction is a frequently tested concept on the MLA-C01 exam.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS streaming ingestion and analytical data warehousing patterns for ML data preparation.

It focuses on using Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with Amazon Redshift when data must be loaded in near real time into a warehouse optimized for fast SQL analytics at scale.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to load the data directly into Amazon Redshift using the native Firehose-to-Redshift integration for fast analytical querying: This is the correct option. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose can deliver streaming data to Amazon Redshift by staging data in Amazon S3 and loading it with the Redshift COPY command. Amazon Redshift is optimized for large-scale analytical SQL queries.

    Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to stream the data into an Amazon S3 bucket, then configure an AWS Glue ETL job to transform and catalog the data before querying it with Amazon Athena: This option is not the best answer. This is a valid serverless analytics pattern, but Amazon Athena is better suited for ad-hoc querying over Amazon S3, not sustained high-concurrency data warehouse workloads.

    Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams to ingest the data, then deploy an AWS Lambda function to transform each record and write it to Amazon DynamoDB for downstream SQL-based reporting: This option is incorrect. Amazon DynamoDB is a NoSQL database for high-throughput transactional access patterns. It is not a SQL data warehouse for analytical joins, aggregations, and reporting.

    Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to stream the data into Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL using the Firehose HTTP endpoint delivery, then run analytical SQL queries against the Aurora cluster: This option is incorrect. Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL is an OLTP relational database. It supports SQL, but it is not optimized for large-scale OLAP data warehousing like Amazon Redshift.

Why Kinesis Data Firehose and Amazon Redshift fit this scenario.

The company is ingesting high-velocity shipment tracking and route log data.

The data must be analyzed in near real time.

The engineering team wants to use SQL queries.

The data must be loaded into a data warehouse optimized for fast analytical querying at scale.

That points to Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with Amazon Redshift.

Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose is a managed streaming delivery service.

It can buffer incoming records, deliver them to supported destinations, and integrate natively with Amazon Redshift.

For Redshift delivery, Firehose stages data in Amazon S3 and then uses the Redshift COPY command to load data into Redshift tables.

Amazon Redshift is a fully managed data warehouse designed for OLAP workloads.

It uses columnar storage and massively parallel processing to support analytical queries over large datasets.

This makes it a better fit than Amazon RDS, Amazon Aurora, Amazon DynamoDB, or Amazon Athena when the scenario explicitly asks for a data warehouse optimized for fast SQL analytics at scale.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    High-velocity ingestion: Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose.

    Near real-time loading: Deliver streaming records into the analytics layer.

    Data warehouse target: Use Amazon Redshift.

    SQL analytics: Query warehouse tables using SQL.

    Fast analytical performance: Use Redshift columnar storage and MPP architecture.

    ML feature pipelines: Support downstream feature extraction and operational analytics.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid custom ingestion code and manual loading workflows.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Streaming ingestion + near real-time data warehouse loading + fast SQL analytics at scale = Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to Amazon Redshift.

Remember:

    Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose: Managed streaming delivery service.

    Amazon Redshift: OLAP data warehouse for analytical SQL at scale.

    Redshift COPY command: Used to load staged data into Redshift tables.

    Amazon S3: Staging layer used by Firehose for Redshift delivery.

    Amazon Athena: Serverless ad-hoc SQL over S3, not a full data warehouse replacement.

    Amazon DynamoDB: NoSQL OLTP database, not analytical SQL storage.

    Amazon Aurora: Relational OLTP database, not optimized for warehouse-scale analytics.

Question 15Skipped

A data scientist at MetroTransit Analytics, a public transportation authority, wants to optimize a machine learning model for deployment across multiple edge devices installed in buses and rail stations for real-time passenger flow prediction.


The model was trained using TensorFlow in Amazon SageMaker.


Which steps should the data scientist take next to optimize the model for edge deployment while maintaining inference performance?
Set up Amazon SageMaker Debugger to capture real-time metrics during inference on edge devices, then iteratively retrain the model based on captured anomalies before deploying.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Debugger is designed to monitor and debug model training jobs by capturing tensors and system metrics during the training phase — it does not compile or optimize models for specific edge hardware platforms, nor does it provide a deployment mechanism for IoT or edge devices such as those installed in buses or rail stations. The correct approach for edge optimization is to use SageMaker Neo for hardware-specific compilation followed by AWS IoT Greengrass for deployment. Keyword tip: when a question asks about debugging or profiling training runs, SageMaker Debugger is relevant — but it is a distractor whenever the scenario focuses on edge deployment optimization.
Manually rewrite the model's inference code in a lower-level language such as C++ to ensure compatibility with the target edge hardware architecture and reduce runtime overhead.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually rewriting model inference code to achieve hardware compatibility is a time-consuming, error-prone approach that is unnecessary when AWS provides SageMaker Neo, which automates cross-platform hardware optimization. Neo handles operator-level graph optimizations for dozens of supported hardware targets — including ARM, Intel, and NVIDIA-based edge devices commonly found in transportation infrastructure — without requiring manual code changes. Keyword tip: any answer suggesting manual low-level rewrites is typically a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam whenever an AWS-managed compilation service exists for the same task.
Correct answer
Use Amazon SageMaker Neo to compile the model for the target hardware platform and deploy it using AWS IoT Greengrass.
Explanation
Correct. After training a TensorFlow model in Amazon SageMaker, the recommended MLOps workflow for edge deployment is to use SageMaker Neo to compile the model for the specific target hardware (such as ARM-based IoT devices, NVIDIA Jetson, or similar embedded platforms deployed in transit vehicles and stations). Neo applies hardware-specific graph optimizations — including operator fusion and memory layout tuning — and generates an optimized binary. The optimized model is then deployed to edge devices using AWS IoT Greengrass, which manages the runtime environment, model updates, and inference execution on the edge without requiring a persistent cloud connection. Keyword tip: the SageMaker Neo + AWS IoT Greengrass pairing is the canonical AWS pattern for edge ML deployment — recognize this combination on any scenario involving hardware-aware inference optimization and edge device management.
Convert the trained TensorFlow model to ONNX format using SageMaker Processing jobs, then deploy the ONNX model directly to edge devices using a custom inference runtime without additional compilation.
Explanation
Incorrect. While converting a model to ONNX format facilitates cross-framework portability, deploying an ONNX model to edge devices without hardware-specific compilation does not yield the performance optimizations that SageMaker Neo provides. Neo performs target-hardware-aware graph compilation, memory layout optimization, and operator fusion that go well beyond format conversion — capabilities that are critical for resource-constrained edge devices in transit environments. Additionally, SageMaker Processing jobs are designed for data preprocessing and feature engineering, not for model format conversion in an edge deployment workflow. Keyword tip: ONNX conversion addresses framework interoperability, not hardware-level inference optimization — distinguish these two separate concerns whenever a question mentions both edge deployment and performance requirements.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of edge ML deployment and inference optimization on AWS.

It focuses on using Amazon SageMaker Neo to compile a trained model for target edge hardware and AWS IoT Greengrass to deploy and run the optimized model on edge devices.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Set up Amazon SageMaker Debugger to capture real-time metrics during inference on edge devices, then iteratively retrain the model based on captured anomalies before deploying: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Debugger is used to debug training jobs by capturing tensors and training metrics. It does not compile models for edge hardware or deploy models to IoT devices.

    Manually rewrite the model's inference code in a lower-level language such as C++ to ensure compatibility with the target edge hardware architecture and reduce runtime overhead: This option is incorrect. Manual low-level rewrites are unnecessary and error-prone when Amazon SageMaker Neo can perform hardware-aware model optimization.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Neo to compile the model for the target hardware platform and deploy it using AWS IoT Greengrass: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Neo optimizes trained models for specific hardware targets, and AWS IoT Greengrass manages deployment and inference execution on edge devices.

    Convert the trained TensorFlow model to ONNX format using SageMaker Processing jobs, then deploy the ONNX model directly to edge devices using a custom inference runtime without additional compilation: This option is incorrect. ONNX conversion can help with model portability, but it does not provide the hardware-specific compilation and optimization that Amazon SageMaker Neo provides.

Why SageMaker Neo and AWS IoT Greengrass fit this scenario.

The model was trained in Amazon SageMaker.

The target environment is a fleet of edge devices installed in buses and rail stations.

The model must run inference close to passengers and transit infrastructure.

The team also needs to optimize performance for constrained hardware.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Neo and AWS IoT Greengrass.

It can optimize models from frameworks such as TensorFlow, PyTorch, and MXNet.

Neo applies hardware-aware optimizations so the model can run more efficiently on edge CPUs, GPUs, or accelerators.

After compilation, AWS IoT Greengrass can deploy and manage the model on edge devices.

Greengrass enables local inference, model updates, and runtime management without requiring every inference request to go back to the cloud.

This is the AWS-native pattern for edge ML deployment where latency, connectivity, and device performance matter.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Edge deployment: Use AWS IoT Greengrass.

    TensorFlow model support: Compile the trained TensorFlow model for target devices.

    Low-latency inference: Run predictions locally on buses and rail stations.

Question 16Skipped

A data science team at OrbitShield Systems, an aerospace and defense contractor, is using Amazon SageMaker Experiments to systematically evaluate different model configurations for a predictive maintenance application.


The team needs to track and compare several training runs that vary
by hyperparameters, input data features, and algorithm choices.


The goal is to identify the best-performing model for deployment.

Which set of components in Amazon SageMaker Experiments should the team use to properly organize and track these training runs?
Pipelines, Trials, Stages, Logs
Explanation
Incorrect. Pipelines and Stages are not native components of the Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracking hierarchy. SageMaker Pipelines is a separate orchestration service used to automate and version ML workflows, and 'Stages' does not exist as a construct within SageMaker Experiments. Conflating pipeline orchestration concepts with experiment tracking components is a common distractor pattern on MLA-C01. Keyword tip: when a question lists 'Stages' or 'Logs' as organizational units alongside SageMaker tracking terminology, recognize these as invalid SageMaker Experiments constructs and eliminate the option.
Correct answer
Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, Trackers
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Experiments is structured around four core components: Experiments (the top-level container that groups related Trials), Trials (individual training runs representing a single end-to-end iteration of an experiment), Trial Components (sub-units of a Trial that capture distinct processing steps such as data preprocessing, training, and evaluation), and Trackers (used to log inputs such as hyperparameters and parameters, as well as outputs such as metrics and artifacts, enabling full reproducibility). This four-tier hierarchy enables systematic comparison of model configurations across hyperparameter settings, feature sets, and algorithm choices. Keyword tip: when a scenario involves tracking, comparing, and organizing multiple training runs with varying hyperparameters and algorithm choices in SageMaker, the presence of all four terms — Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, and Trackers — uniquely identifies the correct hierarchical framework.
Hyperparameters, Experiments, Trackers, Notebooks
Explanation
Incorrect. While Experiments and Trackers are legitimate Amazon SageMaker Experiments constructs, 'Hyperparameters' and 'Notebooks' are not first-class organizational components of the SageMaker Experiments tracking framework. Hyperparameters are logged as input parameters within a Tracker rather than serving as a structural tier, and Notebooks are development environments that have no role in the experiment tracking hierarchy. Keyword tip: on MLA-C01, options that mix genuine SageMaker Experiments components with general ML terminology (e.g., 'Hyperparameters') or development tools (e.g., 'Notebooks') as structural units are designed to test whether candidates can distinguish logged artifacts from organizational constructs.
Trackers, Algorithms, Metrics, Pipelines
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Trackers are a valid Amazon SageMaker Experiments component, 'Algorithms' and 'Pipelines' are not core structural components of the SageMaker Experiments hierarchy. Algorithms are part of training job configurations and are logged as input parameters via Trackers, while Pipelines belong to the SageMaker Pipelines orchestration service. Metrics are captured as outputs within Trial Components through Trackers, but they do not constitute a standalone organizational tier within SageMaker Experiments. Keyword tip: when an answer option conflates SageMaker Experiments tracking constructs with SageMaker Pipelines orchestration concepts or generic ML terms like 'Algorithms' and 'Metrics' as structural units, it is a strong signal to eliminate that option.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the Amazon SageMaker Experiments component hierarchy.

It focuses on identifying the correct organizational structure used to track and compare multiple model training runs, configurations, metrics, and artifacts.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Pipelines, Trials, Stages, Logs: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is a separate workflow orchestration service. Stages and Logs are not core organizational components of the Amazon SageMaker Experiments hierarchy.

    Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, Trackers: This is the correct option. These are the core components used by Amazon SageMaker Experiments to organize, track, compare, and reproduce ML experiments.

    Hyperparameters, Experiments, Trackers, Notebooks: This option is incorrect. Hyperparameters can be logged, but they are not a structural tier in the Experiments hierarchy. Notebooks are development environments, not experiment tracking components.

    Trackers, Algorithms, Metrics, Pipelines: This option is incorrect. Trackers are valid, but algorithms and metrics are logged values, not top-level hierarchy components. Pipelines belong to Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, not Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

Why Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, and Trackers fit this scenario.

The team needs to evaluate multiple model configurations.

Each run may use different hyperparameters, input features, or algorithm choices.

They need to compare results and identify the best-performing model.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

An Experiment is the top-level container for related ML work.

A Trial represents a specific experiment run or attempt, such as one training configuration.

A Trial Component captures a specific step or part of the run, such as preprocessing, training, or evaluation.

A Tracker records parameters, metrics, artifacts, and other metadata needed for comparison and reproducibility.

This hierarchy allows the team to organize model development work systematically instead of tracking results manually in notebooks or spreadsheets.

It also helps distinguish experiment tracking from workflow orchestration.

Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracks and compares ML runs.

Amazon SageMaker Pipelines orchestrates automated ML workflows.

They can work together, but they are not the same service and do not use the same component vocabulary.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Experiment organization: Use Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

    Multiple training runs: Represent runs as Trials.

    Step-level tracking: Use Trial Components for parts of the workflow.

    Metric comparison: Track evaluation metrics across runs.

    Hyperparameter comparison: Log configuration values for each run.

    Artifact lineage: Preserve inputs, outputs, and model artifacts.

    Reproducibility: Use Trackers to record metadata needed to reproduce results.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Track and compare multiple training runs with different hyperparameters, features, and algorithms = Amazon SageMaker Experiments using Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, and Trackers.

Remember:

    Experiment: Top-level group for related ML work.

    Trial: One run or candidate configuration inside an experiment.

    Trial Component: A step or component within a trial, such as preprocessing or training.

    Tracker: Logs parameters, metrics, artifacts, and metadata.

    Amazon SageMaker Experiments: Experiment tracking and comparison.

    Amazon SageMaker Pipelines: Workflow orchestration and automation.

    Metrics and hyperparameters: Logged data, not hierarchy tiers.

Official AWS references.

    Manage machine learning with Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Experiments, Trials, Trial Components, Trackers, experiment tracking hierarchy, hyperparameter comparison, MLOps, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines vs. Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

Question 17Skipped

A sports analytics company, PeakPlay Analytics, uses machine learning to analyze player performance and game environment monitoring data.


The company needs to ingest raw data from multiple sources, including CSV files and relational databases. The team must perform complex cleaning and join operations on the data.


After preprocessing, the processed data should be used to create a curated set of features, including player age, injury history, and real-time sensor readings from wearables.


The company also wants to maintain a reusable, version-controlled feature repository that can be shared across different machine learning models and multiple teams.


Which combination of AWS services should PeakPlay Analytics use to preprocess the data, create features, and maintain the feature repository?
Use Amazon Athena to preprocess the data, store the features in Amazon S3, and use SageMaker Autopilot to automatically train models.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Athena is a serverless interactive query service optimized for ad-hoc SQL queries against data in Amazon S3; it is not designed for complex, iterative data cleaning pipelines or feature engineering workflows. Furthermore, plain Amazon S3 object storage provides no native versioning, lineage tracking, or governance capabilities for machine learning features — those capabilities are specifically provided by SageMaker Feature Store. SageMaker Autopilot automates model selection and training but does not address the feature management requirement stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires structured feature versioning and cross-team reuse, Athena combined with S3 alone is always insufficient as a substitute for a purpose-built feature store.
Use AWS Glue for data cleaning and transformation, store the engineered features in Amazon DynamoDB, and use SageMaker training jobs to train models.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a capable ETL service and can handle data cleaning and joins; however, it does not provide the version-controlled, ML-specific feature storage that the scenario requires. Amazon DynamoDB is a key-value and document NoSQL database that lacks native ML feature governance, point-in-time retrieval, and the online/offline store duality that SageMaker Feature Store offers. This combination would require significant custom engineering to replicate Feature Store capabilities, making it architecturally inappropriate. Keyword tip: whenever the requirement explicitly mentions version-controlled feature storage for reuse across ML models and teams, DynamoDB is not an appropriate substitute for SageMaker Feature Store.
Correct answer
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler for data preprocessing and feature engineering, SageMaker Feature Store to store and manage features with version control, and SageMaker Notebooks as the integrated environment to train and evaluate models.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Data Wrangler provides a visual, low-code interface for importing data from heterogeneous sources — including Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, relational databases, and CSV files — performing complex cleaning, joining, and feature engineering transformations, and exporting the resulting flow directly into a SageMaker Pipeline or SageMaker Feature Store. SageMaker Feature Store provides a fully managed, version-controlled, dual-layer (online and offline) repository specifically designed for ML features, enabling consistent reuse across models and teams with built-in lineage tracking. SageMaker Notebooks offer a tightly integrated development environment for experimenting with and iterating on training code. This combination directly addresses all three stated requirements: multi-source ingestion and preprocessing, curated feature creation, and versioned feature management. Keyword tip: SageMaker Data Wrangler combined with SageMaker Feature Store is the canonical AWS answer for end-to-end feature engineering with governed, cross-team reuse in an MLOps architecture.
Use AWS Glue DataBrew for data preprocessing, store the features in Amazon Redshift, and use SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a pre-built model.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew is a visual data preparation tool useful for profiling and cleaning datasets, but it is less integrated with the SageMaker feature engineering ecosystem than SageMaker Data Wrangler for ML-specific pipelines. Amazon Redshift is a cloud data warehouse optimized for analytical query workloads; while it can store tabular data, it does not natively provide ML feature versioning, lineage tracking, or the online/offline serving model that SageMaker Feature Store delivers. SageMaker JumpStart provides pre-built model solutions and fine-tuning capabilities but does not fulfill the version-controlled feature repository requirement. Keyword tip: Amazon Redshift is appropriate for large-scale analytical queries, not as a replacement for a purpose-built feature store in an MLOps architecture requiring versioning and cross-team feature reuse.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS-native data preparation and feature management services for ML workflows.

It focuses on using Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for preprocessing and feature engineering, Amazon SageMaker Feature Store for reusable feature management, and SageMaker notebooks for model development and evaluation.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon Athena to preprocess the data, store the features in Amazon S3, and use SageMaker Autopilot to automatically train models: This option is incorrect. Amazon Athena is useful for querying data in Amazon S3, but it is not a full feature engineering workflow tool. Plain Amazon S3 storage also does not provide ML-specific feature management, online serving, offline serving, or feature governance.

    Use AWS Glue for data cleaning and transformation, store the engineered features in Amazon DynamoDB, and use SageMaker training jobs to train models: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue can perform ETL, but Amazon DynamoDB is not a purpose-built ML feature repository. It lacks the native online/offline feature store pattern and ML feature governance provided by Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

    Use SageMaker Data Wrangler for data preprocessing and feature engineering, SageMaker Feature Store to store and manage features with version control, and SageMaker Notebooks as the integrated environment to train and evaluate models: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler handles preprocessing and feature engineering, Amazon SageMaker Feature Store manages reusable ML features, and SageMaker notebooks support model development and evaluation.

    Use AWS Glue DataBrew for data preprocessing, store the features in Amazon Redshift, and use SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a pre-built model: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew can clean and transform data, and Amazon Redshift can store analytical datasets, but Redshift is not a purpose-built feature store with online and offline serving for ML. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart accelerates model deployment but does not solve feature repository requirements.

Why Data Wrangler, Feature Store, and SageMaker notebooks fit this scenario.

The company needs to ingest data from multiple sources.

The team must clean, join, and transform the raw data.

They also need to create curated features such as player age, injury history, and real-time sensor readings.

Most importantly, the features must be stored in a reusable, version-controlled feature repository that multiple teams and models can use.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler and Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler provides a managed visual environment for data preparation and feature engineering.

It can help teams import data, clean missing values, transform columns, join datasets, and prepare ML-ready features.

Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is the purpose-built repository for ML features.

It supports an offline store for batch training and historical analysis.

It also supports an online store for low-latency feature retrieval during real-time inference.

SageMaker notebooks provide an integrated environment for training, experimentation, and evaluation using those prepared features.

This combination keeps the workflow inside the SageMaker ecosystem and avoids custom feature management built on generic storage services.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Multi-source preprocessing: Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Data cleaning and joins: Prepare raw CSV, relational, and sensor data for ML.

    Feature engineering: Create curated features for downstream models.

    Reusable feature repository: Use Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

    Offline feature access: Support batch training datasets.

    Online feature access: Support real-time inference lookups.

    Cross-team reuse: Share governed features across multiple models and teams.

Exam tip.

Data preprocessing + feature engineering + reusable version-controlled feature repository = Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler + Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Question 18Skipped

An education technology company, LearnBridge Academy, has deployed a machine learning model into production using Amazon SageMaker.


The model predicts student engagement scores based on platform interaction data. Over time, the model’s performance begins to degrade because of shifts in student behavior patterns caused by curriculum changes and seasonal enrollment cycles.


The data science team wants to continuously monitor the model’s input data distribution and automatically trigger periodic model retraining whenever significant data drift is detected.


Which combination of AWS services should the team use to address both the monitoring and automated retraining requirements?
Correct answer
SageMaker Model Monitor for monitoring data drift, and SageMaker Pipelines to automate model retraining.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Model Monitor is purpose-built to continuously monitor ML models in production by tracking statistical deviations in the input data distribution (data drift), as well as model quality and feature attribution drift — precisely the type of degradation described when student behavior patterns shift over time. SageMaker Pipelines provides a fully managed CI/CD orchestration capability natively integrated with SageMaker, enabling automated end-to-end retraining workflows that can be triggered based on monitoring outcomes, including drift threshold violations. This is the AWS-recommended MLOps pattern for maintaining production model performance without manual intervention. Keyword tip: when a question pairs 'production input data drift detection' with 'automated retraining orchestration within SageMaker,' SageMaker Model Monitor combined with SageMaker Pipelines is the canonical, natively integrated answer.
Amazon CloudWatch for monitoring model performance metrics, and SageMaker Autopilot to automatically retrain the model on a schedule.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch can capture infrastructure-level and invocation metrics (e.g., latency, error rates, invocation counts) from SageMaker endpoints, but it is not designed to perform statistical data distribution monitoring or detect feature-level drift in ML model inputs. SageMaker Autopilot automates model building from scratch using AutoML techniques; it is not the recommended service for triggering periodic retraining based on drift signals detected in an existing production deployment. Keyword tip: CloudWatch combined with Autopilot addresses infrastructure observability and initial model discovery, respectively — not continuous drift-aware retraining of a deployed production model.
SageMaker Clarify for continuous bias and drift monitoring, and AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the retraining workflow.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Clarify can detect bias drift and feature attribution drift in production endpoints, but its primary role is explainability and fairness analysis rather than comprehensive statistical input data distribution monitoring. AWS Step Functions is a valid general-purpose orchestration engine and can technically coordinate retraining workflows across AWS services; however, SageMaker Pipelines is the natively integrated, purpose-built MLOps orchestration service for SageMaker training jobs, offering built-in pipeline lineage and direct integration with Model Monitor drift alerts. Keyword tip: SageMaker Clarify is the correct tool specifically for bias and explainability monitoring, not general data drift; prefer SageMaker Pipelines over Step Functions when the entire workflow resides within the SageMaker ecosystem.
SageMaker Feature Store to track changes in feature distributions, and Amazon EventBridge with AWS Lambda to trigger retraining jobs.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Feature Store is designed to store, share, and serve ML features for training and inference consistency; it does not provide statistical monitoring of live feature distributions or emit drift alerts for production models. While an Amazon EventBridge rule combined with AWS Lambda can technically invoke SageMaker training jobs, this bespoke event-driven architecture lacks the native drift thresholding, baseline comparison, and pipeline lineage tracking that SageMaker Model Monitor and SageMaker Pipelines provide as an integrated solution. Keyword tip: Feature Store manages feature availability and reuse across training and inference, not production monitoring; custom EventBridge and Lambda patterns introduce unnecessary complexity when SageMaker's native MLOps tooling directly satisfies the full requirement.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

It focuses on using Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to detect data drift or model quality degradation and Amazon SageMaker Pipelines to automate retraining workflows.

    Amazon CloudWatch for monitoring model performance metrics, and SageMaker Autopilot to automatically retrain the model on a schedule: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch monitors infrastructure and endpoint metrics such as latency, errors, and invocations. It does not perform ML-specific data drift analysis. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot is used for AutoML model building, not drift-triggered retraining orchestration.

    SageMaker Clarify for continuous bias and drift monitoring, and AWS Step Functions to orchestrate the retraining workflow: This option is not the best answer. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is used for bias and explainability analysis, but the scenario focuses on general input distribution drift and performance degradation. AWS Step Functions can orchestrate workflows, but Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is the SageMaker-native MLOps service.

    SageMaker Feature Store to track changes in feature distributions, and Amazon EventBridge with AWS Lambda to trigger retraining jobs: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Feature Store stores and serves features, but it does not monitor production model drift by itself. Amazon EventBridge and AWS Lambda can trigger workflows, but this custom pattern lacks the native monitoring and lineage capabilities of Model Monitor and Pipelines.

Why SageMaker Model Monitor and SageMaker Pipelines fit this scenario.

The model is already deployed in production.

Its performance is degrading over time.

The cause is likely a shift in student behavior patterns driven by curriculum changes and seasonal enrollment cycles.

That points to data drift or model quality degradation.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is designed to monitor deployed SageMaker models in production.

It can compare live inference data against a baseline and detect statistical changes in input distributions.

It can also monitor model quality when ground truth labels become available.

When drift or quality degradation is detected, the team needs an automated retraining workflow.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Amazon SageMaker Pipelines can orchestrate preprocessing, training, evaluation, model registration, and deployment steps.

Together, these services provide the SageMaker-native pattern for detecting production model degradation and automating retraining.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    End-to-end orchestration: Automate preprocessing, training, evaluation, and registration.

    SageMaker-native integration: Avoid fragmented custom orchestration.

    Low operational overhead: Use managed MLOps services instead of manual retraining.

Question 19Skipped

A data science team at PropVantage Analytics, a commercial real estate firm, is reviewing foundational machine learning concepts as part of an internal training initiative.

The team is evaluating several real-world use cases and needs to classify each one by its machine learning paradigm.

Which scenario is the best example of unsupervised learning?
Training a model on a labeled dataset of property transaction records to predict whether a given deal is likely to close within 30 days or fall through
Explanation
Incorrect. Training a classifier on a labeled dataset of property transaction records — where each record is explicitly tagged as 'closes within 30 days' or 'falls through' — is a binary classification task, which belongs to supervised learning. The presence of ground-truth labels is the defining characteristic of supervised learning. Keyword tip: whenever a task involves predicting a discrete class from labeled training data, it is supervised classification, not unsupervised learning.
Building a time-series regression model to forecast quarterly commercial lease renewal rates for the next fiscal year using historical renewal labels
Explanation
Incorrect. Forecasting quarterly lease renewal rates using historical renewal labels is a regression task under supervised learning. The model learns a mapping from input features to a continuous or semi-continuous numerical target (renewal rate) derived from labeled observations. Keyword tip: any scenario that involves predicting a numerical output from labeled historical data — even if the output is a probability or rate — indicates supervised regression, not unsupervised learning.
Correct answer
Segmenting commercial properties into distinct behavioral groups based on occupancy patterns, foot traffic, and tenant service interactions, without any predefined category labels
Explanation
Correct. Segmenting commercial properties into distinct groups based solely on occupancy patterns, foot traffic, and tenant service interactions — without relying on predefined labels — is a clustering task and the canonical example of unsupervised learning. The algorithm (e.g., k-means, hierarchical clustering, DBSCAN) discovers latent structure in the feature space entirely from unlabeled data. In Amazon SageMaker, the built-in K-Means algorithm is commonly used for this purpose. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario describes discovering natural groupings or patterns in data with no labels provided, it is an unsupervised learning problem.
Fine-tuning a gradient boosting model trained on historical tenant billing records with labeled outcomes to predict whether a tenant will default on their next lease payment
Explanation
Incorrect. Predicting tenant payment default using a gradient boosting model trained on historical billing records with labeled outcomes (default / no default) is a supervised binary classification task. The labeled outcomes — ground-truth default flags — make this a supervised learning problem regardless of the algorithm's complexity or the domain from which the data originates. Keyword tip: the presence of labeled training examples with known target values — whether for regression or classification — is the hallmark of supervised learning and distinguishes it from unsupervised approaches.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of fundamental machine learning paradigms.

It focuses on identifying unsupervised learning when the goal is to discover patterns or groupings in unlabeled data.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Training a model on a labeled dataset of property transaction records to predict whether a given deal is likely to close within 30 days or fall through: This option is incorrect. This is supervised binary classification because the model learns from labeled outcomes, such as “closed” or “fell through.”

    Building a time-series regression model to forecast quarterly commercial lease renewal rates for the next fiscal year using historical renewal labels: This option is incorrect. This is supervised regression because the model predicts a numerical target using historical labeled data.

    Segmenting commercial properties into distinct behavioral groups based on occupancy patterns, foot traffic, and tenant service interactions, without any predefined category labels: This is the correct option. This is unsupervised learning because the algorithm discovers natural groupings in unlabeled data.

    Fine-tuning a gradient boosting model trained on historical tenant billing records with labeled outcomes to predict whether a tenant will default on their next lease payment: This option is incorrect. This is supervised binary classification because the model is trained on labeled outcomes such as default or no default.

Why property segmentation without labels fits this scenario.

The task is to group commercial properties based on behavior.

The features include occupancy patterns, foot traffic, and tenant service interactions.

There are no predefined category labels.

There is no target variable telling the model what each property group should be.

That points directly to unsupervised learning.

In unsupervised learning, the algorithm looks for structure in the data without labeled outcomes.

For this scenario, the model might discover groups such as high-traffic properties, low-engagement properties, seasonal occupancy properties, or properties with similar tenant interaction patterns.

This is a clustering problem.

Common clustering algorithms include k-means, hierarchical clustering, and DBSCAN.

In Amazon SageMaker, the built-in K-Means algorithm is commonly used for clustering and segmentation tasks.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    No labels: The data does not include predefined target categories.

    Pattern discovery: The goal is to find hidden structure in the dataset.

    Grouping: Properties are segmented into behavioral clusters.

    Unsupervised learning: The model learns from input features only.

    Clustering: The correct ML task type is clustering, not classification or regression.

    SageMaker built-in algorithm: Amazon SageMaker K-Means is a common option for this pattern.

    Avoid supervised distractors: Any option with known outcomes or labels is supervised learning.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

No predefined labels + discovering natural groups = unsupervised learning.

For this question, the correct answer is:

Segmenting commercial properties into distinct behavioral groups based on occupancy patterns, foot traffic, and tenant service interactions, without any predefined category labels.

Remember:

    Unsupervised learning: Finds structure in unlabeled data.

    Clustering: Groups similar records together.

    K-means: Common clustering algorithm.

    Amazon SageMaker K-Means: Built-in SageMaker algorithm for clustering.

    Supervised classification: Predicts discrete labels from labeled data.

    Supervised regression: Predicts numeric values from labeled data.

    Ground-truth labels: Strong signal that the task is supervised, not unsupervised.

Official AWS references.
