Question 31Skipped

A security officer at Meridian Community Bank wants to ensure that all Amazon S3 buckets in the account comply with the bank’s security standards.


The required controls include enabling server-side encryption and blocking public access to all buckets.


The bank also requires continuous monitoring and real-time alerts whenever any bucket becomes non-compliant.


Which combination of AWS services should be used to meet these requirements?
Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs to monitor S3 bucket changes and deploy AWS Lambda functions to automatically remediate misconfigurations as they are detected.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Amazon CloudWatch Logs can ingest log data including S3-related events, it does not natively evaluate resource configurations against compliance rules. Relying on AWS Lambda to reactively correct misconfigurations introduces operational complexity, potential race conditions, and lacks the continuous, rule-based evaluation model that AWS Config provides. A Lambda-based remediation approach also requires custom logic for every compliance rule, increasing maintenance burden and the risk of configuration drift going undetected between invocations. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires 'continuous rule-based compliance evaluation' rather than reactive event-driven remediation, AWS Config is the purpose-built service to reach for over custom Lambda-based pipelines.
Set up Amazon CloudWatch Alarms targeting S3 bucket configuration change metrics, including encryption status and access control policy modifications.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms are designed to monitor time-series numerical performance metrics such as CPU utilization, error rates, and request latency; they cannot track resource configuration state changes such as S3 encryption settings or public access block status. CloudWatch Alarms operate on metric thresholds published to Amazon CloudWatch Metrics, and S3 bucket configuration compliance is not surfaced as a CloudWatch metric. Attempting to use CloudWatch Alarms for configuration compliance would require extensive custom instrumentation and still would not provide the structured, policy-as-rules evaluation model native to AWS Config. Keyword tip: CloudWatch Alarms act on numerical metric thresholds; for configuration-level compliance state, AWS Config is the correct service.
Use AWS CloudTrail to track all S3 API calls and configure Amazon SNS notifications to alert the security team whenever encryption or public access settings are modified.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records API activity and can log calls such as PutBucketEncryption or DeleteBucketPublicAccessBlock, but it does not continuously evaluate whether a bucket is currently compliant with a defined policy baseline. CloudTrail is an auditing and forensics tool that captures the history of API calls; it does not maintain an up-to-date compliance state for each resource. While Amazon SNS notifications can be routed from CloudTrail events via Amazon EventBridge, this approach requires custom rule writing and does not provide the structured, managed-rules compliance model of AWS Config, making it operationally fragile at scale. Keyword tip: CloudTrail captures who called which API and when; AWS Config captures whether a resource's current configuration satisfies a defined compliance rule.
Correct answer
Configure AWS Config with managed rules to continuously evaluate S3 bucket settings against defined security standards, and use Amazon EventBridge to trigger real-time alerts on non-compliant resources.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Config is the purpose-built AWS service for continuous configuration compliance evaluation. It provides managed rules such as s3-bucket-server-side-encryption-enabled and s3-bucket-public-read-prohibited that automatically and continuously assess S3 bucket configurations against defined security standards without requiring custom code. When a resource transitions to a non-compliant state, AWS Config integrates natively with Amazon EventBridge to deliver real-time, event-driven alerts and can also invoke AWS Systems Manager Automation documents or AWS Lambda functions for automated remediation. This combination directly satisfies both the continuous compliance monitoring and real-time alerting requirements stated by Meridian Community Bank's security officer. Keyword tip: 'continuous compliance evaluation of resource configurations + real-time alerts on non-compliant state' is the canonical signal for AWS Config paired with Amazon EventBridge.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS services for configuration compliance monitoring and real-time security alerting.

It focuses on using AWS Config managed rules to continuously evaluate Amazon S3 bucket security settings and Amazon EventBridge to trigger alerts when resources become non-compliant.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs to monitor S3 bucket changes and deploy AWS Lambda functions to automatically remediate misconfigurations as they are detected: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Logs stores and queries log data, but it does not natively evaluate resource configurations against compliance rules.

    Set up Amazon CloudWatch Alarms targeting S3 bucket configuration change metrics, including encryption status and access control policy modifications: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms monitor numerical metrics and thresholds. They are not designed to evaluate S3 bucket configuration compliance such as encryption or public access settings.

    Use AWS CloudTrail to track all S3 API calls and configure Amazon SNS notifications to alert the security team whenever encryption or public access settings are modified: This option is not the best answer. AWS CloudTrail records API activity, but it does not continuously evaluate whether the current configuration of each S3 bucket is compliant.

    Configure AWS Config with managed rules to continuously evaluate S3 bucket settings against defined security standards, and use Amazon EventBridge to trigger real-time alerts on non-compliant resources: This is the correct option. AWS Config evaluates resource configurations against compliance rules, and Amazon EventBridge can route non-compliance events to alerting or remediation targets.

Why AWS Config and Amazon EventBridge fit this scenario.

The bank needs to ensure that all Amazon S3 buckets follow security standards.

The standards include server-side encryption and blocking public access.

The bank also needs continuous monitoring.

If a bucket becomes non-compliant, the security team must receive real-time alerts.

That points to AWS Config.

AWS Config continuously records and evaluates AWS resource configurations.

It supports managed rules that can check whether S3 buckets have required security controls enabled.

For example, managed rules can evaluate whether S3 bucket server-side encryption is enabled or whether public read access is prohibited.

When a resource becomes non-compliant, AWS Config can publish compliance change events.

Amazon EventBridge can route those events to targets such as Amazon SNS, AWS Lambda, or automation workflows.

This gives the bank both continuous compliance evaluation and real-time notification.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    S3 security compliance: Use AWS Config managed rules.

    Server-side encryption validation: Check whether S3 buckets meet encryption standards.

    Public access control: Detect buckets that allow public access.

    Continuous monitoring: Evaluate resource configuration over time.

    Real-time alerts: Use Amazon EventBridge to route non-compliance events.

    Security governance: Track configuration state against defined policies.

    Low operational overhead: Use managed rules instead of custom Lambda-only checks.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Continuous configuration compliance + S3 security standards + real-time non-compliance alerts = AWS Config + Amazon EventBridge.

Remember:

    AWS Config: Tracks resource configuration and evaluates compliance.

    Managed rules: Prebuilt compliance checks for common security requirements.

    Amazon EventBridge: Routes compliance change events to alerting or remediation targets.

    Amazon CloudWatch Alarms: Metric threshold alarms, not configuration compliance checks.

    Amazon CloudWatch Logs: Log ingestion and querying, not managed compliance evaluation.

    AWS CloudTrail: API audit history, not current-state compliance evaluation.

    Amazon SNS: Common notification target for security alerts.

Official AWS references.

    What is AWS Config?

    AWS Config managed rules.

Key exam terms: AWS Config, managed rules, continuous compliance evaluation, Amazon EventBridge, S3 bucket security, server-side encryption, public access block, configuration drift, real-time alerts, ML data store governance.

Question 32Skipped

A data science team at AxleDrive Motors, an automotive manufacturer, is using Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to perform text classification labeling on internal engineering and compliance documents.


The team wants to reduce overall labeling costs by automating a subset of labeling tasks. At the same time, any sensitive or proprietary data must be reviewed only by trusted internal personnel.


Which combination of strategies should the team use to achieve both goals?
Rely entirely on a private workforce to handle both simple and complex labeling tasks, with no automation.
Explanation
Incorrect. Relying exclusively on a private workforce for all labeling tasks — both simple and complex — eliminates the cost savings achievable through automation. SageMaker Ground Truth's active learning (automated data labeling) feature is specifically designed to reduce human labeling effort by automatically labeling high-confidence data points, so bypassing it entirely is cost-inefficient. This option satisfies the data security requirement but completely sacrifices cost optimization, making it a partial-solution distractor. Keyword tip: When a scenario requires both 'cost reduction' and 'sensitive data protection,' eliminating automation entirely is always suboptimal in SageMaker Ground Truth labeling architectures.
Correct answer
Use a private workforce for sensitive data, and leverage active learning (automated data labeling) to automate simple, high-confidence labeling tasks.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Ground Truth supports a hybrid labeling approach in which a private workforce — composed of internal employees or vetted contractors — handles sensitive data securely, while the automated data labeling (active learning) feature uses an iterative ML model to automatically assign labels to data points it can classify with high confidence. This combination directly satisfies both requirements: data security through private workforce controls, and cost reduction through progressive automation of high-confidence samples. As the active learning model improves over successive labeling rounds, fewer tasks are routed to human reviewers, further reducing costs. Keyword tip: On the MLA-C01 exam, the phrase 'sensitive or proprietary data + cost reduction via automation' should immediately trigger the private workforce combined with SageMaker Ground Truth active learning pattern.
Configure Amazon Mechanical Turk workers to handle sensitive proprietary data, and use automated labeling only for the most complex edge cases.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Mechanical Turk provides access to a large public crowd workforce made up of external, anonymous contractors. Assigning sensitive or proprietary engineering and compliance documents to Mechanical Turk workers introduces significant data security and confidentiality risks, directly violating the requirement to restrict access to trusted internal personnel. Additionally, routing complex edge cases — rather than simple, high-confidence samples — to automated models contradicts best practices, since active learning performs most reliably on straightforward, high-confidence data points. Keyword tip: On MLA-C01, any option that pairs Amazon Mechanical Turk with sensitive or regulated data handling is a strong candidate for elimination, regardless of how the automation component is framed.
Engage a third-party vendor workforce for sensitive data while relying on fully automated models to label all remaining tasks regardless of confidence score.
Explanation
Incorrect. Using a third-party vendor workforce for sensitive proprietary data introduces the same confidentiality and data governance risks as a public workforce, failing to meet the requirement that sensitive data be handled exclusively by trusted internal personnel. Furthermore, applying fully automated labeling to all remaining tasks without confidence-score thresholds undermines data quality: SageMaker Ground Truth's automated data labeling is designed to label only tasks that meet a defined confidence threshold, deferring ambiguous or low-confidence samples to human reviewers rather than forcing a label on every item. Keyword tip: On MLA-C01, 'third-party vendor workforce for sensitive data' and 'fully automated labeling regardless of confidence score' are both anti-patterns that trade data governance and accuracy for perceived convenience.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth workforce options and automated data labeling.

It focuses on combining a private workforce with automated data labeling to protect sensitive data while reducing labeling cost.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Rely entirely on a private workforce to handle both simple and complex labeling tasks, with no automation: This option is not the best answer. A private workforce protects sensitive data, but using humans for every task misses the cost savings provided by Ground Truth automated data labeling.

    Use a private workforce for sensitive data, and leverage active learning to automate simple, high-confidence labeling tasks: This is the correct option. A private workforce keeps sensitive data restricted to trusted personnel, while automated data labeling reduces human labeling effort for high-confidence items.

    Configure Amazon Mechanical Turk workers to handle sensitive proprietary data, and use automated labeling only for the most complex edge cases: This option is incorrect. Amazon Mechanical Turk is a public workforce and is not appropriate for sensitive or proprietary data. Automated labeling should be used for high-confidence tasks, not complex edge cases.

    Engage a third-party vendor workforce for sensitive data while relying on fully automated models to label all remaining tasks regardless of confidence score: This option is incorrect. A vendor workforce may violate the requirement that sensitive data be reviewed only by trusted internal personnel. Fully automated labeling without confidence thresholds can also reduce label quality.

Why private workforce and automated data labeling fit this scenario.

The company needs to label sensitive engineering and compliance documents.

Sensitive or proprietary data must be reviewed only by trusted internal personnel.

At the same time, the team wants to reduce labeling cost.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth with a private workforce and automated data labeling.

A private workforce allows the organization to restrict labeling access to internal employees or approved contractors.

This protects confidential documents from public or untrusted external workers.

Automated data labeling uses an active learning workflow.

Ground Truth can automatically label examples when the model is confident enough.

Lower-confidence or ambiguous examples are routed to human reviewers.

This reduces the number of human labeling tasks while preserving quality and governance.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Sensitive data protection: Use a private workforce.

    Trusted reviewers: Restrict access to internal personnel or vetted workers.

    Cost reduction: Use automated data labeling.

    Active learning: Let the model label high-confidence examples.

    Human review: Send uncertain or complex examples to private labelers.

    Label quality: Avoid forcing automated labels when confidence is low.

    Managed labeling workflow: Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Sensitive data + trusted internal reviewers + lower labeling cost = Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth private workforce + automated data labeling.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth: Managed data labeling service.

    Private workforce: Best for sensitive, proprietary, or regulated data.

    Amazon Mechanical Turk: Public workforce, not suitable for sensitive data.

    Vendor workforce: External managed workforce, not ideal when only internal personnel may view the data.

    Automated data labeling: Reduces labeling cost by labeling high-confidence examples automatically.

    Active learning: Selects which examples need human review.

    Confidence threshold: Determines when automated labels are acceptable.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

    Use an Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth private workforce.

    Automate data labeling with Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

Question 33Skipped

A data scientist at NovaPetro Chemicals is responsible for ensuring that a machine learning model used to predict equipment failure risk across refinery operations is trustworthy, transparent, and able to avoid risks such as bias and poor generalization.


The team must apply a technique that evaluates model fairness and explainability both before and after training.


The solution must also support regulatory compliance by providing documented evidence of bias detection and model explainability.


Which AWS service is most appropriate for achieving these objectives?
AWS Glue
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a fully managed ETL service used to discover, catalog, and transform data for analytics and ML pipelines. It does not provide bias detection, fairness analysis, or explainability reports at any stage of the model lifecycle. Configuring AWS Glue crawlers or jobs has no bearing on pre-training or post-training fairness assessments, nor does it produce compliance artifacts. Keyword tip: when a scenario combines pre-training or post-training bias analysis with regulatory documentation requirements, AWS Glue — an ETL and data cataloging tool — is never the appropriate service.
Correct answer
Amazon SageMaker Clarify
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is purpose-built to address fairness, bias, and explainability requirements across the ML lifecycle. It supports pre-training bias detection by analyzing training data for class imbalance or label skew before a model is trained, as well as post-training bias detection by evaluating model predictions for discriminatory patterns across demographic or categorical groups. Additionally, it generates SHAP-based feature attribution explanations and produces human-readable bias reports that can serve as compliance artifacts to satisfy regulatory audit requirements. Keyword tip: any scenario combining 'pre-training bias,' 'post-training bias,' 'explainability,' 'SHAP values,' or 'regulatory compliance documentation for ML fairness' points directly to Amazon SageMaker Clarify.
Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is designed to detect data drift, model quality degradation, and statistical deviations in live inference data flowing through a deployed SageMaker endpoint. Although it operates within the monitoring domain and can integrate with Amazon SageMaker Clarify for bias drift detection post-deployment, it does not independently perform pre-training bias detection or generate the explainability and fairness reports required prior to training. Keyword tip: when the requirement explicitly includes pre-training assessment alongside regulatory explainability documentation, SageMaker Model Monitor alone is insufficient — SageMaker Clarify is the primary tool for the full pre- and post-training fairness workflow.
Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is a data labeling service that facilitates the creation of high-quality annotated training datasets through human reviewers, including Amazon Mechanical Turk and private labeling workforces. While well-labeled data can indirectly reduce annotation-level bias, the service provides no bias detection algorithms, explainability analysis, or compliance-grade fairness reports that could satisfy a regulatory mandate. Keyword tip: 'Ground Truth' scenarios involve dataset annotation, labeling workflows, and active learning — not model fairness auditing, bias quantification, or explainability transparency.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Clarify and its role in responsible AI, bias detection, and model explainability.

It focuses on selecting Clarify when the requirement includes pre-training bias analysis, post-training bias analysis, explainability, and documented evidence for regulatory compliance.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    AWS Glue: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue is an ETL and data cataloging service. It can transform and prepare datasets, but it does not provide bias detection, fairness analysis, SHAP explanations, or compliance-ready explainability reports.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Clarify detects bias before and after training and provides explainability using SHAP-based feature attribution. It also generates reports that support audit and compliance requirements.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: This option is not the best answer. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor monitors deployed models for data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift. However, by itself, it does not provide the full pre-training and post-training bias analysis workflow required in this scenario.

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is used for data labeling and annotation workflows. It does not perform model fairness analysis, bias detection, or explainability reporting.

Why Amazon SageMaker Clarify fits this scenario.

The team needs to ensure that the ML model is trustworthy and transparent.

They must assess fairness and explainability before and after training.

They also need documented evidence of bias detection for regulatory compliance.

For example, it can identify class imbalance, label skew, or uneven representation across groups.

Clarify can also evaluate model predictions after training to detect whether the model produces different outcomes across demographic, categorical, or operational groups.

This helps teams understand which features influence model predictions.

Clarify can generate reports that support governance, auditability, and responsible AI requirements.

    Post-training bias detection: Evaluate model predictions after training.

    Fairness assessment: Detect disparities across relevant groups.

    Explainability: Use SHAP-based feature attribution.

    Regulatory documentation: Generate bias and explainability reports.

    Responsible AI: Support trustworthy and transparent ML workflows.

    AWS-native capability: Use a purpose-built SageMaker service instead of ETL, labeling, or generic monitoring tools.

    Post-training bias: Bias detected in model predictions after training.

Question 34Skipped

An agricultural cooperative, HarvestLink Co-op, ingests high volumes of real-time crop yield telemetry and supply chain logistics data into Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.


Multiple downstream ML applications consume this data concurrently.


Because of high-throughput requirements, the cooperative must ensure that adding more consumers does not create bottlenecks or cause consumers to compete for read throughput on the same shards.


Which Amazon Kinesis Data Streams feature should the cooperative configure to meet this requirement?
Correct answer
Enhanced Fan-Out
Explanation
Correct. Enhanced Fan-Out provides each registered consumer with a dedicated read throughput of 2 MB/s per shard via a push-based HTTP/2 subscription model using the SubscribeToShard API. Unlike the standard GetRecords API, where all consumers share the 2 MB/s aggregate read limit per shard, Enhanced Fan-Out eliminates consumer-to-consumer throughput contention entirely, regardless of how many consumers are registered. This is the recommended pattern when multiple simultaneous high-throughput consumers must read from the same Kinesis Data Stream without degrading each other's performance. Keyword tip: 'multiple consumers + dedicated per-consumer throughput + no read bottleneck' → Enhanced Fan-Out with SubscribeToShard.
On-Demand Capacity Mode
Explanation
Incorrect. On-Demand Capacity Mode automatically scales the number of shards in response to aggregate write throughput, relieving operators from manual shard capacity management. However, it does not address read-side bottlenecks caused by multiple consumers competing for shared shard read bandwidth — scaling shards increases total write and aggregate read capacity, but each additional consumer still competes for the same per-shard read quota unless Enhanced Fan-Out is separately configured. Keyword tip: 'automatic shard scaling driven by write throughput' → On-Demand Mode, not a solution for multi-consumer read contention.
Shard-Level Metrics with CloudWatch Auto Scaling
Explanation
Incorrect. Publishing shard-level metrics to Amazon CloudWatch and triggering auto scaling policies based on those metrics is a valid operational practice for managing aggregate stream throughput over time. However, this reactive approach addresses capacity shortfalls by adding shards — which increases write throughput and aggregate read capacity — but does not grant each consumer its own isolated read throughput lane. Multiple consumers continue to share the read bandwidth of each shard under the standard GetRecords model unless Enhanced Fan-Out is enabled. Keyword tip: 'metrics-driven shard scaling via CloudWatch' → operational capacity management, not per-consumer read throughput isolation.
Partition Key-Based Data Routing
Explanation
Incorrect. A Partition Key determines which shard a given record is written to, enabling data distribution and parallel processing across shards on the producer side. While a well-chosen partition key improves write distribution and mitigates hot shards, it is entirely a producer-side concern and does not affect how multiple consumers share read throughput per shard. Consumers still compete for the aggregate 2 MB/s GetRecords limit on each shard regardless of how records were partitioned at ingest time. Keyword tip: 'data distribution across shards at ingest' → Partition Key, but irrelevant to resolving multi-consumer read contention.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor monitors deployed models for data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift.     Regulatory documentation: Generate bias and explainability reports.

    AWS-native capability: Use a purpose-built SageMaker service instead of ETL, labeling, or generic monitoring tools.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Pre-training bias + post-training bias + explainability + regulatory evidence = Amazon SageMaker Clarify.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: Bias detection and explainability across the ML lifecycle.

    Pre-training bias: Bias detected in the training dataset before model training.

    Post-training bias: Bias detected in model predictions after training.

    SHAP values: Explain how features contribute to predictions.

    Fairness report: Useful for audits and compliance documentation.

Question 35Skipped

A data scientist at NexaTel Communications is developing a binary classification model to detect fraudulent billing transactions on a mobile network platform.


After evaluation, the model shows high precision but relatively low recall.


What does this combination of metrics most accurately indicate about the model’s performance?
Correct answer
The model has a high rate of correctly predicted frauds among its positive predictions, but fails to identify many actual fraudulent transactions in the dataset.
Explanation
Correct. High precision means that when the model predicts a transaction as fraudulent, it is correct a large proportion of the time — i.e., very few false positives exist among its positive predictions (Precision = TP / (TP + FP)). However, low recall indicates the model fails to surface many actual fraudulent transactions because it is overly conservative about labeling positives, resulting in a high number of false negatives (Recall = TP / (TP + FN)). In a telecom billing fraud context, this means NexaTel's model raises reliable alerts but allows a significant volume of genuine fraud to go undetected. Keyword tip: When a question pairs high precision with low recall, look for the answer that describes few false positives but many missed true positives (high false negative rate).
The model is performing poorly at identifying true negatives, meaning it frequently misclassifies legitimate transactions as non-fraudulent.
Explanation
Incorrect. Precision and recall are metrics defined entirely with respect to the positive class (fraudulent transactions) and provide no direct information about the model's ability to correctly identify true negatives (legitimate transactions classified as non-fraudulent). True negative performance is captured by specificity or negative predictive value, neither of which is derivable from precision or recall alone. Keyword tip: Any distractor that links precision or recall to true negative performance or specificity is inverting the definitions — these metrics operate exclusively on the positive class.
The model is accurately identifying the majority of fraudulent transactions in the dataset, minimizing missed fraud cases.
Explanation
Incorrect. Accurately identifying the majority of fraudulent transactions corresponds to high recall, which is explicitly the opposite of the low-recall condition stated in the question. Low recall means the model has a high false negative rate, missing a substantial portion of actual fraud cases in the NexaTel billing dataset. This option contradicts the stated constraint and describes the inverse scenario. Keyword tip: High recall means the model captures most actual positives; low recall means many actual positives go undetected — these two states are mutually exclusive.
The model produces consistent predictions across runs, but generates a disproportionately high number of false positive fraud alerts.
Explanation
Incorrect. High precision is directly associated with a low false positive rate, not a high one — a model generating a disproportionate number of false positive fraud alerts would exhibit low precision, because many of its positive predictions would be incorrect. Consistency of predictions across runs is a property related to model variance and reproducibility, which is entirely independent of the precision-recall trade-off. Keyword tip: When a distractor claims that high precision causes many false positives, it inverts the definition; high precision → low FP rate among positive predictions.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

    AWS Glue: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue is an ETL and data cataloging service. It can transform and prepare datasets, but it does not provide bias detection, fairness analysis, SHAP explanations, or compliance-ready explainability reports.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Clarify detects bias before and after training and provides explainability using SHAP-based feature attribution. It also generates reports that support audit and compliance requirements.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: This option is not the best answer. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor monitors deployed models for data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift. However, by itself, it does not provide the full pre-training and post-training bias analysis workflow required in this scenario.

Question 36Skipped

A semiconductor manufacturer, SiliconEdge Technologies, wants to build an automated video transcription and translation pipeline for its multilingual technical support content library.


The solution must transcribe speech from recorded video sessions, translate the resulting text into multiple languages, and generate audio versions of the translated content for playback.


Which combination of AWS services best fulfills all three requirements in a fully managed and purpose-built manner?
Correct answer
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, Amazon Translate for text translation, and Amazon Polly to convert translated text to speech.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Transcribe is a fully managed automatic speech recognition (ASR) service that converts audio and video speech into text. Amazon Translate is a neural machine translation service designed for high-quality, scalable text translation across dozens of languages. Amazon Polly is a text-to-speech service that synthesizes natural-sounding speech from text. Together, these three purpose-built AI services form a cohesive, serverless pipeline that satisfies all three pipeline stages — transcription, translation, and audio synthesis — without requiring custom model training or infrastructure management. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires speech-to-text → text translation → text-to-speech in AWS, the canonical answer is Amazon Transcribe + Amazon Translate + Amazon Polly.
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, deploy a custom translation model on Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoints, and use Amazon Polly for speech synthesis.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Transcribe correctly handles speech-to-text and Amazon Polly handles speech synthesis, deploying a custom translation model on Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoints introduces unnecessary operational overhead. Amazon Translate is the managed, purpose-built service for this task and does not require custom model development, endpoint management, or the associated ML expertise and cost. SageMaker real-time endpoints are appropriate when no managed service meets specific translation requirements — for example, when domain-specific terminology demands fine-tuned models — but that constraint is not stated here. Keyword tip: prefer AWS managed AI services over custom Amazon SageMaker endpoints when a fully managed, purpose-built service exists and the scenario imposes no specialized modeling constraints.
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, Amazon Translate for text translation, and AWS Lambda to orchestrate text-to-speech conversion using third-party libraries.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Transcribe and Amazon Translate correctly address the first two pipeline stages, but AWS Lambda alone cannot perform text-to-speech synthesis. Lambda is a serverless compute service capable of invoking other services and orchestrating workflows; it does not natively produce synthesized audio. Implementing text-to-speech through Lambda would require bundling third-party libraries, introducing maintenance burden, cold-start latency, and runtime size limitations, whereas Amazon Polly is the fully managed service specifically designed for high-quality speech synthesis at scale. Keyword tip: AWS Lambda is a compute and orchestration layer — it does not replace purpose-built AI services such as Amazon Polly for speech synthesis tasks.
Use Amazon Rekognition to extract transcripts from video frames, Amazon Translate for text translation, and Amazon Polly for speech synthesis.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Rekognition is a computer vision service used for image and video analysis tasks such as object detection, facial recognition, scene labeling, and text-in-image extraction. It does not provide automatic speech recognition or transcript extraction from audio tracks embedded in video files. The correct service for converting spoken audio in videos to text is Amazon Transcribe, which processes audio streams rather than visual frames. Keyword tip: Amazon Rekognition analyzes visual content only — it cannot process audio streams or produce text transcripts from speech in video recordings.
Overall explanation

Question 37Skipped

A machine learning engineer at MetroTransit Analytics, a public transportation authority, is responsible for maintaining production ML models deployed on Amazon SageMaker.


The team is implementing a comprehensive monitoring strategy using

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.


During planning, a junior engineer asks what data drift means and why detecting it is important for production model monitoring.


Which statement most accurately describes data drift in machine learning model monitoring, and why is it important to detect?
The sudden failure of a model caused by a defect or bug introduced into the underlying algorithm during a software update.
Explanation
Incorrect. This describes a software or algorithmic failure — for example, a bug introduced during a model update — rather than data drift. Data drift is not caused by code defects; it is driven by natural changes in the real-world data generating process over time, such as shifts in passenger ridership patterns or changes in sensor telemetry from transit vehicles. In MLA-C01 scenarios, watch for distractors that conflate model or system failures with distributional monitoring concepts. Keyword tip: when an option references code defects or software bugs as the cause of model degradation, it is describing an operational or engineering failure, not a data drift scenario.
A condition in which the statistical relationship between input features and the target variable changes over time, causing the model's predicted outputs to deviate systematically from the ground-truth labels.
Explanation
Incorrect. This describes concept drift (also called label drift or model quality drift), which refers specifically to a change in the conditional relationship between input features and the target variable — that is, P(Y|X) changes. Data drift, by contrast, refers to a change in the marginal distribution of the input features themselves — P(X) — regardless of any change in that conditional relationship. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor distinguishes these two types: data quality monitors detect feature distribution shifts, while model quality monitors track prediction accuracy against ground-truth labels. Keyword tip: the phrase 'statistical relationship between input features and the target variable' is the signature of concept drift, not data drift — recognizing this P(Y|X) vs. P(X) distinction is a key MLA-C01 discriminator.
Correct answer
A change in the statistical distribution of input data observed in production over time, such that it no longer reflects the distribution of the data on which the model was originally trained.
Explanation
Correct. Data drift (also called covariate shift or feature drift) refers to a change in the statistical distribution of the input data a model receives in production, relative to the training data distribution. Because a model learns decision boundaries from its training distribution, when the production input distribution diverges — for example, due to seasonal ridership changes, new transit routes, or evolving passenger demographics at a transportation authority — those learned boundaries may no longer be valid, leading to silent performance degradation without any explicit error signal. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor's data quality monitoring capability is designed specifically to detect this type of drift by comparing production feature statistics and constraints against a baseline computed from training data, triggering alerts when violations are detected. Keyword tip: 'input data distribution' + 'production diverges from training' + SageMaker Model Monitor data quality baseline are the canonical signals for a data drift question on the MLA-C01 exam.
A degradation in model serving infrastructure that results in increased latency or endpoint unavailability, ultimately affecting the reliability of real-time inference.
Explanation
Incorrect. Infrastructure downtime, increased endpoint latency, or compute resource exhaustion are operational and availability concerns managed through Amazon CloudWatch metrics, SageMaker endpoint auto scaling, and infrastructure health monitoring — not data drift detection. Data drift is a data-centric concept concerned with statistical distribution shifts, not with serving infrastructure reliability. Keyword tip: when an option references latency, endpoint unavailability, or infrastructure degradation, it belongs to the deployment and operational health domain — associate those signals with Amazon CloudWatch alarms and SageMaker endpoint scaling, not with SageMaker Model Monitor data quality monitoring.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of data drift in production ML monitoring.

It focuses on recognizing data drift as a change in the statistical distribution of production input features compared with the training data distribution.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    The sudden failure of a model caused by a defect or bug introduced into the underlying algorithm during a software update: This option is incorrect. A software defect or algorithm bug is an engineering failure, not data drift. Data drift is caused by changes in the real-world input data distribution over time.

    A condition in which the statistical relationship between input features and the target variable changes over time, causing the model's predicted outputs to deviate systematically from the ground-truth labels: This option is incorrect. This describes concept drift, where the relationship between inputs and labels changes. Data drift focuses on the distribution of input features, not the target relationship.

    A change in the statistical distribution of input data observed in production over time, such that it no longer reflects the distribution of the data on which the model was originally trained: This is the correct option. Data drift occurs when production input data begins to differ statistically from the training data baseline.

    A degradation in model serving infrastructure that results in increased latency or endpoint unavailability, ultimately affecting the reliability of real-time inference: This option is incorrect. Latency and endpoint availability are infrastructure or operational health concerns. They are typically monitored with Amazon CloudWatch, not treated as data drift.

Why production input distribution shift fits this scenario.

The model was trained on historical data.

After deployment, the production data may begin to change.

For example, a public transportation authority may see changes in ridership patterns, route usage, traffic behavior, seasonal demand, or sensor telemetry.

If the input features observed in production no longer match the training data distribution, the model may make less reliable predictions.

That is data drift.

Data drift is also known as covariate shift or feature drift.

It does not necessarily mean the model code is broken.

It also does not necessarily mean the endpoint is unavailable.

The model may continue serving predictions normally, but those predictions may become less accurate because the input data no longer resembles the data the model learned from.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor can help detect this issue.

It creates baseline statistics and constraints from training or reference data.

Then it compares production inference data against that baseline.

When the production distribution violates the expected constraints, Model Monitor can surface the drift and trigger alerts.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Production monitoring: Monitor deployed model inputs over time.

    Input distribution tracking: Compare production feature statistics with the training baseline.

    Data drift detection: Identify when production data no longer reflects training data.

    Baseline comparison: Use reference statistics and constraints.

    Silent degradation detection: Catch performance risks even when the endpoint is still running.

    AWS-native monitoring: Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.

    Operational separation: Do not confuse data drift with infrastructure latency, code bugs, or concept drift.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Production input data distribution changes compared with training data = data drift.

Remember:

    Data drift: Change in the distribution of input features, or P(X).

    Concept drift: Change in the relationship between inputs and target, or P(Y|X).

    Model quality drift: Decline in prediction performance against ground-truth labels.

    Infrastructure failure: Endpoint latency, errors, or unavailability.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: Detects data quality, model quality, bias, and feature attribution drift.

    Baseline statistics: Reference profile used to compare production data.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Useful for endpoint metrics and alerts, not for defining data drift itself.

Official AWS references.

    Monitor models for data and model quality, bias, and feature attribution drift.

    Monitor data quality with Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.

Key exam terms: data drift, covariate shift, Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, data quality monitor, concept drift, P(X) distribution shift, baseline statistics, production vs. training distribution.

Question 38Skipped

A machine learning engineer at MetroTransit Analytics, a public transportation authority, is responsible for maintaining production ML models deployed on Amazon SageMaker.


The team is implementing a comprehensive monitoring strategy
using Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.


During planning, a junior engineer asks what data drift means and why detecting it is important for production model monitoring.


Which statement most accurately describes data drift in machine learning model monitoring, and why is it important to detect?
Pipeline steps cannot be shared between projects; each step must be manually recreated and configured within every new pipeline definition.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines explicitly supports step reuse across different pipelines and projects. Asserting that steps must be manually recreated for every pipeline directly misrepresents a core MLOps feature of SageMaker Pipelines designed to reduce engineering overhead. This distractor conflates the managed orchestration capabilities of SageMaker Pipelines with ad hoc, script-based approaches that lack native reusability primitives. Keyword tip: whenever a distractor claims a well-documented SageMaker feature is entirely unsupported, verify against official AWS documentation — such absolute negation language is a strong signal of an incorrect option.
SageMaker Pipelines supports reuse limited to parameter values only; the step definitions and their associated processing logic must be redefined in each pipeline.
Explanation
Incorrect. While SageMaker Pipelines does support pipeline parameterization through constructs such as ParameterString and ParameterInteger, the reuse capability extends well beyond parameter values alone. Entire step definitions — including ProcessingStep configurations, container images, processing scripts, and instance settings — can be encapsulated and reused across pipelines without redefinition. This option materially understates the actual reuse capability of the service. Keyword tip: 'parameters only' distractors in SageMaker Pipelines questions consistently understate the full feature set; reusability in SageMaker Pipelines applies at the step-definition level, not merely at the parameter level.
Each pipeline must be fully redefined for every new model, including all preprocessing steps, because SageMaker Pipelines does not support cross-pipeline step references.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines does not require a complete redefinition for each new model or use case. The service is specifically architected for MLOps reusability, enabling teams to compose new pipelines from previously defined and validated steps rather than rebuilding from scratch. This distractor incorrectly characterizes SageMaker Pipelines as having the same limitations as unmanaged, imperative scripting workflows. Keyword tip: 'must be fully redefined' language in the context of a managed AWS pipeline service almost always signals an incorrect distractor, particularly when the scenario explicitly involves reuse and reduced development overhead.
Correct answer
Steps in a SageMaker pipeline can be reused across different projects and pipelines, allowing common tasks such as data preprocessing to be replicated efficiently without manual duplication.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Pipelines natively supports the reuse of pipeline steps — such as ProcessingStep for data preprocessing — across different models, use cases, and projects. A step defined and validated in one pipeline can be referenced and incorporated into a new pipeline definition, eliminating redundant development effort and reducing the risk of inconsistencies across workflows. This modular, composable design is a fundamental MLOps best practice that SageMaker Pipelines enables natively, making it the preferred orchestration tool for iterative and scalable ML workflows on AWS. Keyword tip: 'step reuse across projects' combined with 'avoid duplicating pipeline logic' are the distinguishing signals that point to SageMaker Pipelines' cross-pipeline step reusability feature.
Overall explanation

Question 39Skipped

A data engineering team at OrbitShield Defense Systems, an aerospace and defense contractor, needs to set up an automated ETL pipeline using AWS Glue.


The team has semi-structured sensor telemetry and mission log files stored in Amazon S3. The data must be extracted, transformed, and loaded into

Amazon Redshift for further analysis.


The team also requires the data schema to be cataloged automatically and made queryable through SQL without manual intervention.


How should the team implement this solution most efficiently using AWS Glue?
Manually create the schema in Amazon Redshift before loading the data to avoid unnecessary processing by AWS Glue crawlers, then run AWS Glue ETL jobs to load the data into Redshift.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually defining the schema in Amazon Redshift prior to loading defeats the purpose of AWS Glue's automated cataloging capability. AWS Glue crawlers are specifically designed to infer schemas from semi-structured data sources such as JSON, Parquet, or CSV files stored in Amazon S3, eliminating manual schema management and reducing operational overhead. This approach introduces toil and is an anti-pattern for teams that require automatic schema discovery with no manual intervention. Keyword tip: whenever a question contrasts 'manual schema creation in Redshift' against AWS Glue crawlers, treat the manual approach as an anti-pattern for fully automated ETL pipelines.
Correct answer
Use AWS Glue crawlers to automatically infer the schema from the semi-structured data in Amazon S3 and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Then, run AWS Glue ETL jobs to extract, transform, and load the data into Amazon Redshift.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue crawlers automatically connect to Amazon S3, classify the data format, infer the schema, and persist the resulting metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. This catalog entry enables SQL-based querying through Amazon Athena or Amazon Redshift Spectrum and serves as the authoritative metadata repository for subsequent AWS Glue ETL jobs. The ETL jobs then use the cataloged schema to efficiently transform and load the data into Amazon Redshift, satisfying both the automation and SQL queryability requirements simultaneously. This crawler-catalog-ETL job triad is the AWS-recommended, fully automated, and scalable pattern for semi-structured data ingestion. Keyword tip: 'automatic schema inference + AWS Glue Data Catalog + ETL job to Redshift' is the canonical AWS Glue architecture whenever a scenario requires no manual intervention and SQL queryability.
Configure AWS Lambda functions to trigger AWS Glue ETL jobs on a schedule, bypassing schema inference and automatic cataloging to reduce orchestration complexity and runtime costs.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Lambda can legitimately invoke AWS Glue jobs via the AWS SDK or Amazon EventBridge, deliberately bypassing schema inference and the AWS Glue Data Catalog eliminates the SQL queryability requirement stated in the scenario. The Data Catalog is a necessary component for exposing data to SQL-based services such as Amazon Athena and Amazon Redshift Spectrum. Using Lambda solely for job invocation without the cataloging step satisfies the scheduling need but fails the 'queryable via SQL without manual intervention' constraint. Keyword tip: Lambda as a Glue job trigger is valid orchestration, but omitting the AWS Glue Data Catalog disqualifies any option when the scenario explicitly requires automated schema cataloging and SQL queryability.
Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to extract data directly from Amazon S3, apply pre-built transformations, and load it into Amazon Redshift, relying on Redshift's COPY command for schema detection instead of the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although AWS Glue ETL jobs can load data into Amazon Redshift and Redshift's COPY command offers limited schema inference for certain structured formats, this approach bypasses the AWS Glue Data Catalog entirely. Without registering the schema in the Data Catalog, the data cannot be queried via SQL using Amazon Athena or Amazon Redshift Spectrum, which is a key requirement of the scenario. Furthermore, Redshift's COPY command does not provide the centralized, cross-service metadata management that the AWS Glue Data Catalog delivers across the broader AWS analytics ecosystem. Keyword tip: skipping the AWS Glue Data Catalog means losing centralized schema management and cross-service SQL queryability — always a disqualifying trait when those requirements are explicitly stated.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the end-to-end AWS Glue ETL architecture.

It focuses on using AWS Glue crawlers for automatic schema inference, the AWS Glue Data Catalog for metadata management, and AWS Glue ETL jobs to transform and load data from Amazon S3 into Amazon Redshift.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Manually create the schema in Amazon Redshift before loading the data to avoid unnecessary processing by AWS Glue crawlers, then run AWS Glue ETL jobs to load the data into Redshift: This option is incorrect. Manual schema creation violates the requirement for automatic schema discovery and cataloging. AWS Glue crawlers are designed to infer schemas from data in Amazon S3.

    Use AWS Glue crawlers to automatically infer the schema from the semi-structured data in Amazon S3 and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Then, run AWS Glue ETL jobs to extract, transform, and load the data into Amazon Redshift: This is the correct option. AWS Glue crawlers infer the schema, the AWS Glue Data Catalog stores the metadata, and AWS Glue ETL jobs transform and load the data into Amazon Redshift.

    Configure AWS Lambda functions to trigger AWS Glue ETL jobs on a schedule, bypassing schema inference and automatic cataloging to reduce orchestration complexity and runtime costs: This option is incorrect. AWS Lambda can trigger jobs, but bypassing schema inference and the Data Catalog fails the requirement for automatic cataloging and SQL queryability.

    Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to extract data directly from Amazon S3, apply pre-built transformations, and load it into Amazon Redshift, relying on Redshift's COPY command for schema detection instead of the AWS Glue Data Catalog: This option is incorrect. Amazon Redshift COPY can load data, but it does not replace the centralized schema inference and metadata management provided by the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

Why AWS Glue crawlers, Data Catalog, and ETL jobs fit this scenario.

The data is stored in Amazon S3.

The files are semi-structured.

The team needs automatic schema detection.

The schema must be cataloged without manual intervention.

The data must also be transformed and loaded into Amazon Redshift.

That points to the standard AWS Glue architecture.

First, AWS Glue crawlers scan the data in Amazon S3.

They classify the data format, infer the schema, detect partitions, and create or update table metadata.

Second, the inferred metadata is stored in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

The Data Catalog becomes the central metadata repository for analytics and ETL services.

Third, AWS Glue ETL jobs use the cataloged schema to extract, transform, and load the data into Amazon Redshift.

This creates an automated pipeline for preparing semi-structured data for downstream analytics and ML workflows.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Semi-structured data in S3: Use AWS Glue crawlers.

    Automatic schema inference: Detect schema without manual intervention.

    Metadata cataloging: Store schema in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    SQL queryability: Make cataloged data available to services such as Amazon Athena or Amazon Redshift Spectrum.

    ETL processing: Use AWS Glue ETL jobs.

    Data warehouse loading: Load transformed data into Amazon Redshift.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid manual schema creation and custom cataloging logic.

    AWS Glue crawler: Scans data sources and infers schema.

    AWS Glue Data Catalog: Central metadata repository.

    AWS Glue ETL job: Transforms and loads data.

    Amazon S3: Common source for raw and semi-structured data.

    Amazon Redshift: Data warehouse target for analytical workloads.

    Amazon Athena: Can query cataloged S3 data using SQL.

    Manual schema creation: Incorrect when automatic schema discovery is required.

Question 40Skipped

An aerospace and defense contractor, OrbitShield Systems, is piloting a program to transcribe recorded conversations between flight safety officers and ground personnel into text for downstream compliance analysis.


The transcribed content must be stored in Amazon S3.

The solution must automatically detect and redact personally identifiable information to satisfy industry privacy regulations.


Which combination of AWS services best achieves these requirements in a fully automated and compliance-ready manner?
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, store the transcriptions in Amazon DynamoDB, and use AWS Glue to detect and redact PII from the data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon DynamoDB is a key-value and document database optimized for structured, low-latency lookups — it is not suitable for storing large unstructured text files, and the stated requirement explicitly mandates Amazon S3 as the storage destination. Furthermore, AWS Glue is an ETL service designed for batch data transformation workloads, not for event-driven PII detection and redaction from transcription output. Neither DynamoDB nor AWS Glue fulfills the automated, compliance-ready PII redaction requirement described in the scenario. Keyword tip: when DynamoDB is proposed for large unstructured text storage alongside AWS Glue for PII redaction, both represent service mismatches for speech-to-text compliance pipelines targeting Amazon S3.
Correct answer
Use Amazon Transcribe Medical to convert speech to text, utilize AWS Lambda to trigger Amazon Comprehend Medical for PII detection and redaction, and store the processed text in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Transcribe Medical is purpose-built for clinical and highly specialized speech-to-text use cases, offering acoustic models trained on domain-specific terminology that produce higher accuracy for professional technical conversations involving sensitive subject matter. AWS Lambda provides a serverless, event-driven trigger that can invoke Amazon Comprehend Medical immediately after a transcription job completes, enabling end-to-end automation without manual intervention. Amazon Comprehend Medical is specifically designed to detect and de-identify Protected Health Information (PHI) and PII from specialized text, making it the appropriate service for compliance-sensitive redaction workflows. Storing the processed output in Amazon S3 directly satisfies the stated storage requirement. Keyword tip: when a scenario involves domain-specific recorded conversations with strict PII compliance requirements and an Amazon S3 storage mandate, the preferred automated pattern is Transcribe Medical → Lambda → Comprehend Medical → S3.
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, enable the built-in PII redaction feature in Amazon Transcribe, store the redacted transcriptions directly in Amazon S3, and use Amazon Macie to enforce ongoing data classification policies.
Explanation
Incorrect. Standard Amazon Transcribe does offer a built-in PII redaction feature for a defined set of entity types, but it does not apply specialized domain-specific language models and is therefore less accurate for technical or domain-inflected conversations compared to Amazon Transcribe Medical. While incorporating Amazon Macie for data classification is a defensible security posture, Macie is a data security service that identifies sensitive data by scanning Amazon S3 objects using machine learning — it does not perform active, transcription-level PII redaction and cannot replace Amazon Comprehend Medical in an automated compliance pipeline. Keyword tip: standard Transcribe PII redaction combined with Amazon Macie is a plausible but suboptimal distractor when the scenario explicitly demands domain-specific accuracy and active automated PII removal rather than passive data classification.
Use Amazon Transcribe Medical to convert speech to text, store the transcriptions in Amazon S3, and enable Amazon Macie to scan and redact PII from the stored transcriptions.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Transcribe Medical correctly addresses the domain-specific speech-to-text accuracy requirement, Amazon Macie is a data security and classification service that identifies sensitive data patterns within Amazon S3 objects — it does not provide active redaction capabilities for PII or PHI entities at the document level. Macie's function is discovery and alerting, not transformation or de-identification of stored text, and it cannot replace Amazon Comprehend Medical, which is the purpose-built AWS service for detecting and de-identifying medical and domain-specific PII. Keyword tip: Macie detects and classifies but does not redact; whenever active PII removal from text content is required in an automated pipeline, Amazon Comprehend or Amazon Comprehend Medical is the correct service choice, not Amazon Macie.
Overall explanation

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

Amazon Macie detects sensitive data in S3, but it is primarily a discovery and classification service, not the main redaction engine in this pipeline.

    Use Amazon Transcribe Medical to convert speech to text, store the transcriptions in Amazon S3, and enable Amazon Macie to scan and redact PII from the stored transcriptions: This option is incorrect. Amazon Macie can discover and classify sensitive data in Amazon S3, but it does not perform active document-level redaction or de-identification in the same way as Amazon Comprehend Medical.

Why Transcribe Medical, Lambda, Comprehend Medical, and S3 fit this scenario.

The organization needs to transcribe recorded conversations.

The content contains sensitive information that must be detected and redacted automatically.

The final processed text must be stored in Amazon S3.

For a medical or healthcare-specific transcription workflow, Amazon Transcribe Medical is the right speech-to-text service.

It is designed for clinical and healthcare speech transcription use cases.

Amazon Comprehend Medical can then support de-identification workflows by identifying sensitive medical information that should be removed or masked.

This creates a managed, event-driven pipeline with minimal custom infrastructure.

Question 41Skipped

A regional banking institution, MeridianBank Corp, is working on a text classification problem to automatically categorize incoming customer support tickets.


The team decides to use Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms and needs to optimize model performance on a custom labeled dataset.


The team does not want to manually tune hyperparameters and wants to use native SageMaker capabilities that are directly compatible with built-in algorithm training jobs.


Which approach best addresses this requirement?
Manually adjust hyperparameters for the built-in algorithm through iterative trial-and-error training jobs.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually adjusting hyperparameters through trial and error is highly inefficient and does not scale to complex hyperparameter spaces. This approach is susceptible to human bias, fails to systematically explore the search space, and does not leverage any of SageMaker's managed tuning capabilities. On MLA-C01, any option that replaces an automated, managed SageMaker feature with a manual iterative process should be treated as architecturally suboptimal. Keyword tip: When a scenario explicitly requires avoiding manual tuning and leveraging native SageMaker capabilities, reject any option that relies on human-driven iterative trial and error.
Implement a custom training container with a grid search routine to evaluate hyperparameter combinations independently of SageMaker's managed infrastructure.
Explanation
Incorrect. Implementing a custom training container with a self-managed grid search routine is technically feasible but operationally expensive and architecturally misaligned with the stated requirement to use SageMaker's built-in algorithms and native capabilities. Grid search exhaustively evaluates a fixed hyperparameter grid and is significantly less efficient than the Bayesian optimization strategy used by SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning, which learns from prior evaluations to focus on promising regions of the search space. This option also introduces unnecessary infrastructure management overhead that contradicts the intent to use managed, native SageMaker capabilities. Keyword tip: Custom containers with self-managed grid search are a signal for over-engineering when built-in SageMaker AMT integration already covers the requirement.
Use SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a pre-trained text generation foundation model and rely on its zero-shot classification capability without additional tuning.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker JumpStart pre-trained foundation models for text generation are designed for generative tasks such as summarization or open-ended generation, not for supervised text classification on a custom labeled dataset. Relying on a generative model's zero-shot capability instead of training a purpose-built classifier on labeled data would yield suboptimal and unpredictable performance for structured, multi-class classification tasks like ticket categorization. This option conflates generative AI use cases with discriminative supervised classification tasks. Keyword tip: When the scenario involves a custom labeled dataset and a classification objective — not generation — reject zero-shot foundation model options in favor of supervised training with a purpose-built algorithm.
Correct answer
Use SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning (hyperparameter optimization) with the built-in BlazingText algorithm configured for text classification.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning (AMT), also known as hyperparameter optimization (HPO), integrates natively with SageMaker's built-in algorithms, including BlazingText, which supports supervised text classification in its text_classification mode. AMT uses Bayesian optimization by default to efficiently explore the hyperparameter search space, learning from completed training jobs to prioritize configurations most likely to maximize the specified objective metric, thereby eliminating the need for manual tuning. This combination — a purpose-built built-in algorithm paired with native AMT — is the canonical MLOps pattern for automated hyperparameter optimization within SageMaker's managed infrastructure. Keyword tip: When a question specifies built-in algorithms, a custom labeled dataset, and automated performance optimization without manual intervention, SageMaker AMT paired with BlazingText for text classification is the expected answer.
Overall explanation

Question 42Skipped

A machine learning engineer at AeroDyne Systems, an aerospace and defense contractor, is building a model to predict rare component failure events from aircraft subsystem sensor data.


Most observations in the dataset represent normal operating conditions, resulting in a severe class imbalance.


The engineer plans to use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to preprocess the data before training. The solution must balance the classes effectively while minimizing the risk of overfitting and retaining as much original data information as possible.


Which technique available in SageMaker Data Wrangler best addresses this class imbalance under these constraints?
Apply feature scaling to normalize the feature values across the entire dataset prior to model training.
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature scaling (e.g., min-max normalization or standardization) adjusts the range or distribution of individual feature values to improve gradient-based convergence during training, but it has no effect on the class distribution within the dataset. Normalizing sensor readings from aircraft subsystems does not alter how many samples belong to each class, so the failure-event minority class remains severely underrepresented after scaling. Keyword tip: When a question identifies class imbalance as the core problem, any technique that only transforms feature magnitudes without altering sample counts or class proportions is immediately disqualified as a solution.
Apply random undersampling to reduce the number of samples in the majority class until both classes are balanced.
Explanation
Incorrect. Random undersampling restores class balance by discarding majority-class samples until both classes are equal in size; however, this strategy is inherently lossy. In an aerospace sensor dataset, the majority-class observations capture the full diversity of normal operating patterns across flight phases and environmental conditions — information that is critical for the model to generalize correctly. Discarding this data risks degrading overall model performance and violates the explicit constraint of retaining as much original data information as possible. Keyword tip: When a question explicitly requires preserving original data, undersampling strategies are suboptimal and should be eliminated in favor of oversampling approaches such as SMOTE.
Correct answer
Apply the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) to generate new synthetic samples for the minority class.
Explanation
Correct. SMOTE is a well-established, non-lossy oversampling technique that generates synthetic minority-class samples by interpolating between existing minority-class feature vectors in the feature space, rather than simply duplicating them. SageMaker Data Wrangler natively supports SMOTE as a built-in transformation for handling class imbalance. This approach increases minority-class representation while introducing controlled diversity into the new synthetic samples, which reduces the risk of overfitting compared to naive duplication, and it preserves all original majority-class data, satisfying the constraint of retaining as much original information as possible. Keyword tip: In MLA-C01 scenarios that combine SageMaker Data Wrangler, severe class imbalance, overfitting risk, and a data-preservation requirement, SMOTE is the canonical recommended technique.
Apply simple oversampling by duplicating existing samples from the minority class to match the majority class size.
Explanation
Incorrect. Simple random oversampling duplicates existing minority-class records verbatim until the desired class ratio is achieved. While this approach restores class balance without discarding any majority-class data, it introduces exact replicas of the same minority-class data points into the training set, which causes the model to memorize specific failure-event observations and significantly increases the risk of overfitting — the exact constraint the question prohibits. SMOTE addresses this fundamental drawback by generating interpolated synthetic samples rather than identical copies. Keyword tip: When overfitting risk is explicitly stated as a constraint, simple duplication-based oversampling is a strong distractor but is always inferior to SMOTE because it adds no new information to the minority class.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of class imbalance handling in Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

It focuses on selecting SMOTE when the dataset has a severe minority class problem and the solution must balance classes while reducing overfitting risk and preserving original data.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Apply feature scaling to normalize the feature values across the entire dataset prior to model training: This option is incorrect. Feature scaling changes the numerical range of feature values, but it does not change the class distribution. The minority failure class would still remain underrepresented.

    Apply random undersampling to reduce the number of samples in the majority class until both classes are balanced: This option is not the best answer. Undersampling can balance the dataset, but it removes majority-class records and may discard valuable information about normal operating behavior.

    Apply the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) to generate new synthetic samples for the minority class: This is the correct option. SMOTE generates synthetic minority-class examples instead of duplicating existing records, helping balance the dataset while reducing the risk of overfitting.

    Apply simple oversampling by duplicating existing samples from the minority class to match the majority class size: This option is incorrect. Simple oversampling duplicates minority-class records exactly, which can cause the model to memorize repeated examples and overfit.

Why SMOTE fits this scenario.

The dataset contains rare component failure events.

Most observations represent normal operating conditions.

That means the dataset is severely imbalanced.

The engineer needs to balance the classes before training.

However, the solution must also minimize overfitting and preserve as much original information as possible.

That points to SMOTE.

SMOTE creates synthetic minority-class samples by interpolating between existing minority-class examples in feature space.

It does not simply duplicate existing failure records.

This adds more diversity to the minority class and reduces the chance that the model memorizes a small number of repeated failure cases.

SMOTE also preserves the majority-class data.

That makes it better than undersampling when the normal operating data contains valuable information about aircraft subsystem behavior.

Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler supports SMOTE as a built-in transform for handling imbalanced datasets.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Severe class imbalance: Increase minority-class representation.

    Rare failure events: Generate more examples for the failure class.

    Overfitting prevention: Avoid exact duplication of minority samples.

    Data preservation: Keep original majority-class observations.

    Synthetic oversampling: Create new minority-class samples through interpolation.

    Managed preprocessing: Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Training readiness: Prepare a more balanced dataset for downstream classification.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Class imbalance + preserve data + avoid duplicate-driven overfitting = SMOTE.

For this question, the correct answer is:

Apply the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) to generate new synthetic samples for the minority class.

Remember:

    SMOTE: Creates synthetic minority-class samples.

    Simple oversampling: Duplicates existing minority samples and can increase overfitting.

    Undersampling: Removes majority-class data and can lose useful information.

    Feature scaling: Normalizes feature values but does not fix class imbalance.

    Minority class: The underrepresented class, such as rare failure events.

    Class imbalance: Common in fraud, failure prediction, safety, and anomaly detection.

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: Provides built-in preprocessing transforms, including imbalance-handling options.

Official AWS references.
