Question 43Skipped

A construction and engineering firm, BuildCore Analytics, is training a large image classification model on Amazon SageMaker using PyTorch to automate defect detection in structural inspection photos.


The training process is taking significantly longer than expected. The machine learning engineer suspects that a resource bottleneck is reducing training throughput.


The engineer decides to use SageMaker Profiler to diagnose and resolve the issue.


Which actions should the engineer take to identify and resolve the bottleneck? Select TWO.
Correct selection
Use SageMaker Profiler to monitor GPU utilization in real-time and investigate periods of underutilization to optimize GPU usage.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Profiler natively captures GPU utilization metrics in real-time, enabling engineers to identify whether GPU compute resources are underutilized — a common sign that data loading or preprocessing is the true bottleneck rather than GPU capacity. Addressing GPU underutilization (e.g., by increasing batch size, using prefetching, or enabling mixed-precision training) directly resolves training throughput issues in large-scale image classification workloads such as BuildCore Analytics's defect detection model. Keyword tip: When 'GPU underutilization' appears alongside 'SageMaker Profiler' in a training performance scenario, it signals a compute-bound bottleneck diagnosis use case rather than a model correctness or hyperparameter issue.
Configure custom CloudWatch metrics to track memory allocation during training, and reduce the number of epochs if memory usage exceeds the instance threshold.
Explanation
Incorrect. While monitoring memory allocation is a valid operational concern, adjusting the number of training epochs is a hyperparameter change that controls model convergence, not a remediation for memory resource bottlenecks. High memory usage should be addressed by adjusting batch size, applying gradient checkpointing, or selecting a larger instance type — not by reducing epochs, which would degrade model quality without resolving the underlying resource constraint. Keyword tip: When a question pairs 'epoch count reduction' with memory management, recognize that epoch count governs training convergence behavior, not memory consumption or infrastructure throughput.
Correct selection
Use SageMaker Profiler to monitor disk I/O activity and optimize data access patterns — such as switching to Amazon FSx for Lustre or using pipe mode — if slow disk reads are detected as the bottleneck.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Profiler can capture disk I/O metrics that reveal slow data read operations, which is a frequent cause of GPU starvation and extended training times in large-scale image classification workloads. Once slow disk reads are identified as the bottleneck, the engineer can take corrective actions such as migrating the dataset to Amazon FSx for Lustre for high-throughput parallel access, enabling SageMaker pipe mode to stream data directly from Amazon S3, or implementing prefetching in the PyTorch DataLoader. Keyword tip: When 'disk I/O bottleneck' is confirmed via SageMaker Profiler in a training job, 'pipe mode' and 'Amazon FSx for Lustre' are the canonical AWS remediation options for data ingestion optimization.
Dynamically adjust the learning rate schedule during training based on real-time SageMaker Profiler metrics to accelerate convergence and reduce overall training time.
Explanation
Incorrect. Dynamically adjusting the learning rate based on SageMaker Profiler metrics is not a recognized pattern for resolving resource bottlenecks such as GPU underutilization or slow disk I/O. Learning rate schedules are a model optimization technique that affects convergence behavior, not infrastructure throughput. SageMaker Profiler is specifically designed to surface compute, memory, network, and I/O resource utilization — its output is not intended to drive hyperparameter tuning decisions during an active training run. Keyword tip: When a distractor conflates 'SageMaker Profiler metrics' with 'learning rate adjustment,' recognize that Profiler addresses infrastructure efficiency, while learning rate is a model training hyperparameter concern.
Enable SageMaker Debugger rules to automatically halt the training job whenever a NaN loss value is detected, then restart with a lower initial learning rate.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Debugger rules for detecting NaN loss values are valuable for training stability, but they address numerical instability in the model's loss function — a model correctness concern — rather than infrastructure resource bottlenecks that cause training to run slowly. Halting a job on NaN detection and restarting with a lower learning rate does not diagnose or resolve GPU underutilization or slow disk I/O, which are the likely causes of unexpectedly long training times in BuildCore Analytics's scenario. Keyword tip: 'NaN loss detection' paired with 'SageMaker Debugger' indicates numerical stability monitoring; contrast this with SageMaker Profiler, which targets compute and I/O throughput bottlenecks.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Profiler as a training observability tool for infrastructure-level bottlenecks.

It focuses on using Profiler to diagnose GPU underutilization and disk I/O bottlenecks, then applying the correct data pipeline or storage optimization.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use SageMaker Profiler to monitor GPU utilization in real time and investigate periods of underutilization to optimize GPU usage: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Profiler can capture GPU utilization metrics during training and help identify whether GPUs are idle or underused.

    Configure custom CloudWatch metrics to track memory allocation during training, and reduce the number of epochs if memory usage exceeds the instance threshold: This option is incorrect. Reducing epochs affects model convergence, not memory utilization or training throughput. Memory pressure should be addressed through batch size changes, model optimization, or instance selection.

    Use SageMaker Profiler to monitor disk I/O activity and optimize data access patterns, such as switching to Amazon FSx for Lustre or using pipe mode, if slow disk reads are detected as the bottleneck: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Profiler can expose disk I/O bottlenecks, and optimizing data access can reduce GPU starvation and improve training speed.

    Dynamically adjust the learning rate schedule during training based on real-time SageMaker Profiler metrics to accelerate convergence and reduce overall training time: This option is incorrect. Learning rate is a model training hyperparameter. Amazon SageMaker Profiler is used for system-level resource diagnostics, not direct learning rate optimization.

    Enable SageMaker Debugger rules to automatically halt the training job whenever a NaN loss value is detected, then restart with a lower initial learning rate: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Debugger is useful for training stability and tensor-level issues, but NaN detection does not diagnose GPU underutilization or disk I/O bottlenecks.

Why GPU utilization monitoring and disk I/O profiling fit this scenario.

The training job is taking longer than expected.

The model is a large image classification model.

The engineer suspects a resource bottleneck affecting throughput.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Profiler.

Amazon SageMaker Profiler captures infrastructure and system-level metrics during training.

These include GPU utilization, CPU usage, memory usage, disk I/O, and network throughput.

If GPU utilization is low, the GPU may be waiting for data.

That often means the bottleneck is in data loading, preprocessing, storage throughput, or batch construction.

The engineer can then optimize the data pipeline by improving prefetching, increasing data loader workers, tuning batch size, or reducing preprocessing latency.

If disk I/O metrics show slow reads or saturated throughput, the training job may be I/O-bound.

In that case, the engineer can optimize the storage path.

Examples include using Amazon FSx for Lustre for high-throughput training data access, enabling SageMaker pipe mode where appropriate, caching data, or improving the data layout.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Training performance diagnosis: Use Amazon SageMaker Profiler.

    GPU utilization monitoring: Detect whether accelerators are underused.

    Disk I/O monitoring: Identify slow reads or storage bottlenecks.

    Data pipeline optimization: Improve data loading and preprocessing throughput.

    Storage optimization: Use options such as Amazon FSx for Lustre or pipe mode when I/O is the bottleneck.

    Correct tool selection: Use Profiler for infrastructure bottlenecks, not Debugger.

    Avoid hyperparameter confusion: Do not treat learning rate or epoch count as infrastructure remediations.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Slow SageMaker training + suspected resource bottleneck + GPU or disk I/O metrics = Amazon SageMaker Profiler.

For this question, the correct selected options are:

Monitor GPU utilization with Amazon SageMaker Profiler + monitor disk I/O and optimize data access patterns.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Profiler: System-level profiling for training jobs.

    GPU underutilization: Often means the data pipeline is starving the accelerator.

    Disk I/O bottleneck: Indicates slow data loading or storage throughput issues.

    Amazon FSx for Lustre: High-throughput file system commonly used for ML training datasets.

    Pipe mode: Streams data from Amazon S3 to the training container where supported.

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger: Tensor-level model debugging, such as NaN loss or vanishing gradients.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Metrics and alarms, but not the primary profiler for training bottleneck root cause analysis.

Official AWS references.

    Profile computational performance with Amazon SageMaker Profiler.

    Use Amazon FSx for Lustre with Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Profiler, GPU utilization, disk I/O bottleneck, pipe mode, Amazon FSx for Lustre, Amazon SageMaker Debugger, training throughput, resource bottleneck diagnosis.

Question 44Skipped

A global pharmaceutical research organization, PharmaVox Research, collects patient and clinician feedback in multiple languages through email and web forms.


The organization wants to automatically detect sentiment in the feedback, categorize each submission into relevant therapeutic service categories, and translate all submissions into English for further analysis by the clinical operations team.


The solution must be scalable, automated end to end, and require minimal infrastructure management.


Which combination of AWS services would best meet these requirements?
Amazon Rekognition for analyzing the text within images, Amazon Comprehend for categorizing feedback, and Amazon Translate for language translation.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Rekognition is designed exclusively for image and video analysis tasks such as object detection, facial recognition, and content moderation — it does not perform natural language processing on free-form text. Introducing a computer vision service into a text-based feedback pipeline represents a fundamental architectural mismatch. Keyword tip: when an option pairs a computer vision service (Rekognition) with a text-processing task (NLP, sentiment, categorization), it is always a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Amazon Comprehend for both sentiment analysis and categorization, Amazon Translate for translation, and Amazon Kendra for providing intelligent search capabilities across the feedback corpus.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kendra provides powerful ML-based intelligent search and document retrieval capabilities, but the stated requirements are sentiment analysis, text categorization, and translation — none of which require a search engine layer. Adding Amazon Kendra introduces unnecessary complexity and cost without fulfilling any of the core requirements. Keyword tip: distinguish 'intelligent search / query a knowledge base' (Kendra) from 'sentiment analysis / text classification' (Comprehend) when evaluating NLP pipeline options on the MLA-C01 exam.
Amazon Translate for translating feedback into English, Amazon Comprehend for sentiment analysis and entity recognition, and Amazon SageMaker for custom classification of feedback into service categories.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Amazon SageMaker can be used to build and deploy a custom text classification model, Amazon Comprehend already provides fully managed, built-in multi-class and multi-label text classification that requires no model training infrastructure or custom code. Selecting SageMaker here adds unnecessary operational overhead, model training costs, and pipeline complexity when a purpose-built managed service already covers the use case. Keyword tip: on the MLA-C01 exam, prefer managed AWS AI services (Comprehend, Translate) over custom SageMaker solutions whenever the NLP task — sentiment, entity recognition, custom classification — is already addressed by a purpose-built AWS AI service.
Correct answer
Amazon Translate for translating feedback into English, Amazon Comprehend for sentiment analysis and entity recognition, and AWS Lambda for automating and orchestrating the end-to-end process.
Explanation
Correct. This combination precisely satisfies all stated requirements with the least operational overhead. Amazon Translate handles multilingual-to-English translation in a fully managed, scalable manner across a broad range of language pairs. Amazon Comprehend provides built-in sentiment analysis (detecting positive, negative, neutral, or mixed sentiment) and entity recognition — which can surface therapeutic category signals — without requiring custom model training. AWS Lambda orchestrates the invocation of both services in a serverless, event-driven pipeline, enabling automatic and scalable processing of incoming feedback from emails and web forms with no infrastructure management. Keyword tip: when requirements specify automation, scalability, and minimal infrastructure for a standard NLP workflow, look for solutions that pair AWS AI services (Translate, Comprehend) with serverless orchestration (Lambda) as the end-to-end pipeline pattern.
Overall explanation

Question 45Skipped

A data scientist at GreenHarvest Cooperative is running multiple machine learning experiments using Amazon SageMaker to find the best hyperparameter configurations for a crop yield prediction model.


The team needs to track each experiment’s metadata and compare results across runs, including input parameters, output metrics, and artifacts.


The experimentation process must be fully reproducible and organized using native SageMaker capabilities, without requiring additional custom tooling.


Which SageMaker feature would best meet these requirements?
Correct answer
SageMaker Experiments
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Experiments is purpose-built to track, organize, and compare machine learning experiments within Amazon SageMaker. It records metadata such as hyperparameters, metrics, artifacts, and data sources for each trial, and structures them into experiments and trial components, making runs fully reproducible and directly comparable without requiring any custom tooling outside of SageMaker. Keyword tip: When a question specifies 'track experiments', 'compare hyperparameter configurations', 'trial components', and 'reproducible runs' within SageMaker, SageMaker Experiments is the designated service.
SageMaker Autopilot
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Autopilot automates the end-to-end model training and tuning process by exploring multiple algorithm and hyperparameter combinations automatically; however, it is an AutoML service that generates and manages its own candidate pipelines rather than exposing a structured, analyst-controlled framework for tracking, comparing, and reproducing custom experiments. It does not allow the data scientist to define and instrument individual trial components with custom metadata. Keyword tip: Scenario clues such as 'automatic model selection', 'AutoML', or 'no-code training' point to SageMaker Autopilot, which is distinct from fine-grained experiment tracking.
SageMaker Pipelines
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines provides a CI/CD-style orchestration layer for automating and versioning end-to-end ML workflows, including data processing, training, evaluation, and deployment steps. While Pipelines can integrate with SageMaker Experiments to log run metadata, Pipelines itself does not offer the fine-grained experiment-level tracking, trial organization, and hyperparameter-configuration comparison that SageMaker Experiments provides natively. Keyword tip: Phrases such as 'end-to-end workflow automation', 'CI/CD for ML', or 'directed acyclic graph of pipeline steps' are signals for SageMaker Pipelines, not experiment tracking.
SageMaker Clarify
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Clarify is designed to detect bias in datasets and models and to provide feature attribution reports using SHAP values. It addresses fairness and interpretability concerns during model evaluation, not the structured tracking, comparison, or reproducibility of multiple training runs and their hyperparameter configurations. Keyword tip: Keywords such as 'bias detection', 'feature importance', 'SHAP values', or 'model explainability' identify SageMaker Clarify as the relevant service, which is orthogonal to experiment management.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Experiments for structured experiment tracking and reproducibility.

It focuses on selecting Experiments when the team needs to track metadata, compare training runs, organize hyperparameter configurations, and reproduce ML experiments within SageMaker.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    SageMaker Experiments: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracks, organizes, and compares ML runs. It records metadata such as hyperparameters, metrics, artifacts, data sources, and trial components.

    SageMaker Autopilot: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot is an AutoML service that automates model building and candidate generation. It is not the primary service for custom experiment tracking and reproducibility across multiple manual or structured runs.

    SageMaker Pipelines: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines orchestrates ML workflows such as preprocessing, training, evaluation, and deployment. It can integrate with experiment tracking, but it is not the main service for organizing and comparing experiment runs.

    SageMaker Clarify: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is used for bias detection and explainability, including SHAP-based feature attribution. It does not manage experiment metadata or compare hyperparameter configurations across runs.

Why SageMaker Experiments fits this scenario.

The data scientist is running multiple ML experiments.

Each run may use different input parameters, hyperparameters, datasets, metrics, and artifacts.

The team needs to compare results across runs.

They also need the experimentation process to be reproducible.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

Amazon SageMaker Experiments provides structured organization for ML experimentation.

An experiment can group related trials.

Each trial can represent a training run or model configuration.

Trial components capture details about individual steps, such as preprocessing, training, or evaluation.

Trackers can log parameters, metrics, artifacts, and metadata.

This allows teams to compare runs systematically and understand which configuration produced which result.

It also supports reproducibility because the key inputs, outputs, and run details are captured inside SageMaker instead of being tracked manually.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Reproducibility: Preserve enough context to recreate or audit results.

    SageMaker-native workflow: Avoid external spreadsheets or custom tracking tools.

Question 46Skipped

A data science team at PixelForge Studios, an online gaming company,
is organizing machine learning experiments using:
Amazon SageMaker Experiments.


A new team member wants to understand the organizational hierarchy used by the service.


Which statement correctly describes the relationship between Trials and Trial Components in SageMaker Experiments?
A trial component tracks the hardware resource utilization during training, while a trial is used to compare the performance of different algorithms across the same dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. Trial components do not track hardware resource utilization; they capture the discrete stages and associated metadata of an ML workflow, such as input parameters, metrics, and artifacts. Hardware and infrastructure metrics are tracked separately via Amazon CloudWatch, which is a distinct service from SageMaker Experiments. The role of a trial component is to record a specific workflow stage — for example, a preprocessing or evaluation step — not system-level resource consumption. Keyword tip: When an option attributes hardware or resource utilization tracking to SageMaker Experiments trial components, treat it as a distractor and look for the option describing workflow stages with associated metrics and artifacts.
A trial captures different models trained in parallel within the same experiment, and trial components represent the different datasets consumed by each model.
Explanation
Incorrect. A trial does not represent multiple models trained in parallel; it represents a single, specific end-to-end training run with a defined configuration. Trial components are not designed to represent individual datasets consumed by models — they represent discrete workflow stages such as preprocessing, training, and evaluation within a single trial. Comparisons across parallel model training runs are organized by creating multiple trials within the same experiment. Keyword tip: When an option conflates a trial with parallel model training or misdefines trial components as dataset containers, it is inverting the purpose of the SageMaker Experiments hierarchy.
A trial consists of multiple experiments grouped by objective, and each experiment captures metadata about a specific data processing stage.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option inverts the hierarchy of SageMaker Experiments. An experiment is the top-level container that holds multiple trials — trials do not consist of experiments. Additionally, experiments do not capture metadata about specific data processing stages; that is the role of trial components. Recognizing this reversed experiment-trial relationship is a common distractor pattern in MLA-C01 questions on SageMaker Experiments. Keyword tip: Any option that places experiments below trials or inside trials should immediately be identified as an inverted hierarchy distractor for SageMaker Experiments questions.
Correct answer
A trial represents a single end-to-end training run with a specific configuration, and trial components capture the individual stages within that trial, such as data preprocessing, model training, and model evaluation.
Explanation
Correct. In SageMaker Experiments, a trial represents a single run of an ML pipeline with a specific set of hyperparameters and configurations. Each trial is composed of one or more trial components, which capture the individual stages of that run — such as a data preprocessing step, a model training step, and a model evaluation step — along with their associated metrics, parameters, and output artifacts. This three-level hierarchical structure (Experiment → Trial → Trial Component) is the foundational concept tested in MLA-C01 for this service and enables systematic, reproducible comparison of configurations across runs. Keyword tip: The phrase 'single end-to-end training run with a specific configuration' paired with 'individual stages' is the canonical signal for the correct trial-to-trial-component relationship in SageMaker Experiments.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the hierarchy used by Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

It focuses on understanding the relationship between Experiments, Trials, and Trial Components when tracking and comparing ML runs.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    A trial component tracks the hardware resource utilization during training, while a trial is used to compare the performance of different algorithms across the same dataset: This option is incorrect. Trial components do not track hardware utilization. Hardware and system metrics are better associated with services such as Amazon CloudWatch or Amazon SageMaker Profiler.

    A trial captures different models trained in parallel within the same experiment, and trial components represent the different datasets consumed by each model: This option is incorrect. A trial does not represent multiple models trained in parallel. A trial represents one specific run or configuration, and trial components represent stages within that run.

    A trial consists of multiple experiments grouped by objective, and each experiment captures metadata about a specific data processing stage: This option is incorrect. The hierarchy is reversed. An Experiment is the top-level container, and it groups multiple Trials. Trials do not contain Experiments.

    A trial represents a single end-to-end training run with a specific configuration, and trial components capture the individual stages within that trial, such as data preprocessing, model training, and model evaluation: This is the correct option. In Amazon SageMaker Experiments, a Trial represents a run or candidate configuration, while Trial Components capture the individual stages and metadata inside that run.

Why the Trial and Trial Component relationship fits this scenario.

The team needs to understand how Amazon SageMaker Experiments organizes ML experimentation.

The correct hierarchy is:

Experiment → Trial → Trial Component.

An Experiment is the top-level container.

It groups related ML work, such as all runs for a player churn model, fraud model, or recommendation model.

A Trial represents one specific end-to-end run or configuration inside an Experiment.

For example, one Trial may use a specific algorithm, feature set, hyperparameter configuration, and training dataset version.

A Trial Component captures an individual stage of that Trial.

Examples include data preprocessing, model training, model evaluation, or batch transform.

Trial Components can store metadata such as parameters, metrics, input artifacts, output artifacts, and other run details.

This structure allows teams to compare multiple runs systematically and reproduce the configuration that produced a specific result.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Experiment tracking: Use Amazon SageMaker Experiments.

    Top-level grouping: Use Experiments to group related Trials.

    Single run representation: Use Trials for specific training runs or configurations.

    Workflow stage tracking: Use Trial Components for preprocessing, training, and evaluation stages.

    Metadata capture: Track parameters, metrics, inputs, outputs, and artifacts.

    Run comparison: Compare Trials inside the same Experiment.

    Reproducibility: Preserve enough run metadata to understand and recreate results.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Experiment groups Trials, and each Trial contains Trial Components for workflow stages.

Remember:

    Experiment: Top-level group for related ML work.

    Trial: One end-to-end run or candidate configuration.

    Trial Component: A stage inside a Trial, such as preprocessing, training, or evaluation.

    Metrics: Values logged during a Trial Component.

    Parameters: Configuration values such as hyperparameters or input settings.

    Artifacts: Outputs such as processed data, models, or evaluation reports.

    Amazon SageMaker Experiments: Used for experiment tracking, comparison, and reproducibility.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Experiments concepts.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Experiments, Trial, Trial Component, experiment hierarchy, Experiment → Trial → Trial Component, workflow stages, ML pipeline run, metadata tracking.

Question 47Skipped

An ML security engineer at AtmosData Research Institute, a weather and climate research agency, is reviewing AWS IAM policy configurations.


The engineer needs to verify that access controls are properly enforced for Amazon SageMaker endpoints, Amazon S3 data buckets, and AWS Glue Data Catalog resources.


As part of the audit, the engineer must distinguish between identity-based policies and resource-based policies to determine which access control mechanism applies to each resource type.


What is the primary difference between identity-based policies and resource-based policies in AWS IAM?
Correct answer
Identity-based policies are attached to IAM identities such as users, groups, or roles and define what actions those identities are permitted to perform, whereas resource-based policies are directly attached to AWS resources such as Amazon S3 buckets or AWS KMS keys and define which principals are allowed to access those resources.
Explanation
Correct. Identity-based policies are attached to IAM principals — users, groups, or roles — and specify the actions and resources those principals can access, without requiring an explicit Principal element since the principal is implied by the attachment. Resource-based policies, by contrast, are attached directly to AWS resources (e.g., S3 bucket policies, AWS KMS key policies, Amazon SQS queue policies) and include a Principal element specifying which accounts, users, or roles may access the resource. This distinction is critical in MLOps security audits, especially when configuring cross-account access to SageMaker training data or climate model artifacts stored in Amazon S3 at AtmosData Research Institute. Keyword tip: When a question distinguishes IAM policy attachment points, 'identity-based → attached to principal (no Principal element); resource-based → attached to resource (explicit Principal element)' is the definitive signal for the correct answer.
Identity-based policies must always be AWS managed policies, whereas resource-based policies must always be customer-managed inline policies attached directly to the resource.
Explanation
Incorrect. There is no restriction requiring identity-based policies to be AWS managed or resource-based policies to be customer-managed inline policies. Both identity-based and resource-based policies can be implemented as AWS managed policies, customer-managed policies, or inline policies depending on the use case and governance requirements. Keyword tip: Distractor options that invent mandatory policy-type restrictions — such as forcing one category to always be managed or always inline — do not exist in IAM documentation and should be eliminated immediately.
Identity-based policies can only be applied to IAM groups, whereas resource-based policies can only be applied to individual IAM users, making cross-account access impossible with either type alone.
Explanation
Incorrect. Identity-based policies can be applied to IAM users, groups, and roles — not exclusively to groups. Furthermore, cross-account access is fully achievable using resource-based policies that include a Principal referencing an external account, or by combining identity-based policies with IAM role assumption via AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS). Keyword tip: Any option that incorrectly restricts IAM policy attachment to a single identity type, or that falsely claims cross-account access is impossible, is a strong indicator of a distractor regardless of the scenario context.
Identity-based policies are attached directly to AWS resources such as Amazon S3 buckets or AWS Lambda functions, whereas resource-based policies are attached to IAM users, groups, or roles to control which resources those identities can access.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the correct definitions entirely. Identity-based policies are attached to IAM identities (users, groups, roles), not to AWS resources. Resource-based policies are attached to AWS resources such as Amazon S3 buckets or AWS KMS keys, not to IAM principals. Swapping the attachment points of these two policy categories is a classic exam distractor technique. Keyword tip: If an option inverts the core attachment-point definitions of identity-based and resource-based policies — placing each on the wrong entity — it is definitionally incorrect regardless of how plausible the surrounding wording appears.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the difference between IAM identity-based policies and resource-based policies.

It focuses on understanding where each policy type is attached, how permissions are evaluated, and how resource-based policies can explicitly grant access to specific principals.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Identity-based policies are attached to IAM identities such as users, groups, or roles and define what actions those identities are permitted to perform, whereas resource-based policies are directly attached to AWS resources such as Amazon S3 buckets or AWS KMS keys and define which principals are allowed to access those resources: This is the correct option. Identity-based policies are attached to IAM principals, while resource-based policies are attached to resources and include a Principal element.

    Identity-based policies must always be AWS managed policies, whereas resource-based policies must always be customer-managed inline policies attached directly to the resource: This option is incorrect. Identity-based policies are not limited to AWS managed policies, and resource-based policies are not limited to inline customer-managed policies.

    Identity-based policies can only be applied to IAM groups, whereas resource-based policies can only be applied to individual IAM users, making cross-account access impossible with either type alone: This option is incorrect. Identity-based policies can be attached to users, groups, and roles. Resource-based policies can also support cross-account access by specifying external principals.

    Identity-based policies are attached directly to AWS resources such as Amazon S3 buckets or AWS Lambda functions, whereas resource-based policies are attached to IAM users, groups, or roles to control which resources those identities can access: This option is incorrect. It reverses the definitions. Identity-based policies attach to identities, and resource-based policies attach to resources.

Why the identity-based vs. resource-based policy distinction fits this scenario.

The security engineer is reviewing access controls across services such as Amazon SageMaker, Amazon S3, and AWS Glue.

They need to distinguish which policy type applies to identities and which applies to resources.

An identity-based policy is attached to an IAM identity.

That identity can be a user, group, or role.

The policy defines what actions the identity can perform and on which resources.

For example, a SageMaker execution role might have an identity-based policy that allows sagemaker:CreateTrainingJob, s3:GetObject, or glue:GetTable.

A resource-based policy is attached directly to a resource.

Examples include an Amazon S3 bucket policy, an AWS KMS key policy, or an Amazon SQS queue policy.

A resource-based policy includes a Principal element because it must explicitly define who is allowed to access the resource.

This is especially important for cross-account access, where a resource in one AWS account grants access to a role or account in another AWS account.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Identity permissions: Use identity-based policies on IAM users, groups, or roles.

    Resource permissions: Use resource-based policies on supported AWS resources.

    Principal element distinction: Resource-based policies include Principal; identity-based policies do not.

    Cross-account access: Use resource-based policies when a resource must grant access to external principals.

    Least privilege: Grant only the actions and resources required.

    MLOps security: Control access to training data, model artifacts, endpoints, and catalogs.

    Audit clarity: Understand whether permission is granted from the identity side or the resource side.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Policy attached to a user, group, or role = identity-based policy. Policy attached to a resource and includes Principal = resource-based policy.

Remember:

    Identity-based policy: Attached to an IAM identity.

    Resource-based policy: Attached to a supported AWS resource.

    Principal: Required in resource-based policies to specify who gets access.

    No Principal in identity-based policies: The identity is implied by where the policy is attached.

    Amazon S3 bucket policy: Common resource-based policy.

    AWS KMS key policy: Common resource-based policy.

    SageMaker execution role: Common identity that uses identity-based permissions.

Official AWS references.

    Identity-based policies and resource-based policies.

Key exam terms: identity-based policy, resource-based policy, IAM principal, Principal element, cross-account access, Amazon S3 bucket policy, AWS KMS key policy, SageMaker execution role.

Question 48Skipped

A media streaming company called StreamNova is building a generative AI application using Amazon Bedrock.


The company needs to quickly integrate a foundation model to generate personalized content recommendations and viewer engagement insights based on real-time user interaction data.


The team also wants the infrastructure to be scalable and secure without directly managing the underlying model hosting environment.


Which features of Amazon Bedrock make it the best choice for this use case? Select TWO.
On-premises infrastructure for enhanced security
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed AWS cloud service and does not support on-premises infrastructure for model deployment. Organizations requiring on-premises deployments would need to consider self-managed solutions or AWS Outposts, neither of which integrates natively with Bedrock's fully managed foundation model capabilities. Keyword tip: when a question specifies 'no direct infrastructure management' as a requirement alongside generative AI workloads, on-premises deployment options are always invalid for Amazon Bedrock.
Provisioned Throughput for dedicated model capacity with custom instance management
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Bedrock does offer Provisioned Throughput as a capacity reservation option, it is used to purchase dedicated model units for high-volume, latency-sensitive workloads — not as a mechanism for managing custom compute instances. Provisioned Throughput does not eliminate infrastructure management concerns in the way the scenario requires; it is a cost-optimization and throughput-guarantee feature, not a primary scalability or security differentiator for a new integration. Keyword tip: when the requirement emphasizes 'quick integration with automatic scalability and no direct infrastructure management,' Provisioned Throughput is a secondary operational consideration rather than a core Amazon Bedrock value proposition.
Direct access to GPUs for model training and fine-tuning
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Bedrock abstracts all underlying hardware, including GPUs, from the customer. Users do not have direct access to GPUs for model training or fine-tuning through Bedrock. Fine-tuning in Bedrock is performed through a fully managed, API-driven workflow that conceals all compute details from the user. Keyword tip: 'direct GPU access' is a characteristic of Amazon SageMaker training jobs or EC2-based setups — not of managed inference services like Amazon Bedrock.
Correct selection
Serverless environment with automatic scaling
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Bedrock provides a serverless environment that automatically scales based on workload demand, meaning StreamNova does not need to provision, manage, or scale any underlying compute infrastructure. The serverless model is particularly well-suited for applications with variable or unpredictable traffic patterns, such as real-time personalization and recommendation systems driven by live viewer interaction data. Keyword tip: 'serverless + automatic scaling + no infrastructure management' is the canonical value proposition of Amazon Bedrock for deployment scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam.
Correct selection
Data encryption in transit and at rest
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Bedrock enforces encryption of data both in transit (using TLS) and at rest (using AWS KMS-managed keys), ensuring that sensitive viewer interaction data processed through the foundation model API is protected without requiring the customer to implement custom encryption logic. This directly satisfies the 'secure without managing it directly' requirement in the scenario. Keyword tip: whenever a question pairs 'managed generative AI service' with 'security without direct management,' built-in encryption in transit and at rest is a key differentiating security feature of Amazon Bedrock.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of core Amazon Bedrock value propositions.

It focuses on selecting Bedrock features that provide a fully managed foundation model experience: serverless scaling with no infrastructure management and built-in encryption for data protection.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    On-premises infrastructure for enhanced security: This option is incorrect. Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed AWS cloud service. It does not provide on-premises model deployment as a native Bedrock capability.

    Provisioned Throughput for dedicated model capacity with custom instance management: This option is incorrect. Provisioned Throughput is a valid Bedrock capacity option for dedicated throughput, but it does not provide custom instance management. It is not the core feature being tested when the scenario asks for no infrastructure management and automatic scalability.

    Direct access to GPUs for model training and fine-tuning: This option is incorrect. Amazon Bedrock abstracts away the underlying infrastructure. Customers do not directly manage GPUs or training instances through Bedrock.

    Serverless environment with automatic scaling: This is a correct option. Amazon Bedrock provides serverless access to foundation models, so teams can build generative AI applications without provisioning or scaling infrastructure.

    Data encryption in transit and at rest: This is a correct option. Amazon Bedrock supports encryption for data in transit and at rest, helping protect sensitive application data without requiring custom encryption infrastructure.

Why serverless scaling and encryption fit this scenario.

The company wants to integrate foundation models into an application quickly.

The team does not want to manage infrastructure.

The workload may have variable demand.

The application also processes sensitive viewer interaction data.

That points to Amazon Bedrock.

Amazon Bedrock provides access to foundation models through a fully managed API.

The team does not provision GPU instances, manage serving clusters, patch servers, or scale inference infrastructure manually.

The service handles the managed foundation model access layer.

This is why a serverless environment with automatic scaling is a core Bedrock benefit.

Security is also part of the requirement.

Amazon Bedrock protects data in transit and at rest.

Encryption in transit protects data as it moves between the application and the Bedrock service endpoint.

Encryption at rest protects stored service data using AWS-managed encryption capabilities, including AWS KMS integration where supported.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    No infrastructure management: Use Amazon Bedrock.

    Serverless access: Avoid provisioning GPUs, servers, or inference clusters.

    Automatic scaling: Support changing traffic patterns.

    Foundation model integration: Use managed model APIs.

    Security by default: Use encryption in transit and at rest.

    Sensitive data protection: Protect viewer interaction data.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid custom hosting with Amazon EC2 or custom Amazon SageMaker endpoints.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Managed foundation models + no infrastructure management + automatic scaling + built-in encryption = Amazon Bedrock.

For this question, the correct selected options are:

Serverless environment with automatic scaling + data encryption in transit and at rest.

Remember:

    Amazon Bedrock: Fully managed service for building generative AI applications with foundation models.

    Serverless scaling: No need to provision or scale model hosting infrastructure.

    Encryption in transit: Protects data moving between client and service.

    Encryption at rest: Protects stored data using encryption controls.

    AWS KMS: Key management service used across AWS encryption workflows.

    Provisioned Throughput: Dedicated model capacity, not custom instance management.

    Direct GPU access: Associated with self-managed or SageMaker training environments, not Bedrock's managed API model.

Question 49Skipped

A data science team at MetroTransit Analytics, a public transportation authority, is building an end-to-end ML workflow using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.


The team wants to automate model training and evaluation tasks for predictive maintenance and ridership forecasting.


The workflow must be reusable across multiple projects, scale to support dozens of simultaneous model experiments, and automatically manage dependencies between processing, training, and evaluation steps without manual intervention.


Which key capability of Amazon SageMaker Pipelines directly addresses these requirements?
Correct answer
Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) representation with automatic step execution and scalability to tens of thousands of concurrent workflows.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines natively represents ML workflows as Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs), where each node is a pipeline step and edges encode data and execution dependencies. This design enables automatic, dependency-aware step execution without manual intervention and is architected to scale to tens of thousands of concurrent pipeline executions. The DAG model also makes pipelines parameterizable and reusable across projects and environments, directly satisfying all three requirements stated in the MetroTransit Analytics scenario. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires automated dependency management, reusability, and high-concurrency ML workflow orchestration within SageMaker, the DAG-based execution model of SageMaker Pipelines is the defining capability to identify.
Native integration with AWS Step Functions, which orchestrates each pipeline step as a separate state machine and requires manual state transitions to maintain control over execution order.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Step Functions can orchestrate ML workflows and integrates with SageMaker through its dedicated SageMaker service integration, it is a separate orchestration service — not an internal feature of SageMaker Pipelines itself. SageMaker Pipelines provides its own built-in DAG-based orchestration engine that is tightly coupled with SageMaker's training, processing, and model registry APIs, making it the more appropriate and self-contained choice for native SageMaker ML workflow automation. Additionally, SageMaker Pipelines does not require manual state transitions — step sequencing is fully automatic based on declared dependencies. Keyword tip: if a question asks specifically about SageMaker Pipelines' own capabilities, Step Functions-based orchestration is a distractor — it is a valid external alternative but is not an internal SageMaker Pipelines feature.
Pre-built, domain-specific pipeline templates accessible through SageMaker Studio that provide a low-code interface but are constrained to single-region, small-scale experimental workloads.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines does provide a visual DAG interface within SageMaker Studio, but it is neither limited to small-scale workloads nor constrained to a single region. Pipeline definitions are fully programmable via the SageMaker Python SDK and scale to enterprise production workloads spanning multiple regions. Characterizing the capability as 'low-code only' and 'limited in scale' fundamentally misrepresents the service's architecture and production readiness. Keyword tip: options that correctly identify a real UI feature of SageMaker Studio but incorrectly impose scalability or regional constraints are common distractors in MLA-C01 questions involving SageMaker Pipelines.
Event-driven execution via Amazon EventBridge rules, where each pipeline step is triggered independently by Amazon CloudWatch metric thresholds, enabling fine-grained control but requiring separate configuration per step.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon EventBridge can be used to trigger an entire SageMaker Pipeline execution based on scheduled or event-driven rules, but it does not orchestrate individual steps within a pipeline. Step-level dependency management and automatic execution sequencing are handled internally by SageMaker Pipelines' DAG engine — not by Amazon CloudWatch metric thresholds or EventBridge rules targeting individual steps. Configuring per-step EventBridge triggers would require substantial custom infrastructure and is not a supported or recommended orchestration pattern for SageMaker Pipelines. Keyword tip: EventBridge is relevant for pipeline-level invocation (the start trigger), not for intra-pipeline step orchestration — a nuance that is commonly tested in MLA-C01 orchestration scenarios.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the core orchestration model used by Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

It focuses on understanding that SageMaker Pipelines represents ML workflows as a Directed Acyclic Graph, or DAG, where dependencies between steps are automatically managed.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Directed Acyclic Graph representation with automatic step execution and scalability to tens of thousands of concurrent workflows: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines models workflows as DAGs, where each step runs only after its dependencies are satisfied.

    Native integration with AWS Step Functions, which orchestrates each pipeline step as a separate state machine and requires manual state transitions to maintain control over execution order: This option is incorrect. AWS Step Functions is a separate orchestration service. SageMaker Pipelines has its own internal DAG-based orchestration model and does not require manual state transitions.

    Pre-built, domain-specific pipeline templates accessible through SageMaker Studio that provide a low-code interface but are constrained to single-region, small-scale experimental workloads: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines can be defined programmatically and used for production-scale workflows. It is not limited to small-scale or low-code-only experimentation.

    Event-driven execution via Amazon EventBridge rules, where each pipeline step is triggered independently by Amazon CloudWatch metric thresholds, enabling fine-grained control but requiring separate configuration per step: This option is incorrect. Amazon EventBridge can trigger a pipeline execution, but it does not orchestrate each internal pipeline step. Step sequencing is managed by the SageMaker Pipelines DAG.

Why the DAG-based execution model fits this scenario.

The team needs an end-to-end ML workflow.

The workflow includes multiple dependent steps, such as preprocessing, training, evaluation, and model registration.

The workflow must be reusable across projects.

It must also scale to many simultaneous model experiments.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Amazon SageMaker Pipelines represents the workflow as a Directed Acyclic Graph.

Each node in the graph is a pipeline step.

Examples include ProcessingStep, TrainingStep, TransformStep, and model registration steps.

The edges between steps represent dependencies.

This means a downstream step does not run until its required upstream steps have completed successfully.

This dependency-aware execution model removes the need for manual coordination between steps.

It also makes the workflow reproducible, parameterizable, and reusable across different datasets, environments, or projects.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Dependency management: Use the DAG model in Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

    Automatic step execution: Run steps in the correct order without manual intervention.

    Reusable workflows: Parameterize and reuse pipelines across projects.

    Scalable experimentation: Support multiple concurrent model experiments.

    SageMaker-native orchestration: Integrate with SageMaker training, processing, evaluation, and registry workflows.

    Reduced operational overhead: Avoid custom per-step orchestration logic.

    MLOps consistency: Standardize repeatable ML workflows.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Reusable ML workflow + automatic dependency management + scalable concurrent executions = Amazon SageMaker Pipelines DAG-based orchestration.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Pipelines: Native ML workflow orchestration service.

    DAG: Directed Acyclic Graph used to represent step dependencies.

    Pipeline step: A unit of work such as preprocessing, training, evaluation, or registration.

    Automatic execution: Steps run when dependencies are satisfied.

    Pipeline parameters: Enable reuse across environments and executions.

    AWS Step Functions: General workflow orchestration service, not the internal engine of SageMaker Pipelines.

    Amazon EventBridge: Can trigger a pipeline execution, but does not manage internal pipeline step order.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Directed Acyclic Graph, DAG, automatic step execution, dependency management, concurrent pipeline executions, AWS Step Functions distractor, Amazon EventBridge invocation trigger, reusable ML workflow orchestration.

Question 50Skipped

An agricultural cooperative, HarvestLink Co-op, needs to train a deep neural network model with billions of parameters using Amazon SageMaker.


The model is too large to fit into the memory of a single GPU, and the training dataset is also very large.


The team must choose the most appropriate SageMaker distributed training technique to train the model efficiently under these constraints.


Which approach should the team use?
Correct answer
Apply model parallelism, where the model itself is partitioned across multiple GPUs, and each GPU handles a distinct portion of the model's layers or parameters.
Explanation
Correct. When a model's parameter count exceeds the memory capacity of a single GPU, model parallelism is the required technique. SageMaker's distributed training library supports model parallelism by partitioning the model's layers or tensor shards across multiple GPUs or instances, allowing each device to hold and compute only a fraction of the total model. This is the canonical solution for extremely large foundation models and deep networks with billions of parameters, and is configured via the distribution parameter in the SageMaker Estimator. Keyword tip: the phrase 'model too large to fit on a single GPU' is the primary signal to select model parallelism over data parallelism in Amazon SageMaker distributed training scenarios.
Apply data parallelism, where multiple copies of the full model are placed on separate GPUs, each processing a different shard of the dataset, with gradients synchronized across instances.
Explanation
Incorrect. Data parallelism replicates the entire model on each GPU and distributes different mini-batches of training data across devices, synchronizing gradients after each step. While effective for accelerating training when the dataset is large and the model fits comfortably in GPU memory, it does not address the core constraint here — the model itself cannot fit into a single GPU's memory. Attempting to deploy a full model replica on each GPU would immediately result in out-of-memory errors, making this approach unsuitable regardless of dataset size. Keyword tip: data parallelism requires the full model to reside on each device and does not reduce per-GPU model memory footprint — when model size is the bottleneck, model parallelism is required instead.
Use pre-built SageMaker training containers without enabling any distributed training strategy, relying solely on the container's default memory management to handle the large model.
Explanation
Incorrect. Pre-built SageMaker training containers provide a convenient starting point with optimized deep learning frameworks, but they do not automatically resolve GPU memory constraints caused by an oversized model. Without explicitly configuring and enabling SageMaker's model parallelism library via the distribution parameter in the Estimator, the training job will fail with out-of-memory errors or produce undefined behavior when the model exceeds available GPU memory. Relying on default container memory management is not a recognized or documented strategy for handling billion-parameter models. Keyword tip: default SageMaker containers without an explicit distributed training configuration cannot overcome hardware memory limits imposed by a model that exceeds single-GPU capacity.
Provision a single high-memory GPU instance (such as a p4d.24xlarge) with sufficient on-instance memory to load the entire model and dataset without distributing across multiple nodes.
Explanation
Incorrect. While large GPU instances such as the p4d.24xlarge offer significantly more aggregate GPU memory than standard instances, no single commercially available GPU instance can accommodate a model with billions of parameters in its entirety across its individual GPU devices. Furthermore, even if memory capacity were theoretically sufficient, relying on a single instance would represent a cost-prohibitive and operationally inefficient approach compared to distributed model parallelism across a managed SageMaker cluster. Keyword tip: for models at the billions-of-parameters scale, vertical scaling to a larger single instance is not a viable substitute for horizontal distributed model parallelism in Amazon SageMaker.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of distributed training strategies in Amazon SageMaker.

It focuses on selecting model parallelism when the model itself is too large to fit into the memory of a single GPU or accelerator.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Apply model parallelism, where the model itself is partitioned across multiple GPUs, and each GPU handles a distinct portion of the model's layers or parameters: This is the correct option. Model parallelism splits the model across multiple GPUs or instances, allowing each device to hold and compute only part of the model.

    Apply data parallelism, where multiple copies of the full model are placed on separate GPUs, each processing a different shard of the dataset, with gradients synchronized across instances: This option is incorrect. Data parallelism requires a full copy of the model on each GPU. It helps when the dataset is large, but it does not solve a model-size memory constraint.

    Use pre-built SageMaker training containers without enabling any distributed training strategy, relying solely on the container's default memory management to handle the large model: This option is incorrect. Pre-built containers do not automatically split an oversized model across GPUs. A distributed strategy must be explicitly configured.

    Provision a single high-memory GPU instance with sufficient on-instance memory to load the entire model and dataset without distributing across multiple nodes: This option is not the best answer. Vertical scaling can help in some cases, but when the model itself cannot fit on a single GPU, the correct strategy is to partition the model across devices.

Why model parallelism fits this scenario.

The model contains billions of parameters.

The model is too large to fit into the memory of a single GPU.

That means the bottleneck is model size, not only dataset size.

This points directly to model parallelism.

In model parallel training, the model is split across multiple GPUs or instances.

Each device stores and computes only a portion of the model, such as specific layers, tensor shards, or pipeline stages.

This reduces the per-device memory requirement and makes it possible to train models that are too large for one accelerator.

This is different from data parallelism.

In data parallelism, each GPU receives a full replica of the model and trains on a different shard of data.

That works only when the model can fit on every GPU.

For billion-parameter models that exceed single-GPU memory, data parallelism alone would fail with out-of-memory errors.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Model memory constraint: Use model parallelism.

    Billion-parameter model: Split the model across devices.

    Multi-GPU training: Partition layers or tensors across GPUs.

    Reduced per-device memory usage: Avoid placing the full model on one GPU.

    Distributed training: Use SageMaker distributed training capabilities.

    Correct bottleneck match: Use model parallelism for model-size problems.

    Avoid data parallel mismatch: Do not replicate an oversized model on every worker.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Model too large to fit on one GPU = model parallelism.

Remember:

    Model parallelism: Split the model across GPUs or instances.

    Data parallelism: Split the dataset and replicate the full model.

    GPU memory constraint: Strong signal for model parallelism.

    Large dataset: Signal for data parallelism only if the model fits on each worker.

    Amazon SageMaker distributed training: Supports distributed strategies for large-scale ML workloads.

    distribution parameter: Used in SageMaker estimators to configure distributed training.

    Out-of-memory error: Common symptom when trying to train an oversized model on one device.

Official AWS references.

    Model parallel distributed training with Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: model parallelism, data parallelism, Amazon SageMaker distributed training library, GPU memory constraint, billion-parameter model, distribution parameter, SageMaker Estimator, out-of-memory error.

Question 51Skipped

A public transportation authority, MetroGrid Transit Systems, is using Amazon SageMaker to train and deploy a reinforcement learning model that optimizes dynamic allocation of bus and rail resources across a metropolitan transit network.


The use case includes route scheduling, vehicle dispatch, and capacity balancing.


Which features of SageMaker RL would provide the most benefit for this use case?  Select TWO.
Training jobs can only run on remote managed instances and cannot be executed in local mode on notebook instances.
Explanation
Incorrect. This statement is factually wrong. Amazon SageMaker RL explicitly supports local mode execution on notebook instances, which is a valuable feature for iterative development and experimentation before scaling to managed cluster instances. Local mode allows engineers to validate environment logic and reward function behavior rapidly without incurring the overhead of launching remote training jobs. Keyword tip: Claims that restrict SageMaker RL capabilities to remote-only execution are a common distractor pattern — always verify whether a stated limitation actually exists in AWS documentation.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker supports pre-built RL toolkits such as Intel Coach and Ray RLlib, providing access to state-of-the-art reinforcement learning algorithms.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker provides pre-built RL toolkits, including Intel Coach (now OpenVINO-compatible) and Ray RLlib, which expose state-of-the-art algorithms such as Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Deep Q-Networks (DQN), and Actor-Critic methods. For a transit resource optimization use case, being able to leverage these production-grade algorithms without building them from scratch significantly reduces engineering effort and time to deployment. Keyword tip: 'Pre-built RL toolkits,' 'Ray RLlib,' and 'Intel Coach' are strong positive signals for SageMaker RL correctness on the MLA-C01 exam.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker allows monitoring and adjusting the training process through Python SDK integration, enabling real-time visibility into metrics and parameter changes.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker's Python SDK integration allows practitioners to programmatically initiate training jobs, query Amazon CloudWatch metrics, visualize reward curves and other custom RL metrics, and adjust hyperparameters between training runs — all from within a notebook or CI/CD pipeline. This is especially valuable for RL workflows involving complex operational environments such as transit networks, where iterative tuning of reward functions and dispatch policies is essential. Keyword tip: 'Python SDK monitoring,' 'CloudWatch metrics,' and 'hyperparameter adjustment' in the context of SageMaker RL are reliable indicators of a correct feature claim.
The RL model must always be trained and validated against real-world environments; simulated environments are not supported in SageMaker RL.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker RL fully supports simulated environments and in fact encourages their use. Simulation frameworks such as Amazon RoboMaker environments, OpenAI Gym, and custom simulators can be integrated with SageMaker RL to accelerate agent training before deploying policies to real operational systems — a critical capability when real-world rollout of an untested policy carries significant risk. Claiming that only real-world environments are supported is a fabricated limitation and a common exam distractor. Keyword tip: 'Simulated environments,' 'OpenAI Gym,' and 'Amazon RoboMaker' are all supported integrations in SageMaker RL — any option denying simulation support is incorrect.
SageMaker RL requires users to implement custom reward functions and environment wrappers from scratch, as no abstraction layers or pre-built environment interfaces are provided.
Explanation
Incorrect. This statement is false. SageMaker RL provides abstraction layers and integrations with toolkits like Ray RLlib and Intel Coach that include pre-built environment interfaces (e.g., OpenAI Gym-compatible wrappers) and utilities for defining reward functions. Users are not required to build everything from scratch, which is precisely one of the key value propositions of using SageMaker RL over a fully custom implementation. Keyword tip: Distractors that overstate implementation complexity by denying the existence of pre-built abstractions in SageMaker RL are generally incorrect on the MLA-C01 exam.

Question 52Skipped

A machine learning team at SiliconEdge Semiconductors needs to regularly transform and load large datasets from Amazon S3 into a data warehouse for model training.


The team is evaluating AWS Glue for the ETL process. The solution must be scalable, cost-efficient, and able to handle incremental updates without reprocessing the entire dataset on every run.


Which AWS Glue features should the team use to meet these requirements? Select TWO.
Correct selection
Configure incremental ETL jobs in AWS Glue so that only new or modified data is processed on each run.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue supports incremental data loading through job bookmarks, which persist state across job executions to track which data has already been processed. By enabling this feature, ETL jobs process only new or modified records on subsequent runs, significantly reducing both processing time and cost as the semiconductor fab's dataset grows continuously. This directly satisfies the requirement to avoid reprocessing the entire dataset on every run. Keyword tip: When a scenario pairs 'incremental updates' with 'cost-efficiency' for an S3-to-data-warehouse ETL pipeline, AWS Glue job bookmarks are the canonical solution to recognize.
Correct selection
Utilize AWS Glue's serverless architecture and auto-scaling capabilities to manage Apache Spark clusters automatically, ensuring the solution scales with workload demand.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue is a fully serverless ETL service that automatically provisions and manages the underlying Apache Spark infrastructure, dynamically scaling the number of DPUs based on workload size. This removes the operational burden of cluster management and ensures cost-efficiency by charging only for the compute resources actually consumed during each job run. The Glue Auto Scaling feature, available with Glue 3.0 and later, further refines this by releasing DPUs mid-job when they are no longer needed. Keyword tip: When a scenario requires a managed, scalable Spark-based ETL solution without manual cluster administration, AWS Glue's serverless DPU auto-scaling architecture is the defining capability to identify.
Pre-configure a fixed number of Glue Data Processing Units (DPUs) for all ETL jobs to eliminate the risk of unexpected scaling charges.
Explanation
Incorrect. Pre-configuring a fixed DPU allocation for every job defeats the core purpose of AWS Glue's auto-scaling model and leads to over-provisioning during lighter workloads. While setting a maximum DPU cap is a legitimate cost-governance practice, statically assigning the full DPU count at all times increases costs without improving reliability or scalability. The recommended approach is to rely on Glue Auto Scaling (available with Glue 3.0+), which dynamically adjusts DPU allocation up and down during job execution. Keyword tip: A fixed or static DPU configuration is a distractor whenever the question's requirements explicitly call for cost-efficiency and dynamic scalability in AWS Glue.
Use AWS Glue crawlers to infer the schema and classify incoming data in real-time so that all new files are processed immediately upon arrival.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue crawlers are metadata discovery tools that must be explicitly scheduled or manually triggered; they do not operate as a real-time event-driven ingestion mechanism. Crawlers populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog with schema information but do not directly trigger ETL job execution upon file arrival. For event-driven, near-real-time processing when files land in Amazon S3, a pattern using Amazon S3 event notifications combined with Amazon EventBridge or AWS Lambda would be required, not crawlers alone. Keyword tip: AWS Glue crawlers are associated with schema inference and Data Catalog population, not real-time file arrival triggers — distinguish crawlers from event-driven pipeline patterns on the exam.
Schedule AWS Glue ETL jobs at regular intervals and process the complete dataset on every execution to guarantee consistency across all training runs.
Explanation
Incorrect. Processing the entire dataset on every ETL run is neither cost-efficient nor scalable for the large volumes of sensor and fabrication data that SiliconEdge Semiconductors accumulates continuously. This approach negates the incremental loading capability provided by AWS Glue job bookmarks and increases both job duration and DPU costs proportionally with dataset growth. The requirement explicitly calls for avoiding full reprocessing on every run, making this option directly contradictory to the stated constraints. Keyword tip: Any option that describes processing the full dataset on every execution is a direct contradiction of an 'incremental updates' requirement and should be eliminated immediately.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS Glue capabilities for scalable and cost-efficient ETL pipelines.

It focuses on using AWS Glue job bookmarks for incremental processing and Glue's serverless auto-scaling architecture to avoid manual Spark cluster management.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Configure incremental ETL jobs in AWS Glue so that only new or modified data is processed on each run: This is a correct option. AWS Glue job bookmarks track previously processed data so subsequent job runs can process only new or changed records.

    Utilize AWS Glue's serverless architecture and auto-scaling capabilities to manage Apache Spark clusters automatically, ensuring the solution scales with workload demand: This is a correct option. AWS Glue is serverless and can automatically manage and scale the underlying Spark resources required for ETL jobs.

    Pre-configure a fixed number of Glue Data Processing Units for all ETL jobs to eliminate the risk of unexpected scaling charges: This option is not the best answer. Fixed DPU allocation may control capacity, but it can lead to over-provisioning and does not align with dynamic scalability and cost efficiency.

    Use AWS Glue crawlers to infer the schema and classify incoming data in real time so that all new files are processed immediately upon arrival: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue crawlers infer schema and update the AWS Glue Data Catalog, but they are not real-time file processing engines.

    Schedule AWS Glue ETL jobs at regular intervals and process the complete dataset on every execution to guarantee consistency across all training runs: This option is incorrect. Reprocessing the full dataset every time increases cost and runtime, directly violating the incremental processing requirement.

Why job bookmarks and serverless auto-scaling fit this scenario.

The data science team regularly transforms and loads large datasets from Amazon S3 into a data warehouse.

The dataset grows continuously.

The team needs the ETL process to be scalable and cost-efficient.

They also need to avoid reprocessing the entire dataset on every run.

That points to AWS Glue job bookmarks and Glue's managed serverless execution model.

AWS Glue job bookmarks maintain state between job runs.

They track which data has already been processed.

When the next ETL job runs, Glue can process only new or modified data instead of scanning and transforming the entire dataset again.

This reduces job duration, DPU usage, and cost.

AWS Glue also provides a serverless ETL environment.

The team does not need to provision, patch, or manage Spark clusters manually.

Glue provisions the required Apache Spark resources and can scale capacity based on the job workload.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Incremental updates: Use AWS Glue job bookmarks.

    Avoid full reprocessing: Process only new or changed data.

    Cost efficiency: Reduce unnecessary DPU consumption.

    Scalability: Use Glue's serverless Spark execution model.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid manual cluster management.

    S3-based ETL: Read source data from Amazon S3.

    Data warehouse loading: Prepare transformed data for downstream analytics and ML training.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Large S3 ETL pipeline + incremental updates + avoid reprocessing + scalable managed Spark = AWS Glue job bookmarks + serverless auto-scaling.

For this question, the correct selected options are:

Configure incremental ETL jobs using job bookmarks + use AWS Glue serverless auto-scaling.

Remember:

    AWS Glue job bookmarks: Track processed data across ETL job runs.

    Incremental ETL: Processes only new or changed records.

    DPU: Glue compute capacity unit used for ETL processing.

    Glue Auto Scaling: Adjusts resources based on workload demand.

    AWS Glue crawlers: Infer schema and update the Data Catalog, but do not process files in real time.

    Full dataset reprocessing: Costly and inefficient for growing datasets.

    Serverless ETL: No Spark cluster provisioning or maintenance required.

Official AWS references.

    Tracking processed data using job bookmarks.

    Using auto scaling for AWS Glue.

Key exam terms: AWS Glue job bookmarks, incremental ETL, serverless DPU auto-scaling, AWS Glue Data Catalog, Apache Spark cluster management, cost-efficient ETL, S3-to-data-warehouse pipeline, AWS Glue crawlers.
