Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML)
Question 53Skipped

A data engineering team at SolarVantage Energy, a renewable energy company, needs to analyze large datasets stored in an Amazon S3 data lake using SQL queries.

The data is currently stored in semi-structured JSON format. The team wants to improve query performance by converting the data into a columnar format.

The team also wants the schema to be automatically inferred and stored so that the data can be queried directly from Amazon S3 without loading it into a relational database.

Which steps should the team implement using AWS Glue and Amazon Athena? Select TWO.
Enable partitioning in Amazon Athena to automatically split large datasets by date, allowing Athena to create and manage the partition metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog without additional configuration.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although partitioning is a valid query optimization strategy in Amazon Athena, Athena does not automatically create or manage partition metadata. Partitions must be explicitly defined via DDL statements such as ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION, discovered by an AWS Glue Crawler, or registered using the MSCK REPAIR TABLE command. The phrase 'automatically' without any additional configuration is the key signal that makes this option technically inaccurate. Keyword tip: when an option states that 'Athena automatically creates partitions without additional configuration,' treat it as a distractor — partition management in Athena always requires an explicit mechanism.
Correct selection
Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to convert the JSON data into Apache Parquet format and store the transformed files back in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue ETL jobs are specifically designed to transform semi-structured data such as JSON into optimized columnar formats like Apache Parquet. Storing Parquet files in Amazon S3 significantly reduces the volume of data scanned by Amazon Athena, directly lowering both query latency and per-query cost. This Glue ETL → Parquet → S3 pattern is the recommended MLOps approach for data lake optimization on AWS. Keyword tip: 'AWS Glue ETL + Apache Parquet + Amazon S3' is the canonical answer for columnar format conversion in the MLA-C01 data preparation domain.
Correct selection
Set up an AWS Glue Crawler to automatically infer the schema from the JSON files and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue Crawlers can automatically connect to Amazon S3, sample the JSON data, infer the schema, and register the resulting table metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Once registered, the table is immediately available for querying via Amazon Athena without loading the data into a relational database. This fulfills both requirements stated in the scenario: automated schema inference and serverless SQL access directly against S3. Keyword tip: 'AWS Glue Crawler + AWS Glue Data Catalog + schema inference' is the standard pattern for enabling serverless SQL queries against S3 data lakes in MLA-C01 scenarios.
Use an Amazon Athena CTAS (CREATE TABLE AS SELECT) query to convert the JSON data to Apache ORC format and register the resulting table directly in Amazon Redshift Spectrum for downstream consumption.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Athena does support CTAS queries that can write data in Apache ORC format to Amazon S3, this option incorrectly describes registering the result directly in Amazon Redshift Spectrum, which is a distinct service for querying S3 data from within Amazon Redshift clusters. The scenario explicitly requires querying data directly from S3 without loading it into a relational database, and introducing Redshift Spectrum into the pipeline violates that constraint while adding unnecessary complexity and cost. Keyword tip: when a distractor combines multiple services — such as Athena CTAS and Redshift Spectrum — in a way that contradicts an explicit constraint in the scenario ('without loading into a relational database'), it is typically incorrect.
Manually define an Amazon Athena table schema for each JSON dataset and register it in the AWS Glue Data Catalog by running individual DDL statements through the Athena query editor.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually authoring DDL statements for every JSON dataset is operationally inefficient, error-prone, and does not scale for large or continuously evolving data lakes such as those typical in renewable energy telemetry pipelines. AWS Glue Crawlers fully automate schema discovery and catalog registration, which is the recommended and scalable approach endorsed in the AWS Well-Architected ML Lens. Manual schema management is only appropriate when granular control over table definitions is required and automated discovery is insufficient. Keyword tip: 'manual DDL schema definition' versus 'AWS Glue Crawler automation' — MLA-C01 consistently favors automated, managed AWS-native solutions over manual operational approaches.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of building a queryable and performant data lake on Amazon S3 using AWS Glue and Amazon Athena.

It focuses on using an AWS Glue Crawler to infer schema and update the AWS Glue Data Catalog, and using AWS Glue ETL jobs to convert JSON data into Apache Parquet for better query performance.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Enable partitioning in Amazon Athena to automatically split large datasets by date, allowing Athena to create and manage the partition metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog without additional configuration: This option is incorrect. Amazon Athena can query partitioned data, but it does not automatically create and manage partitions without an explicit mechanism such as an AWS Glue Crawler, MSCK REPAIR TABLE, or ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION.

    Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to convert the JSON data into Apache Parquet format and store the transformed files back in Amazon S3: This is a correct option. AWS Glue ETL jobs can convert semi-structured JSON into Apache Parquet, a columnar format that improves query performance and reduces the amount of data scanned by Amazon Athena.

    Set up an AWS Glue Crawler to automatically infer the schema from the JSON files and store the metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog: This is a correct option. AWS Glue Crawlers can scan data in Amazon S3, infer schema, and register metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    Use an Amazon Athena CTAS query to convert the JSON data to Apache ORC format and register the resulting table directly in Amazon Redshift Spectrum for downstream consumption: This option is incorrect. Amazon Redshift Spectrum is used from within Amazon Redshift, which adds unnecessary complexity and conflicts with the requirement to query data directly from Amazon S3 without loading it into a relational database.

    Manually define an Amazon Athena table schema for each JSON dataset and register it in the AWS Glue Data Catalog by running individual DDL statements through the Athena query editor: This option is not the best answer. Manual DDL can work, but it is operationally inefficient and does not satisfy the requirement for automatic schema inference.

Why AWS Glue ETL to Parquet and AWS Glue Crawlers fit this scenario.

The source data is stored in Amazon S3.

The format is semi-structured JSON.

The team wants to query the data using SQL without loading it into a relational database.

They also want to improve query performance.

That points to AWS Glue Crawlers, the AWS Glue Data Catalog, AWS Glue ETL, Apache Parquet, and Amazon Athena.

First, an AWS Glue Crawler scans the JSON files in Amazon S3.

The crawler infers the schema and creates or updates table metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

Amazon Athena can then use that catalog metadata to query the S3 data directly with SQL.

Second, AWS Glue ETL jobs can transform the raw JSON into Apache Parquet.

Parquet is a columnar file format.

For analytical queries, columnar formats allow query engines such as Amazon Athena to read only the required columns instead of scanning every field in every JSON record.

This reduces scanned data volume, lowers query cost, and improves query latency.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Automatic schema inference: Use an AWS Glue Crawler.

    Central metadata catalog: Store schema in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    Serverless SQL query: Query S3 data with Amazon Athena.

    Semi-structured JSON processing: Use AWS Glue ETL.

    Columnar optimization: Convert JSON to Apache Parquet.

    Lower query cost: Reduce the amount of data scanned.

    No relational database loading: Keep data queryable directly from Amazon S3.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

JSON data in Amazon S3 + automatic schema inference + Athena SQL + better query performance = AWS Glue Crawler + AWS Glue Data Catalog + AWS Glue ETL to Parquet.

For this question, the correct selected options are:

Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to convert JSON to Apache Parquet + set up an AWS Glue Crawler to infer schema and update the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

Remember:

    AWS Glue Crawler: Infers schema and updates catalog metadata.

    AWS Glue Data Catalog: Central metadata repository used by Athena and Glue.

    AWS Glue ETL: Transforms data between formats.

    Apache Parquet: Columnar format optimized for analytics.

    Amazon Athena: Serverless SQL over data in Amazon S3.

    Partition management: Requires explicit configuration or discovery.

    Manual DDL: Possible, but less scalable than crawlers for evolving datasets.

Official AWS references.

    Defining crawlers in AWS Glue.

    Using Apache Parquet files with AWS Glue.

    Query AWS Glue Data Catalog tables with Amazon Athena.

Key exam terms: AWS Glue Crawler, AWS Glue Data Catalog,

AWS Glue ETL, Apache Parquet, columnar format, schema inference, Amazon Athena, serverless S3 query.

Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML)
Question 54Skipped

A data scientist at CrestHarvest Foods, a food and beverage producer, needs to build a predictive model using Amazon SageMaker.


The team wants to avoid the complexity of managing infrastructure. They also want to minimize setup time, avoid writing custom training code, and focus only on data preparation and hyperparameter configuration.


Which SageMaker feature is most appropriate for this use case?
Use SageMaker Ground Truth to automatically handle infrastructure setup and dataset labeling, then feed the labeled data directly into a managed training environment.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Ground Truth is a data labeling service that uses human annotators or automated labeling workflows to generate ground-truth labels for training datasets. It does not handle model training infrastructure setup, algorithm selection, or training job execution. Keyword tip: When a question mentions 'data labeling' or 'annotation workflows,' consider Ground Truth — but it is not a substitute for a managed training solution.
Use SageMaker Autopilot to automatically explore multiple ML pipelines, select the best algorithm, and tune hyperparameters without any manual intervention or infrastructure management.
Explanation
Incorrect. While SageMaker Autopilot does eliminate the need to write custom training code and fully manages infrastructure, it goes beyond the stated requirement by automatically selecting algorithms and tuning hyperparameters without team input. The scenario specifically states that the team wants to provide data input and configure hyperparameters themselves, which aligns more precisely with using built-in algorithms. Autopilot is the right answer when the goal is full end-to-end automation of the ML pipeline with minimal human decision-making. Keyword tip: Distinguish 'minimize setup while retaining hyperparameter control' (built-in algorithms) from 'fully automated end-to-end ML with no manual algorithm selection' (Autopilot).
Build a custom container image with proprietary algorithms, push it to Amazon ECR, and use SageMaker Training Jobs to manage execution while the team manually provisions the cluster configuration.
Explanation
Incorrect. Building a custom container image with proprietary algorithms and manually configuring the training cluster introduces significant infrastructure management overhead, which directly contradicts the team's stated goal of minimizing setup complexity. Although SageMaker Training Jobs abstract some infrastructure details, maintaining a custom container image and managing cluster configuration still requires substantial engineering effort. Keyword tip: 'Custom container + manual cluster configuration' signals high operational complexity — the opposite of a low-friction, no-custom-code onboarding path in SageMaker.
Correct answer
Use SageMaker's built-in algorithms, which come pre-packaged in AWS-managed containers, requiring only data input and hyperparameter configuration to launch a training job.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker built-in algorithms are pre-packaged in AWS-managed Docker containers optimized for performance and scalability. The team only needs to point to the training data in Amazon S3 and configure hyperparameters; AWS handles all underlying infrastructure provisioning, scaling, and container management. This is the recommended entry path for teams that want to train models quickly without writing custom training code or managing servers. Keyword tip: 'Pre-packaged AWS-managed containers + S3 data input + hyperparameter configuration + zero infrastructure management' is the defining characteristic of SageMaker built-in algorithms.
Overall explanation

Question 55Skipped

A food and beverage producer, NourishCraft Foods, needs to train a machine learning model using a custom algorithm that is not supported by SageMaker built-in algorithms.


The organization wants to use SageMaker pre-built framework containers to avoid managing the underlying runtime environment, while still providing its own training and inference logic.


The solution must integrate with SageMaker managed training infrastructure and support straightforward deployment to a SageMaker endpoint.

Which approach should the organization take?
Write the custom training code directly in a SageMaker notebook instance and run it using the notebook's execution kernel, relying on the notebook environment for all compute resources.
Explanation
Incorrect. Running custom training code directly on a SageMaker notebook instance uses only the local compute resources of the notebook kernel and does not invoke SageMaker's managed training infrastructure. This approach lacks distributed training support, automatic instance provisioning, built-in checkpointing integration, and the ability to deploy models to managed SageMaker endpoints without additional manual steps. Any scenario that requires managed scaling, spot training, or seamless endpoint deployment disqualifies notebook-local execution as a viable solution. Keyword tip: When a question emphasizes SageMaker managed training jobs, instance provisioning, or endpoint deployment, notebook-local execution is always an incorrect option.
Correct answer
Utilize SageMaker Script Mode, write the custom training logic in a Python script, and specify the script as the entry point in a SageMaker estimator configured with a pre-built framework container.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Script Mode allows practitioners to supply a custom Python script as the entry point for a SageMaker estimator while using AWS-managed, pre-built framework containers for TensorFlow, PyTorch, MXNet, scikit-learn, and others. The framework runtime, CUDA libraries, and distributed training hooks are already present in the managed container, so the team only needs to maintain the training logic itself. The estimator invokes a fully managed SageMaker training job, enabling instance selection, automatic scaling, spot training, and seamless model artifact storage in Amazon S3 for subsequent deployment to a SageMaker endpoint. Keyword tip: The combination of 'pre-built framework container + custom training logic + entry_point parameter in a SageMaker estimator' is the canonical signal for SageMaker Script Mode on the MLA-C01 exam.
Extend one of SageMaker's built-in algorithm containers by injecting additional training code through environment variables and a custom entry-point override defined in the CreateTrainingJob API call.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker's built-in algorithm containers are opaque, optimized images that do not expose a supported mechanism for injecting arbitrary custom training code through environment variables or entry-point overrides via the standard CreateTrainingJob API. Modifying their internal training logic is not a supported pattern. This distractor is plausible because SageMaker does allow hyperparameter-level customization of built-in algorithms, but that is fundamentally different from replacing an algorithm's core training code with entirely custom logic. Keyword tip: Watch for options that conflate hyperparameter tuning or channel configuration of built-in algorithms with custom code injection — these are distinct concepts on the MLA-C01 exam.
Build a fully custom Docker container from scratch, package all framework dependencies alongside the training code, push the image to Amazon ECR, and reference the ECR URI directly in a SageMaker estimator.
Explanation
Incorrect. Building a fully custom Docker container from scratch is a valid SageMaker approach known as Bring Your Own Container (BYOC) and offers maximum flexibility, but it directly contradicts the stated requirement that NourishCraft Foods prefers to use SageMaker's pre-built framework containers to avoid container management overhead. BYOC requires the team to install and maintain all framework dependencies, security patches, and CUDA versions, adding significant operational burden compared to Script Mode. Script Mode achieves the same custom-code goal while delegating container management entirely to AWS. Keyword tip: BYOC is the correct answer only when a scenario explicitly states that the required framework is unavailable as a pre-built SageMaker container or that deep OS-level or container-level customization is mandatory.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of custom-code training strategies in Amazon SageMaker.

It focuses on selecting Amazon SageMaker Script Mode when the team needs custom Python training logic but wants to use SageMaker pre-built framework containers instead of building a custom Docker image.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Write the custom training code directly in a SageMaker notebook instance and run it using the notebook's execution kernel, relying on the notebook environment for all compute resources: This option is incorrect. Notebook-local execution does not use SageMaker managed training infrastructure, automatic instance provisioning, scaling, or managed model artifact handling.

    Utilize SageMaker Script Mode, write the custom training logic in a Python script, and specify the script as the entry point in a SageMaker estimator configured with a pre-built framework container: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Script Mode allows custom training code to run inside managed pre-built framework containers by specifying an entry_point script in a SageMaker estimator.

    Extend one of SageMaker's built-in algorithm containers by injecting additional training code through environment variables and a custom entry-point override defined in the CreateTrainingJob API call: This option is incorrect. SageMaker built-in algorithm containers do not support arbitrary custom training code injection. They can be configured with hyperparameters and input channels, but their internal algorithm logic is not replaced this way.

    Build a fully custom Docker container from scratch, package all framework dependencies alongside the training code, push the image to Amazon ECR, and reference the ECR URI directly in a SageMaker estimator: This option is not the best answer. Bring Your Own Container is valid when a framework or runtime is unsupported, but it adds unnecessary container management overhead when a pre-built framework container can be used with Script Mode.

Why SageMaker Script Mode fits this scenario.

The organization needs custom training logic.

The algorithm is not one of SageMaker's built-in algorithms.

However, the required ML framework is supported by SageMaker's pre-built framework containers.

The team also wants to avoid managing custom Docker images.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Script Mode.

With Script Mode, the team writes the training logic in a Python script.

That script is passed to a SageMaker estimator using the entry_point parameter.

The estimator is configured to use a pre-built framework container, such as TensorFlow, PyTorch, MXNet, or Scikit-learn.

SageMaker manages the training infrastructure, runs the script inside the selected container, stores model artifacts in Amazon S3, and supports deployment to a SageMaker Endpoint.

This gives the team custom-code flexibility without the operational burden of building, patching, scanning, and publishing a Docker image to Amazon ECR.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Custom training logic: Write a Python training script.

    Supported framework: Use a SageMaker pre-built framework container.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid building a custom Docker image.

    Managed training: Use SageMaker training jobs.

    Estimator-based configuration: Specify the script with entry_point.

    Model artifact handling: Store outputs in Amazon S3.

    Endpoint deployment: Deploy the trained model through SageMaker hosting.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Custom Python training logic + supported framework container + avoid custom Docker = Amazon SageMaker Script Mode.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Script Mode: Best for custom scripts using supported frameworks.

    entry_point: Main training script executed by SageMaker.

    Pre-built framework container: Managed container for frameworks such as PyTorch or TensorFlow.

    Bring Your Own Container: Use when the framework, runtime, or OS-level dependencies are unsupported.

    Built-in algorithms: Use when no custom training logic is required.

    Notebook-local execution: Useful for experimentation, not managed production training.

    Amazon ECR: Stores custom Docker images for BYOC workflows.

Official AWS references.

    Use script mode in Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Script Mode, Bring Your Own Container, BYOC, pre-built framework containers, SageMaker Estimator, entry_point parameter, managed training infrastructure, Amazon ECR, SageMaker Endpoint deployment.

Question 56Skipped

A healthcare diagnostics provider, MediCore Diagnostics, needs to monitor application infrastructure hosted on AWS.


The company wants to configure an alarm that triggers an action when the average CPU utilization of its Amazon EC2 instances exceeds 80% over a 5-minute period.


The company also wants the instance to be automatically stopped when the alarm triggers, with minimal operational overhead and without adding unnecessary architectural components.

Which approach should the machine learning engineer take to meet these requirements?
Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to trigger an Auto Scaling policy that stops the instances when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon CloudWatch alarms can integrate with Auto Scaling policies, Auto Scaling is designed to scale out (launch) or scale in (terminate) instances based on demand — not to stop individual instances. Triggering an Auto Scaling policy to stop specific instances is not a supported or appropriate use of this service in this context, and it introduces unnecessary architectural complexity without satisfying the actual stopping requirement. Keyword tip: Auto Scaling policies govern fleet-level scaling actions; when the scenario asks to stop a specific instance based on a metric threshold, Auto Scaling is not the right integration target for a CloudWatch alarm.
Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to invoke an AWS Lambda function that programmatically calls the EC2 StopInstances API when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although invoking an AWS Lambda function via a CloudWatch alarm action is a technically valid pattern, it introduces unnecessary complexity for this use case. Amazon CloudWatch already provides a native built-in action to stop EC2 instances directly, without requiring a Lambda function as an intermediary layer. This approach violates the principle of minimizing operational overhead when a simpler, natively supported capability already exists. Keyword tip: When CloudWatch natively supports the required EC2 lifecycle action — Stop, Terminate, Reboot, or Recover — adding Lambda as an intermediary adds avoidable operational overhead and architectural components.
Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to publish a message to an Amazon SNS topic, which then notifies the operations team to manually stop the instances.
Explanation
Incorrect. Configuring an Amazon SNS topic as the alarm action only delivers a notification to subscribers and does not automate any remediation action. This approach requires manual intervention from the operations team to stop the instances, which directly contradicts the requirement for automated instance stopping with minimal operational overhead. Keyword tip: SNS alarm actions provide notification and fan-out capabilities, not automated EC2 lifecycle management; manual follow-up disqualifies this option whenever automation is explicitly required.
Correct answer
Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to stop the instances directly using the built-in EC2 instance action.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch natively supports EC2 instance actions — including Stop, Terminate, Reboot, and Recover — as direct alarm actions. When the alarm transitions to the ALARM state (CPU utilization exceeds 80% over the 5-minute evaluation period), the alarm directly invokes the EC2 stop action without any additional services, Lambda functions, or intermediary components. This is the most direct, efficient, and operationally simple approach that fully satisfies both the automation and minimal-overhead requirements. Keyword tip: CloudWatch built-in EC2 alarm actions (Stop, Terminate, Reboot, Recover) are the preferred pattern for automated instance lifecycle management based on a metric threshold, eliminating the need for Lambda or Auto Scaling.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon CloudWatch alarm actions and their native integration with Amazon EC2 instance lifecycle actions.

It focuses on using a CloudWatch Alarm to monitor CPU utilization and directly stop an EC2 instance through a built-in EC2 alarm action, without adding unnecessary services.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to trigger an Auto Scaling policy that stops the instances when the threshold is breached: This option is incorrect. Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling is used for fleet-level scaling actions, such as adding or removing instances. It is not the right mechanism for directly stopping a specific EC2 instance based on a metric threshold.

    Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to invoke an AWS Lambda function that programmatically calls the EC2 StopInstances API when the threshold is breached: This option is technically possible, but it is not the best answer. AWS Lambda adds unnecessary custom code and operational overhead because Amazon CloudWatch already supports built-in EC2 stop actions.

    Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to publish a message to an Amazon SNS topic, which then notifies the operations team to manually stop the instances: This option is incorrect. Amazon SNS can notify the operations team, but it does not automatically stop the instance. Manual intervention violates the automation requirement.

    Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to monitor the average CPU utilization of the EC2 instances. Configure the alarm action to stop the instances directly using the built-in EC2 instance action: This is the correct option. Amazon CloudWatch alarms can directly perform EC2 actions such as stop, terminate, reboot, or recover when an alarm enters the ALARM state.

Why a CloudWatch alarm with a built-in EC2 stop action fits this scenario.

The company needs to monitor CPU utilization on Amazon EC2 instances.

The alarm should trigger when average CPU utilization exceeds 80% over a 5-minute period.

The required action is to automatically stop the instance.

The solution must have minimal operational overhead.

That points directly to an Amazon CloudWatch Alarm with a built-in EC2 action.

Amazon CloudWatch collects EC2 metrics such as CPUUtilization.

A CloudWatch alarm can evaluate that metric against a defined threshold.

When the alarm enters the ALARM state, it can directly invoke an EC2 instance action.

Supported EC2 alarm actions include stop, terminate, reboot, and recover.

In this case, the correct action is to stop the instance directly from the alarm.

This avoids unnecessary architecture.

There is no need for AWS Lambda, Amazon SNS manual escalation, or Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    CPU monitoring: Use Amazon CloudWatch.

    Threshold evaluation: Configure a CloudWatch Alarm.

    Average CPU > 80%: Set the alarm threshold and evaluation period.

    5-minute evaluation: Configure the alarm period accordingly.

    Automated action: Use the built-in EC2 stop action.

    Minimal operational overhead: Avoid Lambda code or manual response workflows.

    Native AWS integration: Use CloudWatch's direct EC2 alarm action support.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

CloudWatch metric threshold + automatically stop EC2 instance + minimal overhead = CloudWatch Alarm with built-in EC2 stop action.

Remember:

    Amazon CloudWatch: Monitors metrics and creates alarms.

    CloudWatch Alarm: Evaluates metric thresholds over time.

    CPUUtilization: Default EC2 metric available in CloudWatch.

    Built-in EC2 alarm actions: Stop, terminate, reboot, or recover an instance.

    AWS Lambda: Use only when custom remediation logic is required.

    Amazon SNS: Notification fan-out, not automatic instance lifecycle management.

    Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling: Fleet scaling, not direct stop action for a specific instance.

Official AWS references.

    Use Amazon CloudWatch alarm actions.

Question 57Skipped

A food and beverage producer, NutriBlend Foods, has deployed a machine learning model for batch inference on Amazon SageMaker to predict product demand across its distribution network.


The team wants to monitor the model’s performance over time and receive automated notifications if the model’s accuracy declines, indicating potential model quality drift.


Which steps should the team take to configure model quality monitoring for this batch inference model using SageMaker Model Monitor? Select TWO.
Deploy a new version of the model automatically every time drift is detected by SageMaker Model Monitor.
Explanation
Incorrect. While automated redeployment pipelines are a valid MLOps practice, SageMaker Model Monitor is responsible only for detecting drift and generating alerts — it does not prescribe or trigger automatic model redeployment. Remediation decisions involve evaluating drift severity, initiating retraining pipelines, and weighing business considerations before promoting a new model version. Triggering redeployment on every drift alert would be operationally unsound and is entirely outside the Model Monitor setup workflow. Keyword tip: when a question asks about SageMaker Model Monitor's responsibilities, remember it covers detection and alerting only — automated remediation belongs to downstream pipelines such as SageMaker Pipelines or AWS Step Functions.
Enable AWS CloudTrail logging on the SageMaker endpoint to track data drift across batch inference jobs.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail is an API activity auditing service that records AWS API calls for governance and compliance purposes; it does not capture model inference inputs, prediction outputs, or statistical quality metrics related to data drift or model quality degradation. For inference data capture and drift detection, SageMaker Model Monitor combined with its built-in data capture configuration is the appropriate tool. Confusing CloudTrail with SageMaker data capture is a common distractor on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: CloudTrail = API audit logs for governance — never a source of model quality or data drift metrics.
Correct selection
Configure SageMaker Model Monitor to merge ground truth labels with the captured prediction outputs to assess the model's accuracy over time.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Model Monitor's model quality monitoring capability requires that ground truth labels be merged with the captured prediction records. This merge enables the computation of accuracy metrics such as precision, recall, and F1 score by comparing actual observed outcomes against the model's predictions. Without ground truth data, it is impossible to evaluate predictive accuracy or detect model quality drift over time. Providing and merging ground truth labels is therefore a mandatory configuration step when setting up model quality monitoring jobs. Keyword tip: ground truth merge + captured predictions = model quality evaluation in SageMaker Model Monitor.
Manually review and record the model's prediction results on a daily basis to identify performance degradation.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually reviewing prediction results on a daily basis is not scalable, is error-prone, and does not leverage SageMaker's managed monitoring capabilities. SageMaker Model Monitor automates data collection, statistical analysis, and alerting through scheduled monitoring jobs, eliminating the operational overhead and inconsistency associated with manual review. In an MLOps context, relying on human inspection instead of automated monitoring is an anti-pattern when managed tooling is available. Keyword tip: manual review = anti-pattern when SageMaker Model Monitor's automated scheduling and baseline comparison are available.
Correct selection
Schedule SageMaker Model Monitor jobs to capture incoming inference data and compare results against a pre-established baseline.
Explanation
Correct. Scheduling SageMaker Model Monitor jobs is a foundational step in enabling automated model quality monitoring. These jobs run on a configurable schedule — for example, hourly or daily — capture incoming inference request and response data, and compare statistical properties and quality metrics against a pre-established baseline created from a representative dataset. Violations detected against the baseline trigger Amazon CloudWatch alarms, which can then fan out notifications through Amazon SNS. This scheduling and baseline comparison mechanism is the core continuous drift detection pattern for batch inference scenarios in SageMaker. Keyword tip: scheduled monitoring jobs + baseline comparison = automated drift detection with SageMaker Model Monitor.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor for model quality monitoring.

It focuses on scheduling monitoring jobs, comparing captured inference data against a baseline, and merging ground truth labels with predictions to calculate quality metrics over time.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Deploy a new version of the model automatically every time drift is detected by SageMaker Model Monitor: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor detects issues and can generate alerts, but automatic redeployment is handled by downstream orchestration services such as Amazon SageMaker Pipelines or AWS Step Functions.

    Enable AWS CloudTrail logging on the SageMaker endpoint to track data drift across batch inference jobs: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records API activity for auditing. It does not capture model predictions, ground truth labels, or statistical model quality metrics.

    Configure SageMaker Model Monitor to merge ground truth labels with the captured prediction outputs to assess the model's accuracy over time: This is a correct option. Model quality monitoring requires ground truth labels so predictions can be compared against actual outcomes and metrics such as precision, recall, and F1 score can be calculated.

    Manually review and record the model's prediction results on a daily basis to identify performance degradation: This option is incorrect. Manual review is not scalable and does not use SageMaker's managed monitoring capabilities.

    Schedule SageMaker Model Monitor jobs to capture incoming inference data and compare results against a pre-established baseline: This is a correct option. Scheduled monitoring jobs compare captured inference data and model outputs against a baseline to detect model quality issues over time.

Why scheduled monitoring jobs and ground truth merge fit this scenario.

The model is used for batch inference.

The team wants to monitor model quality over time.

They need to detect whether accuracy is declining.

That requires two key capabilities.

First, Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor must run scheduled monitoring jobs.

These jobs analyze captured inference data and compare it against a baseline.

The baseline represents expected model behavior or statistical characteristics from a reference dataset.

Second, the team must provide ground truth labels.

Model quality cannot be measured from predictions alone.

To compute metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, or F1 score, the model's predictions must be compared with actual observed outcomes.

That is why Model Monitor needs captured predictions merged with ground truth labels.

Together, scheduled monitoring and ground truth merge allow the team to detect model quality drift and receive alerts when performance falls below acceptable thresholds.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Model quality monitoring: Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor.

    Scheduled analysis: Run monitoring jobs on a recurring schedule.

    Baseline comparison: Compare production or batch inference behavior against a reference baseline.

    Ground truth integration: Merge actual labels with captured predictions.

    Accuracy metrics: Compute metrics such as precision, recall, F1 score, or accuracy.

    Automated alerting: Use Amazon CloudWatch alarms when violations occur.

    Avoid audit-tool confusion: Do not use AWS CloudTrail for model quality drift.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Model quality monitoring + prediction accuracy over time + ground truth labels = scheduled Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor jobs with ground truth merge.

For this question, the correct selected options are:

Merge ground truth labels with captured predictions + schedule Model Monitor jobs against a baseline.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: Monitors data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift.

    Model quality drift: Degradation in predictive performance over time.

    Ground truth labels: Required to calculate actual model performance.

    Data capture: Stores inference inputs and outputs for monitoring.

    Baseline: Reference profile used to evaluate future model behavior.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Receives alarms from monitoring violations.

    AWS CloudTrail: API auditing, not model performance monitoring.

Question 58Skipped

A consumer electronics manufacturer, NexaVolt Devices, wants to process large volumes of real-time user interaction data, including device telemetry, feature usage events, and firmware update signals.


The company wants to use Amazon Kinesis services to ingest, analyze, and store the streaming data.


The solution must support complex real-time analytics on the streaming data and persist the data for future model training and analysis.


Which combination of services should the company use to meet these requirements?
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams for data ingestion, AWS Lambda for real-time analysis, and Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to deliver the processed data to Amazon Redshift.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Kinesis Data Streams is correctly used for ingestion and Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose can deliver data to Amazon Redshift, AWS Lambda is not designed for complex, stateful real-time analytics over high-throughput streaming data. Lambda functions are best suited for lightweight, event-driven transformations rather than sustained stream analytics requiring windowing, aggregations, or joins across device telemetry events. Keyword tip: When a scenario specifies 'complex real-time analytics on high-volume streaming data,' Lambda alone is insufficient — look for Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics (Managed Apache Flink) as the correct processing layer.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose for data ingestion, Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics for real-time analysis, and AWS Glue to store data in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose is a managed delivery service optimized for loading streaming data into destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, or Amazon OpenSearch Service — it does not expose consumer APIs or act as a general-purpose ingestion buffer the way Amazon Kinesis Data Streams does. Additionally, AWS Glue is a batch-oriented ETL and data catalog service, not a real-time streaming destination or storage layer. Keyword tip: Confusing Kinesis Data Firehose (delivery) with Kinesis Data Streams (ingestion and buffering) is a frequently tested distractor on the MLA-C01 exam — always distinguish their roles when evaluating Kinesis pipeline architectures.
Correct answer
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams for data ingestion, Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics (Managed Apache Flink) for real-time analysis, and Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to deliver the processed data to Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Kinesis Data Streams provides a durable, scalable ingestion buffer with support for multiple concurrent consumers and replayable records, making it ideal for high-velocity device telemetry from NexaVolt Devices' connected products. Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics (now Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink) enables complex, stateful real-time stream processing including windowing, aggregations, and pattern detection across live event streams. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose then reliably batches and delivers the processed output to Amazon S3, which serves as cost-effective, durable long-term storage for downstream ML model training pipelines. Keyword tip: The canonical Kinesis pipeline for ML data preparation is Kinesis Data Streams → Kinesis Data Analytics (Managed Apache Flink) → Kinesis Data Firehose → Amazon S3.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Streams for data ingestion, Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose for real-time analysis, and Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics to store the processed data in Amazon RDS.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose does not perform real-time analytics — it is exclusively a managed data delivery service that moves streaming data to supported destinations. Furthermore, Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics is a stream processing service and does not natively write its output directly into Amazon RDS as a streaming destination. This combination fundamentally misassigns service roles and references an unsupported output target for Kinesis Data Analytics. Keyword tip: When an answer option describes Kinesis Data Firehose as performing analytics or Kinesis Data Analytics writing to Amazon RDS, both are indicators of Kinesis service role confusion — recognize these as strong MLA-C01 distractors.
Overall explanation

Question 59Skipped

A data science team at AgroVision Analytics, an agricultural technology company, is developing machine learning models using Amazon SageMaker Jupyter Notebooks.


The team analyzes crop yield predictions and soil health data in iterative cycles, frequently pausing and resuming experiments across multiple sessions.


The team is concerned about losing intermediate results, notebooks, and datasets between sessions.


Which SageMaker Jupyter Notebooks feature most directly ensures that data and work artifacts are not lost during the machine learning workflow?
On-demand instance provisioning, which allows the team to spin up compute resources quickly whenever a new session is initiated
Explanation
Incorrect. On-demand instance provisioning refers to the capability to rapidly provision compute resources for a notebook session. While this reduces startup latency and improves resource availability, it does not address data durability or preservation between sessions. If storage is not persistent, a newly provisioned instance would start without any previously saved work artifacts, notebooks, or datasets. Keyword tip: when a question centers on preventing data loss or preserving work state across sessions in SageMaker, look for 'storage' or 'persistence' keywords rather than compute provisioning features.
Correct answer
Persistent storage backed by Amazon EBS volumes, which retains notebooks, datasets, and artifacts across instance stop and restart cycles
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Notebook Instances attach persistent Amazon EBS-backed storage to the underlying EC2 instance, ensuring that all notebooks, datasets, scripts, and intermediate artifacts stored in the /home/ec2-user/SageMaker directory are retained across instance stop, restart, and even instance type changes. This allows team members at AgroVision Analytics to safely stop their instances to reduce costs and resume their exact working state in a subsequent session without any data loss. Keyword tip: when a question asks about preserving work artifacts across iterative ML development sessions or preventing data loss in SageMaker Notebook Instance environments, persistent EBS-backed storage is the distinguishing correct answer.
Real-time inference endpoints integrated within the notebook environment for immediate model serving after training completes
Explanation
Incorrect. Real-time inference endpoints are a SageMaker deployment feature used to serve trained models at low latency for online predictions, operating entirely outside the notebook development environment. They play no role in storing or preserving notebooks, datasets, or experiment artifacts between development sessions. Keyword tip: confusing inference serving features with development environment storage is a common distractor pattern on the MLA-C01 exam — inference endpoints address model deployment, not data durability.
Pre-installed ML libraries such as TensorFlow and PyTorch, which eliminate environment setup time and accelerate the development workflow
Explanation
Incorrect. Pre-installed libraries such as TensorFlow, PyTorch, and scikit-learn reduce environment configuration overhead and accelerate the initial setup of a notebook instance, but they are a convenience feature for the development environment. They provide no mechanism for data persistence or artifact preservation between sessions, as software availability is entirely independent of storage durability. Keyword tip: when evaluating options that address data loss prevention, focus on storage architecture mechanisms rather than software pre-configuration or library availability.

Question 60Skipped

A machine learning team at Nexlara Systems, a mid-size enterprise software vendor, is using Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to label a large dataset for an object detection task involving screenshots of user interface components and application dashboards.


The team has a constrained budget and must reduce labeling costs without sacrificing annotation quality.


The dataset contains a mix of straightforward samples and highly ambiguous edge cases that require expert judgment.


Which strategy provided by Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth would best help the team achieve both cost efficiency and labeling quality?
Contract a third-party specialized annotation vendor for all labeling tasks to ensure the highest accuracy across the entire dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. Engaging a third-party specialized annotation vendor for all labeling tasks may deliver high accuracy, but it does not reduce costs because every data point is still processed by paid external annotators at scale. This approach eliminates the cost savings that active learning automation provides for straightforward samples, directly contradicting the budget constraint stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: when a scenario specifies both cost reduction and quality preservation together, options that satisfy only one dimension — such as outsourcing all annotation — should be eliminated immediately on the MLA-C01 exam.
Use fully manual labeling performed exclusively by a private workforce composed of internal subject-matter experts within the organization.
Explanation
Incorrect. Fully manual labeling by an internal private workforce maximizes organizational control and can achieve high annotation quality, but it increases both time and cost because every data point — including trivially easy samples that a model could label automatically — requires human attention. This approach directly contradicts the cost-reduction requirement stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: on the MLA-C01 exam, fully manual workforce approaches are strong distractors when the scenario explicitly requires cost optimization alongside quality goals.
Deploy a fully automated machine learning model to handle all labeling tasks without any human intervention, relying on confidence thresholds alone.
Explanation
Incorrect. A fully automated labeling pipeline can significantly lower costs but introduces quality risks for ambiguous or complex samples where model confidence is inherently low. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth explicitly discourages fully automated labeling when annotation quality must be preserved for difficult edge cases, because the model's predictions on uncertain samples are unreliable without human review. Keyword tip: 'fully automated without any human intervention' is a red flag on the MLA-C01 exam whenever annotation quality for complex or ambiguous samples is a stated requirement.
Correct answer
Implement automated data labeling (active learning), where a model labels high-confidence simple cases automatically, and human labelers are engaged only for low-confidence complex cases.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth's automated data labeling feature implements an active learning loop: the service incrementally trains an internal labeling model on human-annotated samples and uses it to automatically annotate data points where the model's confidence exceeds a defined threshold, routing only uncertain or low-confidence samples to human annotators. This hybrid approach — automation for simple cases, expert human review for complex ones — is the canonical SageMaker Ground Truth cost-optimization pattern and directly satisfies both the budget constraint and the quality requirement simultaneously. Keyword tip: 'automated data labeling + active learning + confidence threshold' is the MLA-C01 signal for the SageMaker Ground Truth cost-and-quality optimization pattern.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth automated data labeling.

It focuses on using active learning to reduce labeling costs while preserving annotation quality by routing only low-confidence or ambiguous samples to human reviewers.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Contract a third-party specialized annotation vendor for all labeling tasks to ensure the highest accuracy across the entire dataset: This option is incorrect. A specialized vendor may improve quality, but using humans for every item does not reduce labeling cost.

    Use fully manual labeling performed exclusively by a private workforce composed of internal subject-matter experts within the organization: This option is not the best answer. A private workforce can improve control and quality, but labeling every sample manually is expensive and does not satisfy the cost-efficiency requirement.

    Deploy a fully automated machine learning model to handle all labeling tasks without any human intervention, relying on confidence thresholds alone: This option is incorrect. Fully automated labeling can reduce cost, but it risks poor quality on ambiguous or low-confidence examples.

    Implement automated data labeling using active learning, where a model labels high-confidence simple cases automatically, and human labelers are engaged only for low-confidence complex cases: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth automated data labeling uses active learning to label high-confidence examples automatically and route uncertain examples to human annotators.

Why automated data labeling with active learning fits this scenario.

The dataset contains both straightforward samples and ambiguous edge cases.

The team needs to reduce labeling cost.

At the same time, annotation quality must remain high.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth automated data labeling.

Ground Truth uses an active learning workflow.

Human labelers first annotate a subset of the data.

Ground Truth uses those labels to train an internal labeling model.

The model then attempts to label additional data points automatically.

When the model is confident enough, the label can be applied automatically.

When confidence is low, the sample is routed to human labelers.

This hybrid approach balances cost and quality.

Simple examples do not require repeated human effort.

Difficult or ambiguous examples still receive human review.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Cost reduction: Automatically label high-confidence examples.

    Quality preservation: Send low-confidence examples to human reviewers.

    Mixed-complexity dataset: Handle both easy and ambiguous samples.

    Active learning: Improve the labeling model over multiple rounds.

    Human-in-the-loop: Keep humans involved where judgment is needed.

    Managed labeling workflow: Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

    Avoid extremes: Do not rely on fully manual or fully automated labeling only.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Ground Truth + cost reduction + annotation quality + mixed easy and hard samples = automated data labeling with active learning.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth: Managed data labeling service.

    Automated data labeling: Reduces human labeling effort.

    Active learning: Selects which examples should be labeled by humans.

    Confidence threshold: Determines whether the model labels automatically or routes to humans.

    Human-in-the-loop: Preserves quality for difficult samples.

    Fully manual labeling: High quality but expensive.

    Fully automated labeling: Lower cost but risky for ambiguous examples.

Official AWS references.

    Automate data labeling with Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth, automated data labeling, active learning, confidence threshold, private workforce, annotation cost optimization, human-in-the-loop, hybrid labeling pipeline.

Question 61Skipped

An online gaming company, PixelArena Studios, uses Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to ingest real-time player clickstream and in-game event data into Amazon S3.


During ingestion, some records occasionally fail the transformation step because of malformed data caused by client-side instrumentation bugs.


The company needs a reliable mechanism to capture these failed records so data engineers can analyze and remediate them without data loss.


What is the most operationally effective approach to handle this scenario?
Enable error logging in Amazon CloudWatch to identify and manually correct the malformed records before reprocessing them.
Explanation
Incorrect. While enabling error logging in Amazon CloudWatch is a useful monitoring practice that captures error metadata and stack traces, it does not preserve the actual content of malformed records. This approach would require manual identification and reconstruction of the failed data, which is operationally burdensome and does not guarantee data recovery. Keyword tip: When a scenario requires durable capture of the original failed record payload — not just error metadata — CloudWatch alone is insufficient; look for an S3-based backup or archival mechanism instead.
Enable data transformation with AWS Lambda and configure an Amazon SQS Dead Letter Queue (DLQ) to capture malformed records that cannot be processed by the transformation function.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose does not natively integrate with Amazon SQS Dead Letter Queues (DLQs) at the stream level. DLQs are a native construct for AWS Lambda asynchronous invocations and Amazon SQS queues, not for Firehose delivery streams. Although Firehose invokes an AWS Lambda function synchronously during transformation, the Firehose service itself does not route records marked as 'ProcessingFailed' to a DLQ — it routes them to the configured S3 backup location. Keyword tip: When a distractor pairs 'Kinesis Data Firehose' with 'SQS DLQ' for failed transformation records, recognize this as an unsupported integration pattern; DLQs apply to asynchronous Lambda invocations, not Firehose-level record handling.
Configure Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with an aggressive retry policy to reprocess failed transformation records until they succeed.
Explanation
Incorrect. Configuring a retry policy within Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose for transformation failures will not resolve the underlying issue of structurally malformed records. If a record cannot be parsed or transformed due to a schema violation or encoding error, retrying the same transformation logic against the same malformed input will continue to fail deterministically, consuming resources without making progress. Retry policies in Firehose are appropriate for transient delivery failures — such as network errors or destination throttling — not for data quality defects. Keyword tip: Distinguish between transient errors (retriable) and deterministic data quality failures (not retriable); when the root cause is a structurally malformed record, a retry policy is the wrong remediation path.
Correct answer
Configure Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to use the source record backup feature to send all records — or only failed records — to a separate Amazon S3 prefix or bucket for subsequent analysis.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose natively supports a source record backup configuration that can be set to deliver either all records or only records that fail the transformation step to a designated Amazon S3 location. When transformation is enabled via AWS Lambda, records for which the Lambda function returns a 'ProcessingFailed' status are automatically routed to the configurable backup S3 prefix, providing a durable, serverless, and fully managed mechanism for capturing malformed records without any additional infrastructure. This allows PixelArena Studios' data engineers to isolate, analyze, and remediate failed player event records independently from the main delivery path. Keyword tip: When a scenario combines 'Kinesis Data Firehose,' 'Lambda transformation failures,' and 'data loss prevention for failed records,' the canonical solution is the Firehose S3 backup mode set to 'FailedDataOnly.'
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of error handling in Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose transformation pipelines.

It focuses on using Firehose source record backup to capture records that fail AWS Lambda transformation and store them durably in Amazon S3 for later analysis.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Enable error logging in Amazon CloudWatch to identify and manually correct the malformed records before reprocessing them: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch logs can capture error metadata, but they do not reliably preserve the full original failed record payload for later remediation.

    Enable data transformation with AWS Lambda and configure an Amazon SQS Dead Letter Queue to capture malformed records that cannot be processed by the transformation function: This option is incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose does not natively route failed transformation records to an Amazon SQS dead-letter queue. Firehose uses its configured Amazon S3 backup location for failed transformed records.

    Configure Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with an aggressive retry policy to reprocess failed transformation records until they succeed: This option is incorrect. Retries are useful for transient failures, but malformed records usually fail deterministically. Retrying the same invalid payload does not fix schema or parsing errors.

    Configure Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to use the source record backup feature to send all records or only failed records to a separate Amazon S3 prefix or bucket for subsequent analysis: This is the correct option. Firehose can back up source records to Amazon S3, and with transformation failures it can isolate failed records for later inspection and remediation.

Why Firehose S3 backup for failed records fits this scenario.

The company ingests real-time player clickstream and in-game event data.

The data flows through Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose.

Some records fail during the transformation step because they are malformed.

The team needs to preserve those failed records without data loss.

That points to the Firehose source record backup feature.

When AWS Lambda data transformation is enabled, Firehose invokes the Lambda function to transform incoming records.

If a record cannot be transformed and is returned with a failure status, Firehose can deliver the failed source record to a configured Amazon S3 backup location.

The backup mode can be configured to store all source records or only failed records.

For this scenario, failed-record backup is the most operationally effective pattern.

It allows the main delivery stream to continue processing valid records.

At the same time, malformed records are preserved in Amazon S3 for offline analysis, debugging, and reprocessing.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Transformation failure handling: Capture records that fail Lambda transformation.

    No data loss: Store failed records durably in Amazon S3.

    Malformed record analysis: Allow engineers to inspect invalid payloads later.

    Operational continuity: Keep the main delivery path running for valid events.

    Low operational overhead: Use native Firehose backup behavior.

    Serverless architecture: Avoid custom DLQ or polling systems.

    Targeted isolation: Store only failed records when FailedDataOnly is appropriate.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose + Lambda transformation failures + preserve malformed records = S3 backup mode for failed records.

Remember:

    Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose: Managed streaming delivery service.

    AWS Lambda transformation: Optional transformation step inside Firehose.

    ProcessingFailed: Failure status for records that could not be transformed.

    S3 backup mode: Stores source records in Amazon S3.

    FailedDataOnly: Stores only failed records when configured for failed transformation capture.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Useful for logs and metrics, but not sufficient for preserving failed payloads.

    Amazon SQS DLQ: Not the native Firehose-level failed-record mechanism.

Official AWS references.

    Transform source data in Amazon Data Firehose.

Key exam terms: Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose, S3 backup mode, FailedDataOnly, AWS Lambda transformation, ProcessingFailed status, source record backup, malformed records, data loss prevention.

Question 62Skipped

A machine learning team at GenPharma Labs, a pharmaceutical research organization, needs to centralize governance for ML models used in drug candidate screening.


The team wants to track the complete history of model versions, including references to training datasets, hyperparameters used during training, and performance metrics evaluated after each experiment.


The solution must allow the team to retrieve, compare, and promote specific model versions as part of a controlled MLOps workflow.


Which Amazon SageMaker feature should the team use to meet these requirements?
SageMaker Training Jobs
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Training Jobs are used to execute the model training process on managed compute infrastructure. While they record training configuration and produce model artifacts, they do not provide a centralized, versioned catalog for model governance, promotion workflows, or cross-version comparison. Training Jobs are the mechanism that feeds artifacts into the Model Registry, not a replacement for it. Keyword tip: when you see 'version tracking,' 'model governance,' or 'promotion workflow,' Training Jobs alone are insufficient — they are the artifact-producing step upstream of registry-based governance.
Correct answer
SageMaker Model Registry
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Model Registry is the purpose-built feature for centralized model version management in SageMaker. It allows teams to register model versions along with associated metadata such as training data references, hyperparameters, and evaluation metrics, making it ideal for regulated environments like pharmaceutical research where traceability is critical. It supports approval workflows (Approved / Rejected / PendingManualApproval) that enable controlled promotion of models to production, and it integrates natively with SageMaker Pipelines for end-to-end MLOps automation. Keyword tip: whenever a question mentions 'model versioning,' 'metadata tracking,' 'approval workflow,' or 'centralized model governance,' SageMaker Model Registry is the canonical answer.
SageMaker Experiments
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Experiments is designed to organize, track, and compare individual training runs, parameters, and metrics during the experimentation phase. While it captures per-run metadata and facilitates comparison across runs, it does not provide a versioned model catalog with approval and promotion workflows for production governance. It is complementary to, not a replacement for, the Model Registry. Keyword tip: SageMaker Experiments is best associated with 'experiment tracking' and 'run comparison' during research iterations, not with 'model version promotion' or 'centralized governance' of production-bound artifacts.
SageMaker Pipelines
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Pipelines automates and orchestrates ML workflows, including data processing, training, evaluation, and conditional model registration steps. While a pipeline can be configured to register a model in the Model Registry upon successful evaluation, the pipeline itself does not maintain or manage the version history or approval state. The version tracking and governance responsibility belongs to the Model Registry, which Pipelines invokes as a downstream step. Keyword tip: when the question emphasizes 'tracking,' 'versioning,' and 'retrieval of metadata,' Pipelines is an orchestration mechanism, not a model catalog — it enables the workflow but does not store the versioned governance record.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Model Registry for model versioning, governance, and controlled promotion in MLOps workflows.

It focuses on selecting Model Registry when the team needs a centralized catalog of model versions, metadata, approval status, and production promotion controls.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    SageMaker Training Jobs: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Training Jobs execute model training and produce model artifacts, but they do not provide centralized model version governance or approval workflows.

    SageMaker Model Registry: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Model Registry stores and manages model versions, metadata, approval status, and promotion workflows for production governance.

    SageMaker Experiments: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracks and compares experiment runs, metrics, parameters, and artifacts. It is useful during experimentation, but it is not the centralized model version catalog for production governance.

    SageMaker Pipelines: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines orchestrates ML workflows. It can register a model into the Model Registry, but it does not replace the registry's model versioning and approval capabilities.

Why SageMaker Model Registry fits this scenario.

The pharmaceutical research team needs to govern ML models used for drug candidate screening.

They need to track the full history of model versions.

They also need references to training datasets, hyperparameters, and performance metrics.

The team must be able to compare versions and promote specific models through a controlled workflow.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Model Registry.

Amazon SageMaker Model Registry acts as a centralized catalog for model packages and model versions.

Each registered model version can include metadata such as model artifacts, training data references, evaluation metrics, and approval status.

This is important in regulated industries because teams need traceability and control over which model version is approved for deployment.

The Model Registry also supports approval states such as Approved, Rejected, and PendingManualApproval.

This allows teams to promote models in a governed way instead of deploying artifacts directly from training jobs.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Model version history: Use Amazon SageMaker Model Registry.

    Centralized catalog: Store model versions in one governed location.

    Metadata tracking: Associate training data, hyperparameters, metrics, and artifacts with model versions.

    Controlled promotion: Use approval states before deployment.

    Governance: Support traceability for regulated ML workflows.

    MLOps integration: Register models from Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

    Version comparison: Retrieve and compare model versions before production promotion.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Model versioning + metadata tracking + approval workflow + controlled production promotion = Amazon SageMaker Model Registry.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Model Registry: Central catalog for model versions and governance.

    Model package: Versioned model artifact and metadata entry in the registry.

    Approval workflow: Controls whether a model can be promoted.

    PendingManualApproval: Common state before a model is approved.

    Amazon SageMaker Training Jobs: Produce model artifacts.

    Amazon SageMaker Experiments: Track experiment runs and compare metrics.

    Amazon SageMaker Pipelines: Automate workflows and can register models into the registry.

Official AWS references.

    Register and deploy models with Model Registry.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Model Registry, model version governance, approval workflow, metadata tracking, model lineage, MLOps promotion, centralized model catalog, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines integration.
